An introduction to
singular stochastic PDEs:

Abstract




Preface

These notes have been prepared for a series of lectures given at the
Sarajevo Stochastic Analysis Winter School, from January 28 to February 1, 2019.
There already exist several excellent lecture notes and reviews on the subject,
such as
[
Hai09
]
on (non-singular) stochastic PDEs,
and
[
Hai15
,
CW17
]
on singular stochastic PDEs and
regularity structures. The present notes have two main specificities. The first
one is that they focus on a particular example, the Allen–Cahn equation, which
allows to introduce several of the difficulties of the theory in a gradual way,
by increasing the space dimension step by step. The hope is that while this
limits the generality of the theory presented, this limitation is more that made
up by a gain in clarity. The second specific aspect of these notes is that they
go beyond existence and uniqueness of solutions, by covering a few recent
results on convergence to equilibrium and metastability in these system.

The author wishes to thank the organisers of the Winter School, Frank Proske,
Abdol-Reza Mansouri and Oussama Amine, for the invitation that provided the
motivation to compile these notes; his co-workers on SPDEs, Ajay Chandra,
Giacomo Di Gesù, Barbara Gentz, Christian Kuehn and Hendrik Weber, who
greatly contributed to some results presented here; Yvain Bruned for remarks on
an early version of the notes; and Martin Hairer and Lorenzo Zambotti for
patiently explaining some subtleties of the theory of regularity structures.

Thanks are also due to the Isaac Newton Institute in Cambridge, as well as
David Brydges, Alessandro Giuliani, Massimiliano Gubinelli, Antti Kupiainen,
Hendrik Weber and Lorenzo Zambotti for organising the semester “Scaling
limits, rough paths, quantum field theory”. In particular the tutorial
lectures given during the opening workshop of the semester proved of great help
in preparing these notes.

Finally, thanks are due to Adrian Martini, for pointing out some typos in the
original version of these notes.

Chapter 1
A system of interacting diffusions

In this chapter, we will analyse the dynamics of a system of
N\mathbin{\geqslant}2
coupled
stochastic differential equations (SDEs), which will converge, in a certain
sense, to a stochastic Allen–Cahn PDE. This will serve two purposes: firstly,
it will introduce a natural physical model that motivates the use of stochastic
PDEs, and secondly, it will allow us to recapitulate some notions from the
theory of SDEs that will be important in the infinite-dimensional case.

The system of interacting diffusions is given by

\dd\!y^{i}_{t}=\bigl[y^{i}_{t}-(y^{i}_{t})^{3}\bigr]\dd\!t+\frac{\gamma}{2}\bigl[y^{i+1}_{t}-2y^{i}_{t}+y^{i-1}_{t}\bigr]\dd\!t+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dd\!W^{i}_{t}\;,\qquad i=1,\dots,N\;,

(1.0.1)

in the following setting.

•

The real variables
y^{1},\dots,y^{N}\in\mathbb{R}
can be thought of as the
positions of
N
particles. We use periodic boundary conditions, that is,

y^{0}=y^{N}
and
y^{N+1}=y^{1}
. This can also be indicated by considering
i
as an
element of the cyclic group
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
.

•

The term
[y^{i}_{t}-(y^{i}_{t})^{3}]\dd\!t
tends to push each particle
towards
+1
if
y^{i}_{t}>0
and towards
-1
if
y^{i}_{t}<0
, that is, we have a
bistable local dynamics.

•

The term
\smash{\frac{\gamma}{2}}[y^{i+1}_{t}-2y^{i}_{t}+y^{i-1}_{t}]\dd\!t
is a discretised Laplacian interaction, which describes a ferromagnetic
nearest-neighbour coupling pushing the
y^{i}_{t}
towards each other (if

\gamma>0
).

•

The
W^{i}_{t}
are independent standard Wiener processes on a probability
space
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
, modelling thermal noise.

The system (
1.0.1
) can be thought of as a version of the Ising model
with continuous spins. We are mainly interested in its long-time dynamics for
large values of
N
and
\gamma
. The parameter
\varepsilon\mathbin{\geqslant}0
will be either
small or of order
1
.

1.1
Deterministic dynamics

In the deterministic case
\varepsilon=0
, the system (
1.0.1
) reduces to the
system of ordinary differential equations (ODEs)

\dot{y}^{i}=y^{i}-(y^{i})^{3}+\frac{\gamma}{2}\bigl[y^{i+1}-2y^{i}+y^{i-1}\bigr]\;.

(1.1.1)

A first observation is that (
1.1.1
) has gradient form

\dot{y}=-\nabla V(y)\;,

(1.1.2)

where
V
is the quartic potential given by

V(y):=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\Lambda}U(y^{i})+\frac{\gamma}{4}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\Lambda}(y^{i+1}-y^{i})^{2}\;,\qquad U(\xi)=\frac{1}{4}\bigl(\xi^{2}-1\bigr)^{2}\;.

(1.1.3)

The stationary points of (
1.1.1
) are thus the critical points of
V
.
Note that
V
is invariant under the group
G
generated by cyclic permutations
of the
y^{i}
, the reflection
y^{i}\mapsto y^{N+1-i}
, and the point symmetry

y\mapsto-y
.

The simplest situation arises for
\gamma=0
: then the set of all critical
points of
V
is
\{-1,0,1\}^{\Lambda}
and has cardinality
3^{N}
. The critical
points in
\{-1,1\}^{\Lambda}
are all local minima of
V
, and thus describe

2^{N}
stable stationary points of (
1.1.1
). In fact, one can show

[
BFG07a
, Proposition 2.1]
that this situation perturbs to small positive

\gamma
, in the sense that there are still
3^{N}
stationary points,
2^{N}
of
which are stable, for all
\gamma
up to a critical value
\gamma_{0}(N)
which is
larger than
\frac{1}{4}
for all
N
.

We are more interested, however, in the case of large
\gamma
of the order

N^{2}
. Indeed, this is the natural scaling in which the discrete Laplacian
in (
1.1.1
) approaches the continuous Laplacian.

Proposition 1.1.1
.

Let

\gamma_{1}(N):=\frac{1}{2\sin^{2}(\pi/N)}=\frac{N^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\Bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigr]\;.

(1.1.4)

Then the potential
V
admits exactly
3
stationary points
0
and

\pm(1,\dots,1)
if and only if
\gamma\mathbin{\geqslant}\gamma_{1}(N)
.
The points
\pm(1,\dots,1)
are always local minima of
V
, while
0
is a
saddle with exactly one linearly unstable direction if and only if

\gamma>\gamma_{1}(N)
.

Proof:
.

First note that the discrete Laplacian is represented by a Toeplitz matrix (its
entries are constant on diagonals), whose eigenvalues can be computed
explicitly by discrete Fourier transform. They are given by
-\lambda^{N}_{k}

where

\lambda^{N}_{k}=\lambda^{N}_{N-k}:=2\sin^{2}\biggl(\frac{k\pi}{N}\biggr)\;,\qquad k=0,\dots,N-1\;.

(1.1.5)

In particular,
\lambda^{N}_{0}=0
and

\gamma_{1}(N)=1/(\lambda^{N}_{1}-\lambda^{N}_{0})
is the inverse of the spectral
gap. Consider now the function

W(y):=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\Lambda}\bigl(y^{i+1}-y^{i}\bigr)^{2}\;.

(1.1.6)

Note that
W
vanishes on the diagonal
\{y^{1}=\dots=y^{N}\}
and is otherwise
positive. An easy computation (cf.
[
BFG07a
, Proposition 3.1]
) shows that

\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!t}W(y_{t})\mathbin{\leqslant}2\Bigl(1-\frac{\gamma}{\gamma_{1}(N)}\Bigr)W(y_{t})-\frac{1}{N}W(y_{t})^{2}\;.

(1.1.7)

Thus when
\gamma\mathbin{\geqslant}\gamma_{1}(N)
,
t\mapsto W(y_{t})
decreases for
y_{t}

outside the diagonal, i.e.,
W
is a Lyapunov function. Therefore, when

\gamma\mathbin{\geqslant}\gamma_{1}(N)
all orbits of (
1.1.1
) will converge to the
diagonal. The only stationary points on the diagonal are
0
and

\pm(1,\dots,1)
. The eigenvalues of the linearisation of (
1.1.1
) at
0

are given by
-\mu^{N}_{k}
where

\mu^{N}_{k}:=-1+\gamma\lambda^{N}_{k}\;,\qquad k=0,\dots,N-1\;.

(1.1.8)

If
\gamma>\gamma_{1}(N)
, then only
-\mu^{N}_{0}
is positive, while for

\gamma<\gamma_{1}(N)
, there are at least three positive eigenvalues (two if

N=2
). Since
V(y)
is bounded below and goes to infinity as

\lVert y\rVert\to\infty
, there must be critical points outside the diagonal in
the latter case. Finally, the eigenvalues of the linearisation at

\pm(1,\dots,1)
are given by
-\nu^{N}_{k}
where

\nu^{N}_{k}:=2+\gamma\lambda^{N}_{k}\;,\qquad k=0,\dots,N-1\;.

(1.1.9)

These are always negative, showing that these points are always
stable (they are local minima of
V
).
∎

Exercise 1.1.2
.

Show that the potential satisfies the lower bound

V(y_{0}+y_{\perp})\mathbin{\geqslant}V(y_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma_{1}(N)}-1\Bigr)\,\lVert y_{\perp}\rVert^{2}

(1.1.10)

whenever
y_{0}
belongs to the diagonal
\{y^{1}=\dots=y^{N}\}
and

y_{\perp}
is orthogonal to the diagonal.

This result shows that if
\gamma>\gamma_{1}(N)
, then
V
is a double-well
potential, with a saddle having one unstable direction located at the origin,
and the two minima located at
\pm(1,\dots,1)
. The unstable manifolds of the
saddle are subsets of the diagonal, and connect
0
to the two local minima.
This is the case we will mainly be interested in in what follows.

Figure 1.1:
Schematic bifurcation diagram for (
1.1.1
) in the case
N=4
.
Each branch in this diagram represents a family of stationary points globally
invariant under the symmetry group
G
, with only one representative of the
family displayed on the left side of the diagram. Green full lines represent
stable stationary points (local minima of the potential
V
), while broken lines
with
k
dots represent stationary points with
k
unstable directions (saddles
of Morse index
k
of
V
).

As
\gamma
decreases below
\gamma_{1}(N)
, the situation becomes much more
involved, and is not understood in full detail. What is known is that at

\gamma_{1}(N)
, the system undergoes a
G
-symmetric analogue of a pitchfork
bifurcation, at which the origin becomes unstable in more than one direction,
while expelling a multiple of
N
new stationary points with lower symmetry. As

\gamma
decreases further, these points undergo further symmetry-breaking
bifurcations, as illustrated by
Figure

1.1
in the case
N=4
.
However, all these further bifurcation occur for
\gamma={\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}(N^{2})
. In other
words, for any
\tilde{\gamma}>0
, the number of stationary points is at most of
order
N
when
\gamma>\tilde{\gamma}N^{2}
and
N
is sufficiently large.

Exercise 1.1.3
.

In the case
N=2
, find all stationary points of the potential
V
and
determine their stability as a function of
\gamma\mathbin{\geqslant}0
.

Hint:
Express the potential
V
in variables
z_{\pm}=y_{1}\pm y_{2}
.

1.2
Existence and uniqueness of solutions

We now return to the SDE (
1.0.1
) for
\varepsilon>0
. We can write it in
compact form as

\dd\!y_{t}=-\nabla V(y_{t})\dd\!t+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dd\!W_{t}\;.

(1.2.1)

We first note that
y\mapsto\nabla V(y)
is continuous (and therefore
measurable) and locally (but not globally) Lipschitz. We can thus apply a
standard result, such as
[
Øks85
, Theorem 5.2.1]
to the process

y_{t\wedge\tau}
where
\tau
is the first-exit time from a large ball to
obtain local existence and uniqueness of a strong solution to (
1.2.1
).
In particular, local existence follows from the fact that the map

y^{(k)}\mapsto y^{(k+1)}
defined by

y_{t}^{(k+1)}=y_{0}-\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\nabla V(y^{(k)}_{s})\dd\!t+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}W_{t}

(1.2.2)

is a contraction, via Banach’s fixed point theorem.

To extend this result to global existence of a strong solution, we have to rule
out finite-time explosion of solutions. This is, however, not hard to achieve,
since the quartic growth of the potential prevents sample paths from straying
very far from the origin (coercivity). Here are two possible ways of obtaining
global
existence:

1.

One checks that the drift and diffusion coefficients
in (
1.2.1
) satisfy the one-sided growth condition

y\cdot\bigl(-\nabla V(y)\bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\bigr)^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}K(1+\lVert y\rVert^{2})\qquad\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}

(1.2.3)

for a constant
K>0
. See for instance
[
Mao97
, Theorem 3.5]
.

2.

Let

{\mathscr{L}}:=\varepsilon\Delta-\nabla V\cdot\nabla

(1.2.4)

denote the infinitesimal generator of the diffusion (
1.2.1
). Then
we can take advantage of the fact that
V
is itself a Lyapunov function
for the system, in the sense that there exists a constant
c\mathbin{\geqslant}0
such that

({\mathscr{L}}V)(y)\mathbin{\leqslant}cV(y)\qquad\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}\;.

(1.2.5)

This implies that the process is non-explosive according to

[
MT93c
, Theorem 2.1]
.

Exercise 1.2.1
.

Check that the SDE (
1.0.1
) satisfies
the one-sided growth condition (
1.2.3
) as well as the Lyapunov
condition (
1.2.5
).
Show that, in addition, there exists
R<\infty
such that
{\mathscr{L}}V(y)
is
negative outside the ball
\{\lVert y\rVert\mathbin{\leqslant}R\}
. Can we do better than that?

The main result of this section is thus as follows.

Proposition 1.2.2
.

For any initial condition
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
, the SDE (
1.0.1
) admits an
almost surely continuous, pathwise unique strong solution
(y_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}

which is global in time.

1.3
Invariant measure and reversibility

In order to determine an invariant probability measure for the
diffusion (
1.2.1
), it is useful to rewrite the infinitesimal
generator (
1.2.4
) in the form

{\mathscr{L}}=\varepsilon\e^{V/\varepsilon}\nabla\cdot\e^{-V/\varepsilon}\nabla\;,

(1.3.1)

with the usual convention that any differential operator acts on everything on
its right. This is indeed equivalent to (
1.2.4
) by Leibniz’ rule.

Proposition 1.3.1
.

Assume
\varepsilon>0
and the potential
V
is such that

{\mathscr{Z}}:=\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\dd\!y<\infty\;.

(1.3.2)

Then the diffusion (
1.2.1
) admits a unique invariant probability
measure, with density

\pi(y):=\frac{1}{{\mathscr{Z}}}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}

(1.3.3)

with respect to Lebesgue measure
\dd\!y
, known as a
Gibbs measure
.

Proof:
.

Let
f
be a bounded measurable test function in the domain of
{\mathscr{L}}
. Writing

{\mathscr{L}}^{\dagger}
for the adjoint of
{\mathscr{L}}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle{\mathscr{L}}^{\dagger}\pi,f\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\pi,{\mathscr{L}}f\rangle_{L^{2}}

(1.3.4)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\pi(y)\varepsilon\e^{V(y)/\varepsilon}\nabla\cdot\bigl(\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\nabla f(y)\bigr)\dd\!y

(1.3.5)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{{\mathscr{Z}}}\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\nabla\cdot\bigl(\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\nabla f(y)\bigr)\dd\!y

(1.3.6)

\displaystyle=0

(1.3.7)

by the divergence theorem.
Thus
{\mathscr{L}}^{\dagger}\pi=0
, and Kolmogorov’s backward equation (or
Fokker–Planck equation) shows that
\pi
is invariant.

Uniqueness of
\pi
follows from the fact that the diffusion is irreducible
(with respect to Lebesgue measure) by uniform ellipticity of the noise. One way
of seeing this is to apply Theorem 3.3 in
[
MT93c
]
to show
that the process is Harris recurrent (it almost surely hits any set of positive
measure), owing to the improved estimate on
{\mathscr{L}}V
obtained in
1.2.1
.
∎

Exercise 1.3.2
.

Use integration by parts to compute an explicit expression for
{\mathscr{L}}^{\dagger}
,
and use it to verify that
{\mathscr{L}}^{\dagger}\pi=0
.

Another consequence of the specific form (
1.3.1
) of the
generator is that the diffusion is
reversible
.

Proposition 1.3.3
.

The infinitesimal generator
{\mathscr{L}}
is self-adjoint with respect to the weighted
inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\pi}:=\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\overline{f(y)}g(y)\dd\!y\;.

(1.3.8)

As a consequence, the transition probability density
p_{t}
of the diffusion
satisfies the detailed balance condition

\pi(y)p_{t}(y,z)=\pi(z)p_{t}(z,y)

(1.3.9)

for all
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
and all
t>0
.

Proof:
.

The divergence theorem yields

\langle f,{\mathscr{L}}g\rangle_{\pi}=\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\overline{f(y)}\nabla\cdot\bigl[\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\nabla g(y)\bigr]\dd\!y=-\varepsilon\intop\nolimits\overline{\nabla f(y)}\cdot\nabla g(y)\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\dd\!y\;.

(1.3.10)

Since
\overline{f}
and
g
play a symmetric role in this expression, it is
equal to
\langle{\mathscr{L}}f,g\rangle_{\pi}
. It follows that the Markov semigroup
P_{t}=\e^{t{\mathscr{L}}}
is also self-adjoint, that is,

\langle f,P_{t}g\rangle_{\pi}=\langle P_{t}f,g\rangle_{\pi}\;.

(1.3.11)

Taking for
f
and
g
functions converging to Dirac distributions

\delta_{y}
and
\delta_{z}
yields (
1.3.9
).
∎

Remark 1.3.4
.

The reason we have omitted the normalisation
{\mathscr{Z}}^{-1}
in the weighted inner
product (
1.3.8
) is purely for later notational convenience. Of
course, it does not change anything in the presented results.

1.4
Large deviations

We now focus on the weak-noise regime
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
. The theory of large
deviations provides a useful tool to estimate the probability of rare events as

\varepsilon
decreases to
0
.

Definition 1.4.1

(Large-deviation principle)
.

Fix a time horizon
T\in(0,\infty)
. We say that a function
{\mathscr{I}}={\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}

from
the space
{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
of continuous paths
\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
a

good rate function
if it is lower semi-continuous (its sublevel sets are
closed) and has compact level sets. The process
(y_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfies a
sample-paths
large-deviation principle
(LDP) with good rate function

{\mathscr{I}}

if

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\varepsilon\log\mathbb{P}\bigl\{(y_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in O\bigr\}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\geqslant}-\inf_{\gamma\in O}{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma)

(1.4.1)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\varepsilon\log\mathbb{P}\bigl\{(y_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C\bigr\}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}-\inf_{\gamma\in C}{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma)

(1.4.2)

holds for all open
O\subset{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
and all closed
C\subset{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
.

Roughly speaking, the large-deviation principle says that for a
sufficiently nice set
\Gamma
of paths
\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
,
we have

\mathbb{P}\bigl\{(y_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\Gamma\bigr\}\simeq\e^{-\inf_{\Gamma}{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}/(2\varepsilon)}

(1.4.3)

in the sense of logarithmic equivalence. Here are two illustrative examples:

1.

Let
\gamma_{0}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
be a given continuous path, and let

\Gamma
be the set of paths such that
\lVert\gamma(t)-\gamma_{0}(t)\rVert<\delta

for
all
t\in[0,T]
. As
\delta
decreases to
0
, the infimum of
{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}

over

\Gamma
will converge to
{\mathscr{I}}(\gamma_{0})
. Therefore, the probability of sample
paths tracking
\gamma_{0}
up to a small error
\delta
will be close to

\e^{-{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma_{0})/(2\varepsilon)}
for small
\delta
.

2.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
be a bounded, connected subset of

\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
, fix a point
y_{0}\in D
, and let
\Gamma
be the set of continuous
paths starting in
y_{0}
and leaving
D
at least once during the time interval

(0,T)
. Then we have

{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5muy_{0}}\bigl\{\tau_{D}<T\bigr\}\simeq\e^{-\inf_{\Gamma}{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}/(2\varepsilon)}\;,

(1.4.4)

where
\tau_{D}=\inf\{t>0\colon y_{t}\notin D\}
is the first-exit time from
D
.

In the case of scaled Brownian motion
\sqrt{2\varepsilon}W_{t}
,
Schilder
[
Sch66
]
obtained a large-deviation principle with rate
function

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\lVert\dot{\gamma}(t)\rVert^{2}\dd\!t&\text{if $\gamma\in H^{1}$\;, }\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(1.4.5)

Freidlin and Wenzell
[
FW98
]
extended this result to general
diffusions. Their proof uses the Cameron–Martin–Girsanov formula to reduce
the general problem to the special case of scaled Brownian motion. In the case
of the SDE (
1.2.1
), the LDP takes the following form.

Theorem 1.4.2

(LDP for gradient diffusions)
.

The diffusion (
1.2.1
) satisfies a large-deviation principle with good
rate function

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\lVert\dot{\gamma}(t)+\nabla V(\gamma(t))\rVert^{2}\dd\!t&\text{if $\gamma\in H^{1}$\;, }\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(1.4.6)

For SDEs with more general drift coefficients
f(y)
, the term

\lVert\dot{\gamma}(t)+\nabla V(\gamma(t))\rVert^{2}
in the rate function has to be
replaced by
\lVert\dot{\gamma}(t)-f(\gamma(t))\rVert^{2}
, and there also exists a
version for SDEs with general diffusion coefficients. However, the gradient form
of the system (
1.2.1
) entails a substantial simplification, since we
have the identity

\displaystyle{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\lVert\dot{\gamma}(t)-\nabla V(\gamma(t))\rVert^{2}\dd\!t+2\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\dot{\gamma}(t)\cdot\nabla V(\gamma(t))\dd\!t

(1.4.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\lVert\dot{\gamma}(t)-\nabla V(\gamma(t))\rVert^{2}\dd\!t+2\bigl[V(\gamma(T))-V(\gamma(0))\bigr]\;.

(1.4.8)

If for example we want to analyse properties of the first-exit time from a
potential well
D
, starting from its bottom
y^{*}
, the integral
in (
1.4.8
) can be made arbitrarily small by taking
T

large and letting
\gamma(t)
be the solution of the equation with reversed
drift
\dot{\gamma}=+\nabla V(\gamma)
. The rate function will thus be
dominated by the minimum of the potential difference
2[V(y)-V(y^{*})]

over all
y
on the boundary of
D
.

Exercise 1.4.3
.

Compute the rate function
{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}
in the case of the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck
process

\dd\!y_{t}=-y_{t}\dd\!t+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dd\!W_{t}\;,

(1.4.9)

and use it to analyse the cumulative distribution function of

\tau=\inf\{t>0\colon\lvert y_{t}\rvert>L\}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

1.5
Metastability

The existence of a unique invariant probability measure having been settled,
the next natural question to ask is whether the system will converge to this
measure. In fact, as hinted at in
1.2.1
, in the case of the
diffusion (
1.0.1
) we have the even stronger Lyapunov property

({\mathscr{L}}V)(y)\mathbin{\leqslant}-cV(y)+d

(1.5.1)

for two constants
c>0
and
d\mathbin{\geqslant}0
. According
to
[
MT93c
, Theorem 6.1]
, we have an exponential ergodicity
result of the form

\sup_{f\colon\lvert f\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}V+1}\bigl|\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5muy}\!\left[f(y_{t})\right]-\langle\pi,f\rangle\bigr|\mathbin{\leqslant}C[V(y)+1]\e^{-\beta t}\qquad\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}

(1.5.2)

for some constants
\beta,C>0
. However, this result does not yield a good
control on the constants
\beta
and
C
, and in particular
\beta
can behave
quite badly in terms of
\varepsilon
. This is a manifestation of the phenomenon of

metastability
, which is related to the fact that for small
\varepsilon
, the
system may take extremely long to move between potential wells. A classical
approach to quantifying this phenomenon is thus to investigate the law of this
interwell transition time.

1.5.1
Arrhenius law

For simplicity, we will only consider cases where
V
is a double-well
potential, as is the case for the system (
1.0.1
) for
\gamma>\gamma_{1}(N)
according to
Proposition

1.1.1
. We can always assume that
the saddle is located at the origin, and denote the two local minima by

y^{*}_{\pm}
. Assume that the diffusion starts in
y^{*}_{-}
, and given a small
constant
\delta>0
, denote by

\tau_{+}:=\inf\bigl\{t>0\colon\lVert y_{t}-y^{*}_{+}\rVert<\delta\bigr\}

(1.5.3)

the first-hitting time of a small neighbourhood of
y^{*}_{+}
.

A first type of results on the law of
\tau_{+}
can be obtained by applying the
theory of large deviations outlined in
Section

1.4
.

Theorem 1.5.1

(Large-deviation results for interwell transitions)
.

Let
H=V(0)-V(y^{*}_{-})
be the potential difference between the starting
minimum and the saddle. Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\log\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5muy^{*}_{-}}\!\left[\tau_{+}\right]=H\;.

(1.5.4)

Furthermore, for any
\eta>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5muy^{*}_{-}}\Bigl\{\e^{(H-\eta)/\varepsilon}\mathbin{\leqslant}\tau_{+}\mathbin{\leqslant}\e^{(H+\eta)/\varepsilon}\Bigr\}=1\;.

(1.5.5)

Finally, let
D_{-}
denote the basin of attraction of
y^{*}_{-}
under the
deterministic dynamics. Then the location
y_{\tau_{D_{-}}}
of first exit from

D_{-}
satisfies

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5muy^{*}_{-}}\Bigl\{y_{\tau_{D_{-}}}\in{\mathscr{N}}\Bigr\}=0

(1.5.6)

for any closed
{\mathscr{N}}\subset\partial D_{-}
that does not contain the saddle at
0
.

Relation (
1.5.4
) is called
Arrhenius’ law
. It states that
the expected transition time behaves like
\e^{H/\varepsilon}
in the sense of
logarithmic equivalence. The exponential dependence in
H/\varepsilon
goes back to
works by van t’Hoff and Arrhenius in the late 19th century
[
Arr89
]
.
Relation (
1.5.5
) shows that the law of
\tau_{+}

concentrates around its expectation, albeit in a rather weak sense. The last
result (
1.5.6
) states that the exit location from the
starting potential well concentrates near the saddle in the vanishing noise
limit.

Theorem

1.5.1
can be obtained in two main steps. Firstly, analogous
results for the first exit from a bounded subset of
D_{-}
follow from
Theorems 2.1, 4.1 and 4.2 in
[
FW98
, Chapter 4]
. The main idea
of the proof is that successive attempts to exit this subset are almost
independent, and have an exponentially small probability of success as
discussed at the end of
Section

1.4
. Secondly, these estimates can be
extended to the distribution of
\tau_{+}
by showing that the process behaves
like a Markov chain jumping between neighbourhoods of critical points of
V
,
see Theorems 5.1 and 5.3 in
[
FW98
, Chapter 6]
.

Particularising to the diffusion (
1.0.1
) for
\gamma>\gamma_{1}(N)
, we
obtain that Arrhenius’ law (
1.5.4
) holds with

H=V(0)-V(-1,\dots,-1)=\frac{N}{4}\;.

(1.5.7)

We point out that at this stage, we do not claim any control on the speed of
convergence in (
1.5.4
), (
1.5.5
)
and (
1.5.6
) as a function of
N
. This is a more subtle
point that we will come back to later on.

Remark 1.5.2
.

The case where
\gamma
lies below
\gamma_{1}(N)
but is still of order
N^{2}

can be analysed in a similar way. The main difference is that instead of a
single saddle located at the origin, the number of relevant saddles is
proportional to
N
. Thus
H
is no longer given by (
1.5.7
), and
the exit location from the starting well is concentrated in the union of
the relevant saddles. See
[
BFG07a
, Theorem 2.10]

and
[
BFG07b
, Theorem 2.4]
.

1.5.2
Potential theory

One of the approaches allowing to obtain sharper asymptotics on metastable
transition times is based on potential theory. The starting point is the
observation that owing to Dynkin’s formula, several probabilistic quantities of
interest solve boundary value problems involving the infinitesimal generator

{\mathscr{L}}
of the process.

Let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
be a closed set with smooth boundary, and let

\tau_{A}=\inf\{t>0\colon y_{t}\in A\}
be the first-hitting time of
A
. Then the
map
y\mapsto w_{A}(y)=\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5muy}\!\left[\tau_{A}\right]
satisfies the Poisson problem

\begin{cases}({\mathscr{L}}w_{A})(y)=-1&\qquad y\in A^{c}\;,\\
w_{A}(y)=0&\qquad y\in A\;.\end{cases}

(1.5.8)

This is a particular case of
[
Øks85
, Corollary 9.1.2]
.

y^{*}_{-}
z^{*}
y^{*}_{+}
a
y
V(y)

Figure 1.2:
A one-dimensional double-well potential.

Exercise 1.5.3
.

Consider the SDE (
1.2.1
) in the one-dimensional case
y\in\mathbb{R}
, for a
potential
V
growing sufficiently fast at infinity. Show that for

A=(-\infty,a]
, the solution of (
1.5.8
) is given by

w_{A}(y)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{a}^{y}\intop\nolimits_{y_{2}}^{\infty}\e^{[V(y_{2})-V(y_{1})]/\varepsilon}\dd\!y_{1}\dd\!y_{2}\qquad\forall y>a\;.

(1.5.9)

Assume now that
V
is a double-well potential, with two local minima located
at
y^{*}_{-}<a
and
y^{*}_{+}>a
, and a saddle (local maximum) at
z^{*}\in(a,y^{*}_{+})
(
Figure

1.2
). Use Laplace asymptotics to show that

w_{A}(y)=\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{\lvert V^{\prime\prime}(z^{*})\rvert V^{\prime\prime}(y^{*}_{+})}}\e^{[V(z^{*})-V(y^{*}_{+})]/\varepsilon}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\bigr]\;.

(1.5.10)

Relation (
1.5.10
) is known as
Kramers’ law
.

In general, there is no explicit solution to the Poisson
equation (
1.5.8
). However, the solution can be represented as

w_{A}(y)=-\intop\nolimits_{A^{c}}G_{A^{c}}(y,z)\dd\!z\;,

(1.5.11)

where
G_{A^{c}}
is the
Green function
associated with
A^{c}
, which
solves

\begin{cases}({\mathscr{L}}G_{A^{c}})(y,z)=\delta(y-z)&\qquad y\in A^{c}\;,\\
G_{A^{c}}(y,z)=0&\qquad y\in A\;.\end{cases}

(1.5.12)

Reversibility of the SDE (
1.2.1
) implies that
G_{A^{c}}
satisfies the
detailed-balance relation

\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}G_{A^{c}}(y,z)=\e^{-V(z)/\varepsilon}G_{A^{c}}(z,y)\;.

(1.5.13)

Let now
A
and
B
be two disjoint closed sets with smooth boundary.
A second quantity of interest is the
committor function

h_{AB}(y)=\mathbb{P}^{\mskip 1.5muy}\{\tau_{A}<\tau_{B}\}
, also called
equilibrium
potential
. It satisfies the Dirichlet problem

\begin{cases}({\mathscr{L}}h_{AB})(y)=0&\qquad y\in(A\cup B)^{c}\;,\\
h_{AB}(y)=1&\qquad y\in A\;,\\
h_{AB}(y)=0&\qquad y\in B\;.\end{cases}

(1.5.14)

Exercise 1.5.4
.

Consider again the SDE (
1.2.1
) in the one-dimensional case
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
Given
a<b
, let
A=(-\infty,a]
and
B=[b,\infty)
. Show that

h_{AB}(y)=\frac{\displaystyle\intop\nolimits_{y}^{b}\e^{V(z)/\varepsilon}\dd\!z}{\displaystyle\intop\nolimits_{a}^{b}\e^{V(z)/\varepsilon}\dd\!z}\qquad\forall y\in[a,b]\;.

(1.5.15)

Use Laplace asymptotics to determine the behaviour of
h_{AB}(y)
as

\varepsilon\to 0
.

There exists again an integral representation of the solution
to (
1.5.14
) in terms of the Green function, namely

h_{AB}(y)=-\intop\nolimits_{\partial A}G_{B^{c}}(y,z)e_{AB}(\dd\!z)\;.

(1.5.16)

Here
e_{AB}
is a measure concentrated on
\partial A
called the

equilibrium measure
. It is defined by

e_{AB}(\dd\!y):=(-{\mathscr{L}}h_{AB})(dy)

(1.5.17)

interpreted in the weak sense (i.e., both sides have to be integrated against
test functions). The
capacity
is then defined by

\capacity(A,B):=\intop\nolimits_{\partial A}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}e_{AB}(\dd\!y)\;.

(1.5.18)

Note that the capacity is the normalisation required to make

\nu_{AB}(\dd\!y):=\frac{1}{\capacity(A,B)}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}e_{AB}(\dd\!y)

(1.5.19)

a probability measure on
\partial A
.

Remark 1.5.5
.

The name
capacity
is due to an analogy with electrostatics. Indeed, in
the case without potential
V=0
, (
1.5.14
) is the equation for the
electric potential of a capacitor, consisting of a conductor
A
at potential

1
and a grounded conductor
B
. The integral relation (
1.5.16
)
expresses the fact that
h_{AB}
is the electric potential created by a charge
density
e_{AB}
on
\partial A
with zero boundary conditions on
B
. Since
the
potential difference between the conductors is equal to
1
, the capacity is
equal to the total charge on the conductor
A
.

The main result that makes the potential-theoretic approach so successful is
the following relation between expected first-hitting time and capacity.

Theorem 1.5.6
.

For any disjoint sets
A
and
B
with smooth boundary,

\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mu\nu_{AB}}\!\left[\tau_{B}\right]:=\intop\nolimits_{\partial A}w_{B}(y)\nu_{AB}(\dd\!y)=\frac{1}{\capacity(A,B)}\intop\nolimits_{B^{c}}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}h_{AB}(y)\dd\!y\;.

(1.5.20)

Proof:
.

It follows from the integral representation (
1.5.11
) of
w_{B}
,
the detailed balance (
1.5.13
) of the Green function, and the
integral representation (
1.5.16
) of
h_{AB}
, that

\displaystyle\capacity(A,B)\intop\nolimits_{\partial A}w_{B}(y)\nu_{AB}(\dd\!y)

\displaystyle=-\intop\nolimits_{\partial A}\intop\nolimits_{B^{c}}G_{B^{c}}(y,z)\dd\!z\,\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}e_{AB}(\dd\!y)

(1.5.21)

\displaystyle=-\intop\nolimits_{B^{c}}\intop\nolimits_{\partial A}G_{B^{c}}(z,y)e_{AB}(\dd\!y)\e^{-V(z)/\varepsilon}\dd\!z

(1.5.22)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{B^{c}}h_{AB}(z)\e^{-V(z)/\varepsilon}\dd\!z\;.

(1.5.23)

Dividing by the capacity yields (
1.5.20
).
∎

The exact relation (
1.5.20
) is useful because there exist
variational principles that often allow to obtain good upper and lower bounds
on the capacity. The first of these principles states that the capacity is a
minimiser of the
Dirichlet form
.

Definition 1.5.7

(Dirichlet form)
.

The
Dirichlet form
associated with the diffusion (
1.2.1
) is the
quadratic form acting on test functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}\to\mathbb{R}
in the domain of

{\mathscr{L}}
, defined by

{\mathscr{E}}(f):=\langle f,-{\mathscr{L}}f\rangle_{\pi}=\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\lVert\nabla f(y)\rVert^{2}\dd\!y\;.

(1.5.24)

The integral expression for the Dirichlet form in (
1.5.24
) is a
consequence of the first Green identity, which is essentially the divergence
theorem (using the fact that
\e^{-V/\varepsilon}[\varepsilon\nabla f\cdot\nabla g+g{\mathscr{L}}f]=\varepsilon\nabla\cdot(g\e^{-V/\varepsilon}\nabla f)
) and says that for a set
D
with smooth
boundary,

\intop\nolimits_{D}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\bigl[\varepsilon\nabla f(y)\cdot\nabla g(y)+g(y)({\mathscr{L}}f)(y)\bigr]\dd\!y=\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\partial D}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}g(y)\partial_{n(y)}f(y)\sigma(\dd\!y)\;,

(1.5.25)

where
\smash{\partial_{n(y)}}f(y)=\nabla f(y)\cdot n(y)
denotes the
derivative in the direction of the unit outer normal
n(y)
at a point

y\in\partial D
, and
\sigma(\dd\!y)
is the Lebesgue measure on
\partial D
. In
the particular case
D=\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
, the right-hand side of (
1.5.25
)
vanishes and one obtains the integral expression in (
1.5.24
).

The quadratic form
{\mathscr{E}}
can be extended by polarisation to a bilinear form
given by

{\mathscr{E}}(f,g)=\langle f,-{\mathscr{L}}g\rangle_{\pi}=\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\overline{\nabla f(y)}\cdot\nabla g(y)\dd\!y\;,

(1.5.26)

which satisfies the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality

{\mathscr{E}}(f,g)^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{E}}(f){\mathscr{E}}(g)
.

Theorem 1.5.8

(Dirichlet principle)
.

Let
{\mathscr{H}}_{AB}
be the set of functions
h:\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}\to[0,1]
that are in
the domain of
{\mathscr{E}}
and such that
h|_{A}=1
and
h|_{B}=0
. Then

\capacity(A,B)={\mathscr{E}}(h_{AB})=\inf_{h\in{\mathscr{H}}_{AB}}{\mathscr{E}}(h)\;.

(1.5.27)

Proof:
.

Pick any
h\in{\mathscr{H}}_{AB}
. Since
h_{AB}
satisfies the Dirichlet
problem (
1.5.14
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}(h,h_{AB})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}h(y)(-{\mathscr{L}}h_{AB})(y)\dd\!y

(1.5.28)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\partial A}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}e_{AB}(\dd\!y)

(1.5.29)

\displaystyle=\capacity(A,B)\;.

(1.5.30)

Since
h_{AB}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{AB}
, we obtain
{\mathscr{E}}(h_{AB})=\capacity(A,B)
.
The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality then yields

{\mathscr{E}}(h_{AB})^{2}={\mathscr{E}}(h,h_{AB})^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{E}}(h){\mathscr{E}}(h_{AB})
, i.e.
{\mathscr{E}}(h)\mathbin{\geqslant}{\mathscr{E}}(h_{AB})
. A sketch is given
in
Figure

1.3
.
∎

0
h_{AB}
h
{\mathscr{H}}_{AB}

Figure 1.3:
Dirichlet principle. The capacity minimises the distance to the
origin given by the Dirichlet form over all functions
h
satisfying the
boundary conditions.

There also exists a variational principle allowing to obtain lower bounds on
the capacity, the
Thomson principle
, which involves divergence-free
flows.

Definition 1.5.9

(Divergence-free unit flow)
.

A divergence-free unit
AB
-flow is a vector field
\varphi
of class

{\mathscr{C}}^{1}
on
(A\cup B)^{c}
such that
\nabla\cdot\varphi=0
in
(A\cup B)^{c}
and

\intop\nolimits_{\partial A}\varphi(y)\cdot n_{A}(y)\sigma(\dd\!y)=1=-\intop\nolimits_{\partial B}\varphi(y)\cdot n_{B}(y)\sigma(\dd\!y)\;,

(1.5.31)

where
n_{A}(y)
,
n_{B}(y)
denote the unit outward normal vectors at

y\in\partial A
and
y\in\partial B
respectively. We denote by

\mathfrak{U}^{1}_{AB}
the set of divergence-free unit

AB
-flows.
The harmonic unit flow is defined as

\varphi_{AB}(y):=-\frac{\varepsilon}{\capacity(A,B)}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\nabla h_{AB}(y)

(1.5.32)

and it satisfies
\varphi_{AB}\in\mathfrak{U}^{1}_{AB}
.

The fact that
\varphi_{AB}
is divergence-free follows from the
form (
1.3.1
) of the generator and the fact that
h_{AB}
is
harmonic. Its satisfies (
1.5.31
) as a consequence of the
divergence theorem.

We define a bilinear form on
\mathfrak{U}^{1}_{AB}
by

{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi,\psi):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{(A\cup B)^{c}}\e^{V(y)/\varepsilon}\varphi(y)\cdot\psi(y)\dd\!y\;,

(1.5.33)

and set
{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi,\varphi):={\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)
.

Proposition 1.5.10

(Thomson principle)
.

The capacity satisfies

\capacity(A,B)=\frac{1}{{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi_{AB})}=\sup_{\varphi\in\mathfrak{U}^{1}_{AB}}\frac{1}{{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)}\;.

(1.5.34)

Proof:
.

Set
{}_{h_{AB}}=\capacity(A,B)\varphi_{AB}=-\varepsilon\e^{-V/\varepsilon}\nabla h_{AB}
. Then

{\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}})=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{(A\cup B)^{c}}\e^{V(y)/\varepsilon}\lvert\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}\nabla h_{AB}(y)\rvert^{2}\dd\!y=\capacity(A,B)\;.

(1.5.35)

This implies
{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi_{AB})=(\capacity(A,B))^{-1}
by bilinearity.
For any unit flow
\varphi\in\mathfrak{U}^{1}_{AB}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{(A\cup B)^{c}}\e^{V(y)/\varepsilon}{}_{h_{AB}}(y)\cdot\varphi(y)\dd\!y

(1.5.36)

\displaystyle=-\intop\nolimits_{(A\cup B)^{c}}\nabla h_{AB}(y)\cdot\varphi(y)\dd\!y

(1.5.37)

\displaystyle=-\intop\nolimits_{(A\cup B)^{c}}\nabla\cdot\bigl(h_{AB}(y)\varphi(y)\bigr)\dd\!y+\intop\nolimits_{(A\cup B)^{c}}h_{AB}(y)\nabla\cdot\varphi(y)\dd\!y\;.

(1.5.38)

The second integral vanishes because
\varphi
is divergence-free. By the
divergence
theorem, the first term is equal to

-\intop\nolimits_{\partial A\cup\partial B}h_{AB}(y)\varphi(y)\cdot n(y)\sigma(\dd\!y)\;,

(1.5.39)

where
n(y)
is the unit normal vector pointing
inside

A\cup B
. The
integral on
\partial B
vanishes, while the integral on
\partial A
is equal
to
1
because
n(y)=-n_{A}(y)
and
\varphi

satisfies (
1.5.31
).
This implies

{\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}},\varphi)=1
,
and thus, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

1={\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}},\varphi)^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}}){\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)=\capacity(A,B){\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)\;,

(1.5.40)

showing that indeed
\capacity(A,B)\mathbin{\geqslant}1/{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)
.
∎

Remark 1.5.11
.

The potential-theoretic approach described in this section admits a simple
analogue in the setting of discrete Markov chains. This will not play any role
in what follows, but for completeness and since it may help intuition, we
summarise this theory in
Chapter

A
.

1.5.3
Eyring–Kramers law

We now apply the potential-theoretic approach to the system of interacting
diffusions, in order to obtain sharper asymptotics on the transition time
between the two states
y^{*}_{\pm}=\pm(1,\dots,1)
. For simplicity, we only
consider the case
\gamma>\gamma_{1}(N)
, when the potential is a double-well
potential with a saddle located at the origin. The theory works, however, in
much greater generality.

One simplification due to the condition
\gamma>\gamma_{1}(N)
is that we will
be able to use the following symmetry argument.

Lemma 1.5.12
.

Let
A
and
B
satisfy
B=-A
. Then

\intop\nolimits_{(A\cup B)^{c}}\e^{-V(y)/\varepsilon}h_{AB}(y)\dd\!y=\frac{1}{2}{\mathscr{Z}}

(1.5.41)

where
{\mathscr{Z}}
is the
partition function
of the system defined
in (
1.3.2
).

Exercise 1.5.13
.

Prove
Lemma

1.5.12
, using the fact that
V(-y)=V(y)
and the
relations
h_{AB}(y)=h_{BA}(-y)
and
h_{AB}(y)=1-h_{BA}(y)
.

To apply
Theorem

1.5.6
, it is thus sufficient to estimate the
partition function
{\mathscr{Z}}
and the capacity
\capacity(A,B)
. In order to do so,
it
turns out to be useful to make a change of variables. Let
(e_{0},\dots,e_{N-1})

be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
, where

e_{0}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}(1,\dots,1)^{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm T$}}}\;.

(1.5.42)

The precise form of the other basis vectors will not matter – one possibility
is to use those appearing in the discrete Fourier transform. We denote by

\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}_{\perp}
the span of
e_{1},\dots,e_{N-1}
. The change of variables is
given by

y=y_{0}e_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y_{\perp}

(1.5.43)

where

y_{0}=y\cdot e_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}y^{i}\;,\qquad y_{\perp}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\bigl(y-y_{0}e_{0}\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}_{\perp}\;.

(1.5.44)

The role of the factor
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
is to highlight the scaling properties of
some quantities with
\varepsilon
. Due to this factor, the Jacobian of the
transformation
y\mapsto(y_{0},y_{\perp})
is equal to
\varepsilon^{(N-1)/2}
. Performing
the change of variables in the potential
V
, and using the fact that the sum
of the coordinates of
y_{\perp}
vanishes, we obtain

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(y)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V_{0}(y_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\langle y_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(y_{0})y_{\perp}\rangle+R_{\varepsilon}(y_{0},y_{\perp})\;.

(1.5.45)

Here

V_{0}(y_{0}):=NU\Bigl(\frac{y_{0}}{\sqrt{N}}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{4N}y_{0}^{4}-\frac{1}{2}y_{0}^{2}+\frac{N}{4}\;,

(1.5.46)

while
\langle y_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(y_{0})y_{\perp}\rangle
is the quadratic form defined by

Q_{\perp}(y_{0}):=\Bigl(\frac{3}{N}y_{0}^{2}-1\Bigr){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}-\gamma\Delta_{\perp,N}

(1.5.47)

where
\Delta_{\perp,N}
is the discrete Laplacian acting on

\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}_{\perp}
, and
R_{\varepsilon}
is a remainder given by

R_{\varepsilon}(y_{0},y_{\perp})=\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{N}}y_{0}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\Lambda}\bigl(y^{i}_{\perp}\bigr)^{3}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\Lambda}\bigl(y^{i}_{\perp}\bigr)^{4}\;.

(1.5.48)

Proposition 1.5.14
.

The partition function has the asymptotic form

{\mathscr{Z}}=2\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi\varepsilon)^{N}}{2\det Q_{\perp}(-\sqrt{N})}}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]=2\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi\varepsilon)^{N}}{\prodop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{N-1}\nu^{N}_{k}}}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;,

(1.5.49)

where the
\nu^{N}_{k}
are the eigenvalues (
1.1.9
) of the Hessian of

V(y^{*}_{-})
.

Proof:
.

This result follows rather directly from standard Laplace asymptotics, but we
will give some details of the proof as they will be useful later on.
Using (
1.5.45
), we get

{\mathscr{Z}}=\varepsilon^{(N-1)/2}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{-V_{0}(y_{0})/\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}_{\perp}}\e^{-\langle y_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(y_{0})y_{\perp}\rangle/2}\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}(y_{0},y_{\perp})}\dd\!y_{\perp}\dd\!y_{0}\;.

(1.5.50)

The idea is to view the integral over
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}_{\perp}
as an expectation
under the Gaussian measure
g_{N}(y_{0})
with covariance
Q_{\perp}(y_{0})^{-1}
.
Taking the normalisation into account, we obtain

{\mathscr{Z}}=\varepsilon^{(N-1)/2}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{-V_{0}(y_{0})/\varepsilon}\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi)^{N-1}}{\det Q_{\perp}(y_{0})}}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(y_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}(y_{0},y_{\perp})}\bigr]\dd\!y_{0}\;.

(1.5.51)

The expectation is bounded uniformly in
\varepsilon
and
N
, because the potential
satisfies the quadratic lower bound (
1.1.10
) derived in

1.1.2
. Therefore it converges to
1
as
\varepsilon\to 0
by the
dominated convergence theorem. The result then follows by one-dimensional
Laplace asymptotics for the integral over
y_{0}
, since
V_{0}
has quadratic
minima in
\pm\sqrt{N}
. The fact that the error has order
\varepsilon
instead of

\sqrt{\varepsilon}
is due to the fact that the potential is even in
y_{0}
.
∎

Remark 1.5.15
.

We have not claimed that the error term
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
is (
1.5.49
) is
uniform in
N
. In fact, this is indeed the case, but proving it needs a little
bit more work. We will come back to this point in
Section

2.7
.

To simplify the computation of the capacity, we will assume that the sets
A

and
B
are of the form

A=-B=\bigl\{y\colon\lvert y_{0}+\sqrt{N}\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}\delta,y_{\perp}\in D_{\perp}\bigr\},

(1.5.52)

where
\delta\ll\sqrt{N}
and
D_{\perp}
is a ball sufficiently large for
A\cup B
to contain most of the mass of the invariant measure
\pi
, in the sense
that
\pi((A\cup B)^{c})={\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
. This holds for
D_{\perp}
of radius of
order
\sqrt{\log(\varepsilon^{-1})}
, see for
instance
[
BDGW17
, Lemma 5.9]
.

Proposition 1.5.16
.

For
A
and
B
satisfying (
1.5.52
), one has

\capacity(A,B)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi\varepsilon)^{N}\lvert\mu^{N}_{0}\rvert}{2\det Q_{\perp}(0)}}\e^{-N/(4\varepsilon)}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]=\frac{\lvert\mu^{N}_{0}\rvert}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi\varepsilon)^{N}}{\prodop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{N-1}\mu^{N}_{k}}}\e^{-N/(4\varepsilon)}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;,

(1.5.53)

where the
\mu^{N}_{k}
are the eigenvalues (
1.1.8
) of the Hessian of

V(0)
.

Proof:
.

We will apply the Dirichlet and Thomson principles with appropriate choices of

h
and
\varphi
. For the upper bound, we use a function depending only on the
coordinate
y_{0}
, and which is simply given by the one-dimensional
committor (
1.5.15
) derived in
1.5.4
:

h(y)=h_{0}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{c_{0}}\intop\nolimits_{y}^{a}\e^{V_{0}(\xi)/\varepsilon}\dd\!\xi\;,\qquad c_{0}:=\intop\nolimits_{-a}^{a}\e^{V_{0}(\xi)/\varepsilon}\dd\!\xi\;,

(1.5.54)

where
a=\sqrt{N}-\delta
is the left boundary of
B
. For
\lvert y_{0}\rvert>a
,

h_{0}(y_{0})
is continuously extended by constant values
1
or
0
. Inserting
this in the Dirichlet form, we obtain

{\mathscr{E}}(h)=\frac{\varepsilon}{c_{0}^{2}}\varepsilon^{(N-1)/2}\intop\nolimits_{-a}^{a}\e^{V_{0}(y_{0})/\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}_{\perp}}\e^{-\langle y_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(y_{0})y_{\perp}\rangle/2}\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}(y_{0},y_{\perp})}\dd\!y_{\perp}\dd\!y_{0}\;.

(1.5.55)

Observe that the sign of
V_{0}
has changed in the exponent with respect
to (
1.5.50
), which means that
y_{0}
close to
0
will now dominate
the integral. Writing again the integral over
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}_{\perp}
as an
expectation under the Gaussian measure
g_{N}
yields an upper bound of the
desired form, noting that
c_{0}=\e^{V_{0}(0)/\varepsilon}[2\pi\varepsilon/\lvert\mu^{N}_{0}\rvert]^{1/2}[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)]
and
V_{0}(0)=N/4
.

For the lower bound, we apply the Thomson principle with the unit
flow
1
1

1

We could have included the quartic part of the potential in the
exponent as well, yielding a better control on the decay for large
y_{\perp}
.
This is, however, not needed if we do not care about uniformity in
N
.

\varphi(y)=\frac{1}{K}\mathbbm{1}_{D_{\perp}}(y_{\perp})\e^{-\langle y_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(0)y_{\perp}\rangle/2}e_{0}\;,\qquad K:=\varepsilon^{(N-1)/2}\intop\nolimits_{D_{\perp}}\e^{-\langle y_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(0)y_{\perp}\rangle/2}\dd\!y_{\perp}\;.

(1.5.56)

Since
\varphi
depends only on
y_{\perp}
and is directed along
e_{0}
, it is indeed
divergence-free. In addition, it has intensity
1
by definition of
K
. We
obtain

{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon K}\intop\nolimits_{-a}^{a}\e^{V_{0}(y_{0})/\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(0)}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{D_{\perp}}\e^{\langle\cdot,[Q_{N}(\varphi_{0})-Q_{\perp}(0)]\cdot\rangle/2}\e^{R_{\varepsilon}(y_{0},y_{\perp})}\bigr]\dd\!y_{0}\;.

(1.5.57)

The expectation is bounded because
D_{\perp}
is bounded, and equal to

1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
thanks to the assumption on
D_{\perp}
. The result then follows
by computing the Gaussian integral
K
and performing Laplace asymptotics on
the integral over
y_{0}
.
∎

Combining the above estimates, we obtain the following sharp asymptotics on
the transition time, which is the main result of this section.

Theorem 1.5.17

(Eyring–Kramers law for the double-well situation)
.

Assume
\gamma>\gamma_{1}(N)
and
B
is as in (
1.5.52
). Then

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5muy^{*}_{-}}\bigl[\tau_{B}\bigr]=\frac{2\pi}{\lvert\mu^{N}_{0}\rvert}\sqrt{\frac{\lvert\det\Hess V(0)\rvert}{\det\Hess V(y^{*}_{-})}}\e^{[V(0)-V(y^{*}_{-})]/\varepsilon}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;,

(1.5.58)

where
\Hess V(y)
denotes the Hessian matrix of
V
at
y
.

Proof:
.

The result when starting with the distribution
\nu_{AB}
follows directly
by inserting (
1.5.49
) and (
1.5.53
) in the exact
relation obtained in
Theorem

1.5.6
. To extend this to solutions
starting in
y^{*}_{-}
, there are two possibilities. One of them is to use Harnack
inequalities, which bound the oscillation of harmonic functions, to show that

w_{B}(y)
does not depend too badly on
y
, cf.
[
BEGK04
, Lemma 4.6]
. An
alternative is to use a coupling argument as
in
[
MOS89
]
.
∎

Note that (
1.5.58
) is indeed a generalisation to
higher dimension of the one-dimensional expression (
1.5.10
) obtained
in
1.5.3
.

Remark 1.5.18
.

Similar results as (
1.5.58
) hold in much more
general finite-dimensional situations, with a less sharp control of the error
term, including situations with more than
2
wells
[
BEGK04
]
. Furthermore,
the spectral gap of the generator
{\mathscr{L}}
can be shown to be exponentially close
to the inverse of the expected transition
time (
1.5.58
)
[
BGK05
]
.

1.6
Bibliographical notes

The system (
1.0.1
) of coupled diffusions was introduced
in
[
BFG07a
,
BFG07b
]
to understand the general theory of metastability in a
specific example. These works provide a number of results on the potential
landscape, both for small and large coupling
\gamma
, and asymptotic results
for large
N
. In particular,
Proposition

1.1.1

is
[
BFG07a
, Proposition 2.2]
.

General results on solutions of SDEs can be found in the
monographs
[
McK69
,
Øks85
,
KS91
,
Mao97
]
.
The use of Lyapunov functions to prove non-explosion, Harris recurrence, and
various ergodicity results has been developed by Meyn and Tweedie in a series
of works
[
MT92
,
MT93b
,
MT93c
]
, as well
as the monograph
[
MT93a
]
.

The theory of large deviations for SDEs is developed by Freidlin and Wenzell
in the monograph
[
FW98
]
. Other general monographs on large deviations
include
[
DZ98
,
DS89
]
.

The potential-theoretic approach to metastability was mainly developed
in
[
BEGK02
]
for Markov chains, and in
[
BEGK04
,
BGK05
,
Eck05
]
for
reversible diffusions. A comprehensive account of the potential-theoretic
approach can be found in the monograph
[
BdH15
]
. Short
overviews are also found in
[
Slo12b
,
Ber13
]
. The Thomson
principle is proved (in a more general, non-reversible setting)
in
[
LMS19
]
.

Another successful approach to sharp asymptotics for metastable transition
times is based on semiclassical analysis of the Witten Laplacian, and was
initiated in
[
HKN04
,
HN05
]
. Extensions can be found,
e.g., in
[
LP10
,
LP11
,
LPNV13
]
.

There exist several extensions of the results on the Eyring–Kramers
formula presented here. The case of saddles with vanishing Hessian determinant
has been considered in
[
BG10
]
. Situations with many
degeneracies due to symmetries have been considered in
[
BD16a
]
for
markovian jump processes, and
[
Dut15
,
BD16b
]
for diffusions.
Uniformity in
N
of the error terms in the Eyring–Kramers formula for the
system (
1.0.1
) was obtained in
[
BBM10
]
. Results on
the spectral gap that are uniform in
N
have been obtained
in
[
DGLP17
]
.

Chapter 2
Allen–Cahn SPDE in one space dimension

Consider the formal limit of the system (
1.0.1
) of coupled diffusions as

N\to\infty
with
\gamma\sim N^{2}
. Given a parameter
L>0
, that we will
choose below as a function of the limit of
\gamma/N^{2}
, we define a function

\phi(t,x)
by setting

y^{i}_{t}=\phi\biggl(t,\frac{i}{N}L\biggr)\;,\qquad i\in\Lambda

(2.0.1)

and interpolating linearly (or with some higher-order polynomials) between
lattice points. The discrete Laplacian then formally satisfies, for
x=(i/N)L\in(0,L]
,

\displaystyle y^{i+1}_{t}-2y^{i}_{t}+y^{i-1}_{t}

\displaystyle=\phi\biggl(t,x+\frac{L}{N}\biggr)-2\phi(t,x)+\phi\biggl(t,x-\frac{L}{N}\biggr)

(2.0.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{L^{2}}{N^{2}}\partial_{xx}\phi(t,x)+{\mathcal{O}}\Bigl(\frac{L^{4}}{N^{4}}\Bigr)\;.

(2.0.3)

Taking
L
such that

L^{2}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{2N^{2}}{\gamma}\;,

(2.0.4)

we see that the SDE (
1.0.1
) converges formally, as
N\to\infty
, to the
equation

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\partial_{xx}\phi(t,x)+\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)\;,

(2.0.5)

where
\xi(t,x)
is a stochastic process called
space-time white noise
,
which we will have to properly define. In what follows, we will write
\Delta\phi
instead of
\partial_{xx}\phi
, even though
x
is one-dimensional,
because the same notation will apply for higher-dimensional
x
. Note in
particular that for the critical value
\gamma_{1}(N)
of
\gamma
obtained in

Proposition

1.1.1
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{2N^{2}}{\gamma_{1}(N)}=(2\pi)^{2}\;,

(2.0.6)

indicating that the value
L=2\pi
will play a special role.

Remark 2.0.1
.

The equation with a negative coefficient in front of
\phi

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)-m^{2}\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)

(2.0.7)

(where
m^{2}\mathbin{\geqslant}0
) is called the
\Phi^{4}

model

[
GJ68
,
GJS75
,
GJ81
]
(massive
\Phi^{4}
model if

m>0
) or
stochastic quantisation equation

[
PW81
]
, and plays an
important role in Quantum Field Theory. The solution theory is the same
for (
2.0.5
) and (
2.0.7
), but their long-time behaviour is
very different, since (
2.0.5
) displays metastability
while (
2.0.7
) does not.

2.1
Deterministic dynamics

We start by briefly analysing (
2.0.5
) in the deterministic case

\varepsilon=0
, where it takes the form of the PDE

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}\;.

(2.1.1)

This equation is commonly known as
Allen–Cahn equation
, though one
finds other names in the literature, including
Chaffee–Infante
equation
, or
real Ginzburg–Landau equation
.

In the present setting,
x
belongs to the scaled circle
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}/(L\mathbb{Z})
,
which implicitly implies that we consider (
2.1.1
) with periodic
boundary conditions.

A first useful observation is that the right-hand side of (
2.1.1
)
derives again from a potential, obtained as the continuum limit of the
potential (
1.1.3
), which is given by

V(\phi):=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{L}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\Bigl(\phi(x)^{2}-1\Bigr)^{2}\biggr]\;.

(2.1.2)

Here we have written
\nabla\phi(x)
instead of
\partial_{x}\phi(x)
, to have a
notation also valid in higher dimensions. Indeed, the Gâteaux derivative of

V
at
\phi
in the direction
\psi
is given by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\psi}V(\phi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}V(\phi+\lambda\psi)\Bigr|_{\lambda=0}

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{L}\bigl[\nabla\phi(x)\cdot\nabla\psi(x)-\phi(x)\psi(x)+\phi(x)^{3}\psi(x)\bigr]\dd\!x

(2.1.4)

\displaystyle=-\langle\Delta\phi+\phi-\phi^{3},\psi\rangle_{L^{2}}\;,

(2.1.5)

where we have used integration by parts and the periodic boundary conditions in
the last step. In particular, this shows that stationary solutions
of (
2.1.1
) are critical points of
V
.

0
2\pi
4\pi
6\pi
8\pi
L
\phi^{*}_{0}
\phi^{*}_{1,\theta}
\phi^{*}_{2,\theta}
\phi^{*}_{3,\theta}
\phi^{*}_{4,\theta}
\phi^{*}_{\pm}

Figure 2.1:
Schematic bifurcation diagram of the deterministic Allen–Cahn
equation (
2.1.1
). The vertical coordinate represents roughly the
amplitude of the stationary solution.

The role of the Hessian of
V
at
\phi
is played by the
bilinear form mapping two periodic functions
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
to

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}V(\phi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}V(\phi+\lambda_{1}\psi_{1}+\lambda_{2}\psi_{2})\Bigr|_{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0}

(2.1.6)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{L}\bigl[\nabla\psi_{1}(x)\cdot\nabla\psi_{2}(x)-\psi_{1}(x)\psi_{2}(x)+3\phi(x)^{2}\psi_{1}(x)\psi_{2}(x)\bigr]\dd\!x

(2.1.7)

\displaystyle=\langle\psi_{1},[-\Delta-1+3\phi(\cdot)^{2}]\psi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}\;.

(2.1.8)

This reflects the fact that the linearisation of (
2.1.1
) around a
stationary solution
\phi^{*}
is given by the variational equation

\partial_{t}\psi(t,x)=\Delta\psi(t,x)+\bigl[1-3\phi^{*}(x)^{2}\bigr]\psi(t,x)\;.

(2.1.9)

Determining the stability of
\phi^{*}
is equivalent to solving the
Sturm–Liouville problem consisting in computing the spectrum
of (
2.1.9
) with periodic boundary conditions.

The PDE (
2.1.1
) has three obvious stationary solutions, which are
constant in space, and natural analogues of the constant stationary solutions
of the discrete system. We will denote by
\phi^{*}_{\pm}
the constant solutions
equal to
\pm 1
, and by
\phi^{*}_{0}
the constant solution equal to
0
. The
following result describes the set of all stationary solutions and their
stability. The bifurcation diagram is sketched in
Figure

2.1
.

Proposition 2.1.1
.

The stationary solutions
\phi^{*}_{\pm}
are always stable. The solution

\phi^{*}_{0}
has one unstable direction if
0<L<2\pi
, and more generally
2k+1

unstable directions if
2k\pi<L<2(k+1)\pi
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
In addition, at every multiple of
2\pi
, a one-parameter family of
non-constant solutions bifurcates from the origin. These solutions are
unstable, and of the form
\phi^{*}_{k,\theta}(x)=\phi^{*}_{k,0}(x+\theta)
.

Proof:
.

Setting
\phi=\pm 1
in (
2.1.8
), we find that the Hessian of

V
at
\phi^{*}_{\pm}
is given by
-\Delta+2
. This has eigenvalues

\nu_{k}=\biggl(\frac{2k\pi}{L}\biggr)^{2}+2\;,\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}\;,

(2.1.10)

which are all positive. Therefore, the Sturm–Liouville
equation (
2.1.9
) has negative spectrum, and
\phi^{*}_{\pm}
are
stable. In the case of
\phi^{*}_{0}
, we obtain a Hessian equal to
-\Delta-1
,
which has eigenvalues

\mu_{k}=\biggl(\frac{2k\pi}{L}\biggr)^{2}-1\;,\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}\;.

(2.1.11)

Therefore,
\mu_{0}
is always negative, while two additional
\mu_{k}
become
negative whenever
L
exceeds
2k\pi
.

H=\frac{1}{4}
q
p
-1
1

Figure 2.2:
Solutions of the Hamilton equations (
2.1.14
). Closed
orbits correspond to periodic boundary conditions, while the orbit shown in red
corresponds to Neumann boundary conditions. The curves
\{H=\frac{1}{4}\}
are
separatrices reached in the limit of the period going to infinity.

The other stationary solutions are periodic solutions of

\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)=\phi(x)^{3}-\phi(x)

(2.1.12)

of period
L
.
This second-order ODE is equivalent to the Hamiltonian system

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=p(x)

(2.1.13)

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=q(x)^{3}-q(x)\;,

(2.1.14)

which derives from the Hamiltonian

H(p,q)=\frac{1}{2}p^{2}+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}-\frac{1}{4}q^{4}\;.

(2.1.15)

The energy
H
is constant along solutions of (
2.1.14
), and
therefore orbits are contained in level curves of
H
. Some level curves are
shown in
Figure

2.2
. Non-constant stationary solutions
of (
2.1.1
) correspond to closed level curves whose period is of
the form
L/k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
(as they can be tracked multiple times).
Expressing
p=q^{\prime}
in terms of
H
and
q
and integrating, one obtains that the
period of the closed level curve
\{H(q,p)=E\}
is given by

T(E)=2\intop\nolimits_{q_{-}(E)}^{q_{+}(E)}\frac{\dd\!q}{\sqrt{2E-q^{2}+\frac{1}{2}q^{4}}}\;,\qquad q_{\pm}(E)=\pm\sqrt{1-\sqrt{1-4E}}\;,

(2.1.16)

which is a Jacobi elliptic integral that is known to be an increasing function
of
E
, starting at
2\pi
for
E=0
.

It follows that whenever
L
is a multiple of
2\pi
, a new family of
non-constant stationary solutions bifurcates from
\phi^{*}_{0}
. This is a family
of solutions because one can choose the origin
x=0
anywhere on the closed
level curve. The fact that these solutions are unstable has topological reasons
(conservation of the degree of stationary points of functions depending on a
parameter), and can be checked locally by a bifurcation analysis.
∎

In what follows, we will again mostly concentrate for simplicity on the case

0<L<2\pi
, when
V
is a double-well potential with two local minima located
at
\phi^{*}_{\pm}
and a saddle at the origin. However, the results hold in more
generality.

Exercise 2.1.2
.

Discuss the case of zero-flux Neumann boundary conditions

\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x}(0)=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x}(L)=0\;.

(2.1.17)

This setting corresponds to the orbit shown in red in
Figure

2.2
.
Derive the bifurcation diagram representing the stationary solutions and their
stability as a function of
L
.

2.2
Space-time white noise

We now turn to the precise definition of the space-time white noise process

\xi
formally introduced in (
2.0.5
). It should have the following
properties:

•

each
\xi(t,x)
should be a Gaussian random variable;

•

each
\xi(t,x)
should be centred;

•

the values of
\xi
at different space-time points should be
independent, and thus uncorrelated, which is sometimes written informally as

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\xi(t,x)\xi(s,y)\bigr]=\delta(t-s)\delta(x-y)\;.

(2.2.1)

It turns out that there is no random function having the above properties, but
that there exists a random Schwartz distribution doing the job, i.e., one can
consider
\xi
as a random linear functional acting on test functions.
We will denote by
{\mathscr{H}}
be the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda)
, where we
recall that
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}/(L\mathbb{Z})
. Let
{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}({\mathscr{H}})
be the space of Schwartz
distributions, and denote by
\langle\zeta,\varphi\rangle
the duality pairing between a
distribution
\zeta\in{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}({\mathscr{H}})
and a test function
\varphi\in{\mathscr{H}}
.

Definition 2.2.1

(Space-time white noise)
.

Space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda
is a random distribution
\xi
on a
probability space
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
such that for any smooth test function

\varphi\in{\mathscr{H}}
,
\langle\xi,\varphi\rangle
is a centred Gaussian random variable of
variance
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{{\mathscr{H}}}^{2}
, and the covariances are given by

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\xi,\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr]=\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{{\mathscr{H}}}

(2.2.2)

for any two smooth test functions
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in{\mathscr{H}}
.

The fact that such a process
\xi
exists is a consequence of general results
on Gaussian measure theory. Details can be found, for instance, in Chapter 3 of
Martin Hairer’s lecture notes
[
Hai09
]
.

Note that we recover the expression (
2.2.1
) by formally
replacing the test functions
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
by Dirac distributions. Let us
consider a couple more examples.

Example 2.2.2
.

Let
A_{1},A_{2}\subset\Lambda
be open sets, fix
T_{1},T_{2}>0
and
take

\varphi_{i}(t,x)=\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\mathbin{\leqslant}t\mathbin{\leqslant}T_{i}\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x\in A_{i}\}}\;,\qquad i=1,2\;.

(2.2.3)

These test functions are not smooth, but can be obtained as limits of smooth
functions. Define

W^{A_{i}}_{T_{i}}=\langle\xi,\varphi_{i}\rangle=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T_{i}}\intop\nolimits_{A_{i}}\xi(t,x)\dd\!x\dd\!t\qquad i=1,2

(2.2.4)

(where the last integral is of course formal).
Then (
2.2.2
) takes the form

\mathbb{E}\bigl[W^{A_{1}}_{T_{1}}W^{A_{2}}_{T_{2}}\bigr]=(T_{1}\wedge T_{2})\lvert A_{1}\cap A_{2}\rvert\;,

(2.2.5)

where
\lvert\cdot\rvert
denotes the Lebesgue measure of a set. Thus the

W^{A_{i}}_{T_{i}}
behave like Brownian motions, which are independent if the sets

A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are disjoint.

Example 2.2.3
.

Let
\{e_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
be an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Lambda)
, for
instance a Fourier basis, fix
T_{1},T_{2}>0
and
take

\varphi_{i}(t,x)=\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\mathbin{\leqslant}t\mathbin{\leqslant}T_{i}\}}e_{k_{i}}(x)\;,\qquad i=1,2\;.

(2.2.6)

Let

W^{(k_{i})}_{T_{i}}=\langle\xi,\varphi_{i}\rangle=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T_{i}}\langle\xi(t,\cdot),e_{k_{i}}\rangle\dd\!t\qquad i=1,2\;.

(2.2.7)

Then (
2.2.2
) yields

\mathbb{E}\bigl[W^{(k_{1})}_{T_{1}}W^{(k_{2})}_{T_{2}}\bigr]=(T_{1}\wedge T_{2})\delta_{k_{1}k_{2}}\;,

(2.2.8)

showing that the
W^{(k_{i})}_{T}
are independent Brownian motions.

An important property of space-time white noise is related to scaling,
in a way that should be reminiscent of Brownian motion. Given
\tau,\lambda>0
,
define a scaling operator acting on test functions by

({\mathscr{S}}^{\tau,\lambda}\varphi)(t,x):=\frac{1}{\tau\lambda}\varphi\biggl(\frac{t}{\tau},\frac{x}{\lambda}\biggr)\;.

(2.2.9)

Then we define a scaled version
\xi_{\tau,\lambda}
of space-time white noise
by

\langle\xi_{\tau,\lambda},\varphi\rangle=\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{\tau,\lambda}\varphi\rangle

(2.2.10)

for any test function
\varphi
.

Exercise 2.2.4
.

Show that if
\xi
is replaced by a smooth function
f

in (
2.2.10
), then one simply has
f_{\tau,\lambda}(t,x)=f(\tau t,\lambda x)
.

Proposition 2.2.5

(Scaling property of space-time white noise)
.

We have

\xi_{\tau,\lambda}\eqinlaw\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau\lambda}}\xi\;,

(2.2.11)

where
\eqinlaw
denotes equality in distribution.

Proof:
.

Both processes are Gaussian and centred. Therefore, it suffices to show that
they have the same covariance. Given two compactly supported test functions

\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
, we have
by (
2.2.2
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi_{\tau,\lambda},\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\xi_{\tau,\lambda},\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{\tau,\lambda}\varphi_{1}\rangle{\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{\tau,\lambda}\varphi_{2}\rangle}\bigr]

(2.2.12)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}({\mathscr{S}}^{\tau,\lambda}\varphi_{1})(t,x)({\mathscr{S}}^{\tau,\lambda}\varphi_{2})(t,x)\dd\!x\dd\!t

(2.2.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau\lambda}\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{\mathscr{H}}\;.

(2.2.14)

This is indeed the covariance of
\xi/\sqrt{\lambda\tau}
.
∎

In what follows, it will be important to find some functions spaces to which
the relevant objects belong. A first important family of such spaces
are scaled versions of the classical Hölder spaces. The scaling comes from
the fact that it turns out to be useful to work with the
parabolic
distance

\lVert(t,x)-(s,y)\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\lvert t-s\rvert^{1/2}+\lvert x-y\rvert

(2.2.15)

between space-time points, owing to the parabolic nature of the
SPDE (
2.0.5
), which is first-order in time and second-order in space.
This is not a genuine distance because it fails to satisfy the triangle
inequality, but this will not be important.

Definition 2.2.6

(Parabolic Hölder space)
.

For
0<\alpha<1
, the parabolic Hölder space

{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda)
consists of all continuous functions

f:\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
such that for every compact
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda

\lVert f\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathfrak{K}})}:=\sup_{z\in{\mathfrak{K}}}\bigl|f(z)\bigr|+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathfrak{K}}\\
z_{1}\neq z_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{\lvert f(z_{1})-f(z_{2})\rvert}{\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha}}<\infty\;.

(2.2.16)

There exists an extension of these spaces to negative index
\alpha
, which are
a scaled version of a particular class of Besov spaces, commonly denoted

{\mathscr{B}}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}
. Since these are closely related to the above
Hölder spaces, as we will see in the next section, we keep the notation

{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
as in
[
Hai14
]
. To define these spaces, we
slightly adapt the notation (
2.2.9
) to allow for zooming in
at any space-time point, setting

({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{t,x}\varphi)(s,y):=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\varphi\biggl(\frac{s-t}{\lambda^{2}},\frac{y-x}{\lambda}\biggr)\;.

(2.2.17)

Given a positive integer
r
, we further let
B_{r}
be the set of smooth test
functions
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
supported in the unit

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}
-ball, such that

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{r}}:=\sup_{k\colon\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbin{\leqslant}r}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda}\bigl|\DD^{k}\varphi(z)\bigr|\mathbin{\leqslant}1\;.

(2.2.18)

Here
k=(k_{0},k_{1})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
is a multiindex,
\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}:=2k_{0}+k_{1}
is its scaled norm, and
\DD^{k}:=\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x}^{k_{1}}
.

Definition 2.2.7

(Parabolic negative index Besov–Hölder space)
.

For
\alpha<0
, the space
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda)
consists of
all
Schwartz distributions
\zeta\in{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda)
such that for every
compact
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda

\lVert\zeta\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathfrak{K}})}:=\sup_{z\in{\mathfrak{K}}}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\biggl|\frac{\langle\zeta,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle}{\lambda^{\alpha}}\biggr|<\infty\;,

(2.2.19)

where
r=\lceil-\alpha\rceil
.

In the case where
\zeta=\xi
is space-time white noise, it follows
from (
2.2.14
) that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle^{2}\bigr]=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}\;.

(2.2.20)

Since for Gaussian random variables, second moments determine all other
moments, this suggests that
\xi
might belong to
{\mathscr{C}}^{-3/2}_{\mathfrak{s}}
. The
following important result shows that this is almost the case.

Theorem 2.2.8

(Regularity of space-time white noise)
.

We have
\xi\in{\mathscr{C}}^{-3/2-\kappa}_{\mathfrak{s}}
for any
\kappa>0
.

Sketch of proof:
.

We follow the argument outlined in
[
CW17
, Theorem 2.7]
.
First recall the fact that if
X
is a Gaussian random variable, then there
exists, for every integer
p>0
, a finite constant
C_{p}
such that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lvert X\rvert^{p}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}C_{p}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[X^{2}\bigr]\Bigr)^{p/2}

(2.2.21)

(see also
3.2.4
).
It thus follows from (
2.2.20
) that for every integer
p>0
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl|\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle\bigr|^{p}\Bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}C_{p}\lambda^{-3p/2}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}^{p}\;.

(2.2.22)

Fix a compact
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda
, and let
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
be the set of
points in
\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda
at parabolic distance at most
1
from
{\mathfrak{K}}

(called the
1
-fattening
of
{\mathfrak{K}}
). Given
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we introduce a
dyadic lattice discretisation of
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
on scale
2^{-k}
, given by

{\mathfrak{K}}_{k}=\bigl(2^{-2k}\mathbb{Z}\times 2^{-k}\mathbb{Z}\bigr)\cap\bar{\mathfrak{K}}\;.

(2.2.23)

The crucial observation is that one can show that for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, there
exists a test function
\varphi
and a constant
C
such that

\lVert\xi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathfrak{K}})}\mathbin{\leqslant}C\sup_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\sup_{z\in{\mathfrak{K}}_{k}}2^{k\alpha}\bigl|\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{2^{-k}}_{z}\varphi\rangle\bigr|\;.

(2.2.24)

The fact that
\varphi
can be chosen independent of
k
is not trivial, but there
are various ways of constructing such a
\varphi
(using for instance wavelets, or
Paley–Littlewood blocks). Bounding the suprema by sums and taking the
p
th
power, we arrive at

\Bigl(\lVert\xi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathfrak{K}})}\Bigr)^{p}\mathbin{\leqslant}C^{p}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\sumop\displaylimits_{z\in{\mathfrak{K}}_{k}}2^{k\alpha p}\bigl|\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{2^{-k}}_{z}\varphi\rangle\bigr|^{p}\;.

(2.2.25)

Note that so far, the argument is purely deterministic. At this point, we take
expectations and use (
2.2.22
) to arrive at

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl(\lVert\xi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathfrak{K}})}\Bigr)^{p}\Bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}\overline{C}_{p}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}2^{3k}2^{k\alpha p}2^{3kp/2}

(2.2.26)

for some constant
\overline{C}_{p}
, where we have used the fact that
{\mathfrak{K}}_{k}

contains of the order of
2^{3k}
points. If
\alpha<\smash{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{3}{p}}
, one can sum the geometric series, yielding the bound

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl(\lVert\xi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathfrak{K}})}\Bigr)^{p}\Bigr]<\infty\qquad\forall\alpha<-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{3}{p}\;.

(2.2.27)

It then follows from a version of Kolmogorov’s continuity theorem that there
exists a modification of
\xi
with bounded
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathfrak{K}})
-norm.
Taking
p
large enough yields the result.
∎

2.3
The stochastic heat equation

Before turning to the Allen–Cahn SPDE (
2.0.5
), we consider the
linear
stochastic heat equation
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Lambda
, given by

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\xi(t,x)\;.

(2.3.1)

Here we have taken
2\varepsilon=1
, since the case of general
\varepsilon
can easily be
recovered by scaling.

First recall that the
heat equation

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)\;,\qquad\phi(0,x)=\phi_{0}(x)

(2.3.2)

admits the solution
\phi(t,x)=(\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0})(x)
, where
\e^{t\Delta}

is the
heat semigroup
defined by

(\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0})(x):=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P(t,x-y)\phi_{0}(y)\dd\!y\;.

(2.3.3)

Here
P(t,x)
is the
heat kernel
. If
\Lambda
were equal to
\mathbb{R}
, it
would be given by

P_{\,\mathbb{R}}(t,x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi t}}\e^{-x^{2}/(4t)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>0\}}\;.

(2.3.4)

Since we are working in
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}/(L\mathbb{Z})
, however, we have to use a
periodicised version of the heat kernel, defined by

P(t,x)=P_{\Lambda}(t,x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}P_{\,\mathbb{R}}(t,x-kL)\;.

(2.3.5)

Next recall that the
forced heat equation

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+f(t,x)\;,\qquad\phi(0,x)=\phi_{0}(x)\;,

(2.3.6)

where
f
is some smooth forcing, can be solved by the method of variation of
constant, also known as
Duhamel principle
in the theory of PDEs. The
result is

\displaystyle\phi(t,x)

\displaystyle=(\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0})(x)+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\bigl(\e^{(t-s)\Delta}f\bigr)(s,x)\dd\!s

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle=(\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0})(x)+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P(t-s,x-y)f(s,y)\dd\!y\dd\!s\;.

(2.3.8)

Note that the second term on the right-hand side is the space-time convolution

(P*f)(t,x)
. Therefore, we will sometimes write (
2.3.8
) in the
short form

\phi=P\phi_{0}+P*f\;,

(2.3.9)

keeping in mind that the star denotes space-time convolution, while no star
stands for convolution in space only.

It is thus natural to define the solution of the stochastic heat
equation (
2.3.1
) by

\phi=P\phi_{0}+P*\xi\;,

(2.3.10)

where
(P*\xi)(t,x)
is called the
stochastic convolution
. It seems
reasonable to expect that this definition makes sense, since the stochastic
convolution looks similar to pairing
\xi
with a test function. One should be
careful, however, with the fact that
P
has a singularity at the origin, and
is thus not strictly speaking a test function.

We will discuss two equivalent ways of analysing the stochastic convolution.
The first one is based on Fourier series and fractional Sobolev spaces, while
the second one uses Besov– Hölder spaces and the so-called Schauder estimate.

2.3.1
Fourier series and fractional Sobolev spaces

Define an orthonormal basis
(e_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
L^{2}(\Lambda)
by

e_{k}(x):=\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{L}}\cos\biggl(\dfrac{2k\pi x}{L}\biggr)\quad\text{if $k>0$\;,}\qquad e_{0}(x):=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\;,\qquad e_{k}(x):=\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{L}}\sin\biggl(\dfrac{2k\pi x}{L}\biggr)\quad\text{if $k<0$\;.}

(2.3.11)

These basis functions satisfy the eigenvalue problem

\Delta e_{k}=-\lambda_{k}e_{k}\;,\qquad\lambda_{k}:=\biggl(\frac{2k\pi}{L}\biggr)^{2}\;.

(2.3.12)

We will write the expansion of
\phi\in L^{2}(\Lambda)
in the Fourier
basis (
2.3.11
)

\phi(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{\phi}_{k}e_{k}(x)\;.

(2.3.13)

Remark 2.3.1
.

It might seem more convenient to use a complex Fourier basis of the form

e_{k}(x)=\e^{2\icx k\pi x/L}/\sqrt{L}
. While this simplifies certain
computations involving nonlinear terms, it has the drawback that the modes
k

and
-k
become correlated. This is not really a problem, but makes it simpler
to work with real Fourier series, at least in the case of linear equations.

Fourier series are intimately related to the scale of
fractional Sobolev
spaces
(also called
Bessel potential spaces
).

Definition 2.3.2

(Fractional Sobolev spaces)
.

For
s\mathbin{\geqslant}0
, the fractional Sobolev space
H^{s}(\Lambda)
is given by the
subspace of functions
\phi\in L^{2}(\Lambda)
such that

\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+k^{2})^{s}\hat{\phi}_{k}^{2}<\infty\;.

(2.3.14)

In particular,
H^{0}(\Lambda)=L^{2}(\Lambda)
. For
s<0
,
H^{s}(\Lambda)
is
the closure of
L^{2}(\Lambda)
under the norm (
2.3.14
).

A first use of fractional Sobolev spaces is that they allow to quantify the
regularising effect of the heat semigroup.

Lemma 2.3.3
.

For any
s\mathbin{\geqslant}0
, there exists a constant
C(s)<\infty
such that

\lVert\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0}\rVert_{H^{s}}\mathbin{\leqslant}\bigl(1+C(s)t^{-s/2}\bigr)\lVert\phi_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}

(2.3.15)

holds for all
\phi_{0}\in L^{2}(\Lambda)
.

Exercise 2.3.4
.

Prove
Lemma

2.3.3
, using the Fourier representation of

\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0}
and the fact that the map
x\mapsto x^{s}\e^{-x}
is
bounded uniformly in
x>0
by a constant depending on
s
.

Projecting the stochastic heat equation (
2.3.1
) on the basis
function
e_{k}
, and using the observation on
\langle\xi,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\mathbin{\leqslant}t\mathbin{\leqslant}T\}}e_{k}\rangle
made in
Example

2.2.3
, we obtain that each Fourier
mode
evolves according to the SDE

\dd\!\hat{\phi}_{k}=-\lambda_{k}\hat{\phi}_{k}\dd\!t+\dd\!W^{(k)}_{t}\;,

(2.3.16)

where the
(W^{(k)}_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
are independent Wiener processes. The
solution is given by

\hat{\phi}_{k}(t)=\e^{-\lambda_{k}t}\hat{\phi}_{k}(0)+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{-\lambda_{k}(t-s)}\dd\!W^{(k)}_{s}\;.

(2.3.17)

Hence
\hat{\phi}_{0}(t)
is a Brownian motion, while all other
\hat{\phi}_{k}(t)
are
Orstein–Uhlenbeck processes. The stochastic convolution can thus be defined as

(P*\xi)(t,x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{-\lambda_{k}(t-s)}\dd\!W^{(k)}_{s}e_{k}(x)\;,

(2.3.18)

which together with the initial-condition term
\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0}
defines the
so-called
mild solution
of the stochastic heat equation. Using Itô’s
isometry, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\lVert(P*\xi)(t,\cdot)\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}\Bigr]=t+\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}(1+k^{2})^{s}\frac{1-\e^{-2\lambda_{k}t}}{2\lambda_{k}}\;,

(2.3.19)

which is finite for finite
t
for all
s<1/2
. In fact, we have the following
result, which is a special case of
[
Hai09
, Theorem 5.13]
.

Theorem 2.3.5

(Sobolev regularity of the stochastic convolution)
.

The stochastic convolution
(P*\xi)(t,\cdot)
belongs to
H^{s}(\Lambda)
for
every
s<\frac{1}{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Exercise 2.3.6
.

Compute the covariance
\mathbb{E}\bigl[(P*\xi)(t,x)(P*\xi)(s,y)\bigr]
of the stochastic
convolution (
2.3.18
).

Hint:
It may help to distinguish the cases
t>s
,
t=s
and
t<s
.

2.3.2
Besov–Hölder spaces and Schauder estimate

It is also possible to quantify the Hölder regularity of the stochastic
convolution. One way of doing this is to use

Theorem

2.2.8
on the regularity of space-time white
noise in conjunction with a regularising estimate for the heat semigroup,
called
Schauder estimate
. To state this result in full generality, we
extend the definition of parabolic Hölder spaces given in

Definition

2.2.6
to exponents
\alpha>1
in the following way.

Definition 2.3.7

(Parabolic Hölder spaces of positive index)
.

Let
\alpha\mathbin{\geqslant}0
. The space
