{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda)
consists of
all
f:\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
such that there exist polynomials

\{P_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda}
of parabolic degree less than
\alpha
, such
that for every compact
{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda

\lVert f\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathfrak{K}})}:=\sup_{z\in{\mathfrak{K}}}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{0}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\biggl|\frac{\langle f-P_{z},{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle}{\lambda^{\alpha}}\biggr|<\infty\;,

(2.3.20)

where
B_{0}
is defined right before (
2.2.18
).

At first sight, this definition may seem redundant with
Definition

2.2.6

when
0<\alpha<1
, but it is in fact equivalent. To see this, consider the case

z=(0,0)
, and take
P_{0}(t,x)=f(0,0)
. Then

\displaystyle\langle f-P_{0},{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\bigl[f(t,x)-f(0,0)\bigr]\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\varphi\biggl(\frac{t}{\lambda^{2}},\frac{x}{\lambda}\biggr)\dd\!x\dd\!t

(2.3.21)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\bigl[f(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x)-f(0,0)\bigr]\varphi(t,x)\dd\!x\dd\!t\;,

(2.3.22)

which has indeed order
\lambda^{\alpha}
if
f
is
\alpha
-Hölder in the
parabolic norm. For
\alpha>1
, one can easily check that
P_{z}
must be
the Taylor expansion of
f
at
z
to parabolic order
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
.

Exercise 2.3.8
.

Prove that if
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
is a multiindex, then

\DD^{k}f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha-\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}}_{\mathfrak{s}}
, where derivatives are
understood in the sense of distributions if
\alpha<\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Hint:
Show that

\langle\DD^{k}(f-P_{z}),{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle=-\frac{1}{\lambda^{\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}}}\langle f-P_{z},{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\DD^{k}\varphi\rangle

(2.3.23)

using integration by parts.

The main result quantifying the regularising properties of the heat semigroup
is the following Schauder estimate.

Theorem 2.3.9

(Schauder estimate)
.

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
and
t>0
, there exists a constant
C
such
that for every
\zeta\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Lambda)
, one has

\lVert P*\zeta\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha+2}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,t]\times\Lambda)}\mathbin{\leqslant}C\lVert\zeta\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,t]\times\Lambda)}\;.

(2.3.24)

Therefore
P*\zeta\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha+2}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Lambda)
.
The heat semigroup is said to be
regularising of
parabolic order
2
.

Remark 2.3.10
.

This result is in general
not
true if
\alpha
is an integer. In fact,
our definition of the spaces
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
implies a kind of
discontinuity at integer values of
\alpha
. For instance,
\alpha
-Hölder
functions approach continuously differentiable functions as
\alpha
decreases
to
1
from above, and Lipschitz functions as
\alpha
increase to
1
from
below. This will, however, not cause any problems, since we will always be able
to slightly decrease
\alpha
.

Sketch of proof:
.

The proof we sketch here is not the only possible one, and perhaps not the
shortest, but it will prepare us for the theory of regularity structures.
The main idea is to decompose
P
as a sum

P(z)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}P_{n}(z)+R(z)\;,

(2.3.25)

where
R
is smooth and bounded, and each
P_{n}
is supported in a ball

\{z\colon\lVert z\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbin{\leqslant}2^{-n}\}
, and satisfies the bound

\bigl|\DD^{k}P_{n}(z)\bigr|\mathbin{\leqslant}C2^{(1+\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}})n}

(2.3.26)

for every multiindex
k
. Such a decomposition is possible as shown
in
[
Hai14
, Lemma 5.5]
. We then have

(P*\zeta)(z)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\langle\zeta,P_{n}(z-\cdot)\rangle+\langle\zeta,R(z-\cdot)\rangle\;.

(2.3.27)

The term involving
R
is smooth, so we only need to concern ourselves with
the sum over
n
.

Consider first the case
\alpha<-2
. The aim is to show that for a test
function
\varphi
,

\biggl|\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\intop\nolimits_{[0,t]\times\Lambda}\langle\zeta,P_{n}(\bar{z}-\cdot)\rangle({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi)(\bar{z})\dd\!\bar{z}\biggr|\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha+2}\;.

(2.3.28)

The proof proceeds differently depending on the relative value of
2^{-n}
and

\lambda
. If
2^{-n}>\lambda
, it follows from the fact that

\zeta\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
and the properties of
P_{n}
that

\bigl|\langle\zeta,P_{n}(\bar{z}-\cdot)\rangle\bigr|\lesssim 2^{-(\alpha+2)n}\;.

(2.3.29)

Indeed,
P_{n}
can be bounded above by a scaled test function multiplied by

2^{-2n}
, cf.
[
Hai14
, Remark 2.21]
. Since
\alpha+2<0
, the sum
over all
n
such that
2^{-n}>\lambda
is dominated by the largest
n
, and
satisfies (
2.3.28
) as required.

If
2^{-n}\mathbin{\leqslant}\lambda
, we write the quantity to be bounded as

\langle\zeta,Y^{\lambda}_{n}\rangle
, where, for fixed
z
,

Y^{\lambda}_{n}(z^{\prime}):=\intop\nolimits_{[0,t]\times\Lambda}P_{n}(\bar{z}-z^{\prime})({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi)(\bar{z})\dd\!\bar{z}\;.

(2.3.30)

Here we note that
Y^{\lambda}_{n}
is supported in a ball of parabolic radius

2\lambda
around
z
(see
Figure

2.3
). Bounding
{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi

by
\lambda^{-3}
and using the properties of
P_{n}
, we obtain

\bigl|Y^{\lambda}_{n}(z^{\prime})\bigr|\lesssim 2^{-2n}\lambda^{-3}\;.

(2.3.31)

A similar bound can be obtained for derivatives of
Y^{\lambda}_{n}

(cf.
[
Hai14
, Lemma 5.19]
), and using once again the fact that

\zeta\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
, we arrive at

\bigl|\langle\zeta,Y^{\lambda}_{n}\rangle\bigr|\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha}2^{-2n}\;.

(2.3.32)

Summing over all
n
such that
2^{-n}\mathbin{\leqslant}\lambda
yields again a bound of the
form (
2.3.28
).

z
z^{\prime}
\lambda
2^{-n}
{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi
P_{n}

Figure 2.3:
Domain of the integral (
2.3.30
) defining

Y^{\lambda}_{n}(z^{\prime})
. The functions
{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}
and
P_{n}(\bar{z}-\cdot)
are
supported, respectively, in a ball of (parabolic) radius
\lambda
centred in

z
, and in a ball of radius
2^{-n}
centred in
z^{\prime}
. Therefore,

Y^{\lambda}_{n}(z^{\prime})
vanishes if
\lVert z^{\prime}-z\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}>\lambda+2^{-n}
. The
support of the integrand lies in the intersection of the balls, and is thus
contained in a ball of radius
2^{-n}
.

Consider next the case
-2<\alpha<-1
. Then, instead of the
bound (
2.3.28
), we have to show that

\biggl|\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\intop\nolimits_{[0,t]\times\Lambda}\langle\zeta,P_{n}(\bar{z}-\cdot)-P_{n}(z-\cdot)\rangle({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi)(\bar{z})\dd\!\bar{z}\biggr|\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha+2}\;.

(2.3.33)

The difference is that we now subtract the term
P_{n}(z-\cdot)
, which accounts
for the polynomial
P_{z}
in (
2.3.20
). If
2^{-n}>\lambda
, using
a Taylor expansion, one obtains instead
of (
2.3.29
)

\bigl|\langle\zeta,P_{n}(\bar{z}-\cdot)-P_{n}(z-\cdot)\rangle\bigr|\lesssim\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\{(1,0),(0,1)\}}\lVert z-\bar{z}\rVert^{\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}}_{\mathfrak{s}}2^{(\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}-\alpha-2)n}\;.

(2.3.34)

Combining this with the bound

\intop\nolimits_{[0,t]\times\Lambda}\lVert z-\bar{z}\rVert^{\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}}_{\mathfrak{s}}({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi)(\bar{z})\dd\!\bar{z}\lesssim\lambda^{\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}}

(2.3.35)

and summing over
n
yields the result.
For
2^{-n}\mathbin{\leqslant}\lambda
, the term
Y^{\lambda}_{n}(z^{\prime})
has to be replaced by

Y^{\lambda}_{n}(z^{\prime})-P_{n}(z-z^{\prime})

(2.3.36)

since the scaled test function integrates to
1
. When tested against
\zeta
,
the new term
P_{n}(z-\cdot)
produces an extra term
2^{-(\alpha+2)n}
, whose
sum over
n
again satisfies the required bound.

For large values of
\alpha
, the proof is similar, except that one has to
extract more terms in the Taylor expansion of the
P_{n}
.
∎

We now directly obtain the nontrivial fact that the stochastic convolution is
Hölder continuous of exponent almost
1/2
. This will become very useful when
solving nonlinear equations with a fixed-point argument.

Corollary 2.3.11

(Hölder regularity of the stochastic convolution)
.

The stochastic convolution
P*\xi
belongs to

{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Lambda)
for all
\alpha<1/2
.

Proof:
.

This follows immediately by combining
Theorem

2.2.8
and
the Schauder estimate of
Theorem

2.3.9
.
∎

2.4
Existence and uniqueness of solutions

We turn now to semilinear SPDEs of the form

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+F(\phi(t,x))+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)

(2.4.1)

with initial condition
\phi_{0}(x)
and locally Lipschitz nonlinearity
F
. For
instance, in the case of the Allen–Cahn SPDE (
2.0.5
),

F(\phi)=\phi-\phi^{3}
. By analogy with the Duhamel principle
yielding (
2.3.10
) in the case of the stochastic heat
equation,
one way of defining a notion of solution to (
2.4.1
) is as a fixed
point of

\phi=P\phi_{0}+P*F(\phi)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}P*\xi\;.

(2.4.2)

A more explicit notation of this relation, analogous to (
2.3.8
), is

\phi(t)=\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0}+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{(t-s)\Delta}F(\phi(s))\dd\!s+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{(t-s)\Delta}\dd\!\xi(s)\;.

(2.4.3)

Definition 2.4.1

(Mild solution)
.

•

A stochastic process
t\mapsto\phi(t)
with values in a Banach space

{\mathscr{B}}
satisfying (
2.4.3
) almost surely for every
t>0
is
called a
mild solution
of the SPDE (
2.4.1
).

•

A
local mild solution
of (
2.4.1
) is a pair

(\phi,\tau)
, where
t\mapsto\phi(t)
is a stochastic process and
\tau
is a
stopping time such that (
2.4.3
) is satisfied almost
surely
for all
t\mathbin{\leqslant}\tau
.

•

A local mild solution
(\phi,\tau)
is
maximal
if for any local
mild solution
(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\tau})
, one has
\tilde{\tau}\mathbin{\leqslant}\tau
almost
surely.

Existence and uniqueness of a maximal local mild solution can be proved in a
way which is analogous to what is done for ODEs and SDEs.

Theorem 2.4.2

(Existence of a unique maximal local mild solution)
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}^{0}
be the Banach space of continuous functions
f:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
,
equipped with the supremum norm. For every
\phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}
, the semilinear
equation (
2.4.1
) admits a unique maximal local mild
solution
\phi
taking values in
{\mathscr{C}}^{0}
. Moreover, this solution has
continuous sample paths, and
satisfies
\lim_{t\nearrow\tau}\lVert\phi(t)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}=\infty
almost surely
on the set
\{\tau<\infty\}
.

Proof:
.

Given
T>0
and a continuous function
g:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to{\mathscr{C}}^{0}
, we define a map
{\mathscr{T}}

from the space of continuous functions from
[0,T]
to
{\mathscr{C}}^{0}
to itself by

({\mathscr{T}}\phi)(t):=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{(t-s)\Delta}F(\phi(s))\dd\!s+g(t)\;,\qquad t\in[0,T]\;.

(2.4.4)

Hölder’s inequality shows that
\lVert\e^{t\Delta}f\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}\mathbin{\leqslant}\lVert f\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}
for any
t\mathbin{\geqslant}0
. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert({\mathscr{T}}\phi)(t)-({\mathscr{T}}\psi)(t)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}T\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert F(\phi(t))-F(\psi(t))\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}

(2.4.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert({\mathscr{T}}\phi)(t)-g(t)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}T\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert F(\phi(t))\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}\;.

(2.4.6)

Now fix a constant
R>0
. Since
F
is locally Lipschitz, we obtain the
existence of a constant
M=M(R)>0
such that for any
\phi,\psi
in a ball of
radius
R
around
g
, one has

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert({\mathscr{T}}\phi)(t)-({\mathscr{T}}\psi)(t)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}TM\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\phi(t)-\psi(t)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}

(2.4.7)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert({\mathscr{T}}\phi)(t)-g(t)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}TM\;.

(2.4.8)

Choosing
T
small enough, one can guarantee that
{\mathscr{T}}
maps the ball of radius

R
into itself, and is a contraction of constant
\lambda<1/2
there.
Therefore, Banach’s fixed point theorem shows that
{\mathscr{T}}
admits a unique fixed
point in that ball. Choosing

g(t)=\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{(t-s)\Delta}\dd\!\xi_{s}\;,

(2.4.9)

which is in
{\mathscr{C}}^{0}
for all
t
according to
Corollary

2.3.11
,
we obtain the existence of a unique local mild solution on
[0,T]
. Note that
this is a pathwise solution, that is, for each realisation of
\xi
, we find
such a solution, where
T
may depend on the particular realisation of the
noise.

To show maximality, we argue exactly as in the deterministic case. Since
R
is
finite, we may restart the process to extend it to a slightly longer time
interval. This procedure can be iterated as long as the solution remains
bounded, proving that either
\tau=\infty
, or
\phi(t)
diverges as
t

approaches
\tau
. Finally, uniqueness and continuity can be shown in the same
way as for finite-dimensional SDEs, see for
instance
[
Øks85
, Theorem 5.2.1]
.
∎

To obtain global existence of solutions, we have again to exploit the sign of
the nonlinearity. There exist various one-sided conditions on the nonlinearity
allowing to do that. One of them is that
F
satisfy the following
property: for any
R>0
, there exists a
C>0
such that

uF(u+v)\mathbin{\leqslant}Cu^{2}\qquad\text{when $\lvert v\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}R$\;.}

(2.4.10)

One easily checks that this holds in the case
F(u)=u-u^{3}
of the Allen–Cahn
equation.

Theorem 2.4.3

(Global existence of the solution)
.

If
F
is locally Lipschitz and satisfies (
2.4.10
),
then for any
\phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}
, the solution of the semilinear stochastic
PDE (
2.4.1
) exists globally in time.

Proof:
.

We follow the proof given in
[
Hai09
, Proposition 6.23]
, using a
Lyapunov function to show that
\phi(t)
cannot blow up. Let
W_{\Delta}(t)

denote the stochastic convolution in (
2.4.3
). Then

\psi(t)=\phi(t)-\sqrt{2\varepsilon}W_{\Delta}(t)
satisfies

\psi(t)=\e^{t\Delta}\phi_{0}+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{(t-s)\Delta}G(s)\dd\!s\;,\qquad G(s)=F(\psi(s)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}W_{\Delta}(s))\;.

(2.4.11)

Continuity of sample paths and the Markov property imply

\psi(t+h)=\e^{h\Delta}\bigl[\psi(t)+hG(t)\bigr]+{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}(h)\;.

(2.4.12)

Consider now the Lyapunov function

{\mathscr{V}}(\psi):=\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\psi(x)^{2}\;.

(2.4.13)

Note that since the heat semigroup is contracting,
1
1

1

This holds actually
for any convex function
{\mathscr{V}}
, cf.
[
Hai09
, Lemma 6.22]
.

{\mathscr{V}}(\e^{h\Delta}\psi)\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{V}}(\psi)
. Thus we have

\limsup_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\bigl[{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t+h))-{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t))\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}\limsup_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\bigl[{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t)+hG(t))-{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t))\bigr]\;.

(2.4.14)

Now it follows from the one-sided condition (
2.4.10
) that as
long as
\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\lvert W_{\Delta}(t)\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}R
, one has

\displaystyle{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t)+hG(t))

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\Bigl(\psi(t,x)^{2}+2h\psi(t,x)G(t,x)+h^{2}G(t,x)^{2}\Bigr)

(2.4.15)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t))+2hC{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t))+h^{2}{\mathscr{V}}(G(t))\;.

(2.4.16)

It follows that

\limsup_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\bigl[{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t+h))-{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t))\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}2C{\mathscr{V}}(\psi(t))\;.

(2.4.17)

By a Gronwall argument,
\psi(t)
cannot blow up unless
W_{\Delta}(t)
does,
which is not the case.
∎

We note that for SPDEs with confining nonlinearities, one can obtain much
better bounds on solutions. For instance,
[
Cer99
, Proposition 3.4]

applied to
F(u)=u-u^{3}
provides the bound

\sup_{\phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}\lVert\phi(t)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{C}{\sqrt{t}}\Bigl(1+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\sup_{0\mathbin{\leqslant}s\mathbin{\leqslant}t}\lVert W_{\Delta}(s)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}\Bigr)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\lVert W_{\Delta}(t)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{0}}\;.

(2.4.18)

The remarkable point is that
\phi(t)
decreases like
1/\sqrt{t}
for small
times, whatever the size of the initial condition. This property is known as

coming down from infinity
, and is very useful to obtain continuation
results for solutions with very little prior knowledge. This phenomenon
actually already exists for very simple ODEs, as shows the following exercise.

Exercise 2.4.4
.

Show that the solution of the ODE

\dot{x}=-x^{3}\;,\qquad x(0)=x_{0}

(2.4.19)

can be bounded by a function independent of the value of
x_{0}
.

2.5
Invariant measure

2.5.1
The Gaussian free field

Consider first the case of linear SPDEs. The stochastic heat
equation (
2.3.1
) does not admit an invariant measure, since we
have seen that its zeroth Fourier mode performs a Brownian motion. This
problem, however, is easily cured by considering the equation

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)\;,

(2.5.1)

where we have reintroduced the parameter
\varepsilon
to keep track of its effect.
The modes of the Fourier series (
2.3.13
) then obey the SDEs

\dd\!\hat{\phi}_{k}(t)=-(\lambda_{k}+1)\hat{\phi}_{k}(t)\dd\!t+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dd\!W^{(k)}_{t}\;,

(2.5.2)

where
-\lambda_{k}
are the eigenvalues of the Laplacian,
cf. (
2.3.12
). Hence each
\hat{\phi}_{k}
is an
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process given by

\hat{\phi}_{k}(t)=\e^{-(\lambda_{k}+1)t}\hat{\phi}_{k}(0)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{-(\lambda_{k}+1)(t-s)}\dd\!W^{(k)}_{s}\;.

(2.5.3)

Asymptotically as
t\to\infty
, each
\hat{\phi}_{k}
follows a centred normal law
of variance
\varepsilon/(\lambda_{k}+1)
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 2.5.1

(Gaussian free field)
.

The massive Gaussian free field (GFF) with covariance

(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
on
\Lambda
is the random
function
\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}\in L^{2}(\Lambda)
with Fourier series

\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{Z_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}+1}}e_{k}(x)\;,

(2.5.4)

where the
Z_{k}\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
are i.i.d. standard normal random variables.

This definition is justified by the fact that
\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}
is centred, and thus has
covariance

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(y)\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}+1}e_{k}(x)e_{k}(y)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{k}(x)(-\Delta+1)^{-1}e_{k}(y)\;.

(2.5.5)

Note in particular that the trace of the covariance is given by

\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)^{2}\bigr]\dd\!x=\Tr\Bigl[(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\Bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}+1}<\infty\;.

(2.5.6)

The covariance operator is said to be
trace class
. This is indeed a
necessary and sufficient condition for a Gaussian invariant measure to exist,
cf.
[
DPZ96
, Theorem 6.2.1]
; see
also
[
Hai09
, Proposition 3.15]
.

Exercise 2.5.2
.

Show that for any test functions
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}:L^{2}(\Lambda)\to\mathbb{R}
, one has

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}},\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}},\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr]=\langle\varphi_{1},(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\varphi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}\;,

(2.5.7)

where we have identified elements of
L^{2}(\Lambda)
and its dual in the
canonical way.

More generally,
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}
is a Gaussian free field with covariance

\varepsilon(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
, which defines a Gaussian probability measure

\smash{\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}^{(\varepsilon)}}
on
L^{2}(\Lambda)
. This measure is invariant under
the dynamics of (
2.5.1
), meaning that

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}^{(\varepsilon)}}\Bigl[f(\phi_{t})\Bigr]=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[f(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}})\Bigr]\qquad\forall t\mathbin{\geqslant}0

(2.5.8)

for any integrable functional
f:L^{2}(\Lambda)\to\mathbb{R}
. Note that in particular,
any linear functional
\ell:L^{2}(\Lambda)\to\mathbb{R}
follows a centred normal
distribution, which is in fact a general way to characterise Gaussian measures
on infinite-dimensional spaces. See for
instance
[
Hai09
, Definition 3.2]
.

Exercise 2.5.3
.

Show that for a linear functional
\ell:L^{2}(\Omega)\to\mathbb{R}
, one has

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\e^{\icx\langle\ell,\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}\rangle}\bigr]=\exp\biggl\{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\ell,(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\ell\rangle_{L^{2}}\biggr\}\;.

(2.5.9)

What is the variance of
\langle\ell,\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}\rangle
?

2.5.2
Invariant Gibbs measures

We return now to semilinear SPDEs of the form

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+F(\phi(t,x))+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)\;,

(2.5.10)

where the nonlinearity
F
is such that
Theorem

2.4.3

applies. We denote its Markov semigroup

P_{t}(\phi_{0},A):={\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi_{0}}\bigl\{\phi_{t}\in A\bigr\}

(2.5.11)

(where
P_{t}
should not be confused with the heat kernel). More generally, for
a test function
f:{\mathscr{H}}=L^{2}(\Lambda)\to\mathbb{R}
, we write

(P_{t}f)(\phi_{0}):={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi_{0}}\bigl[f(\phi_{t})\bigr]\;.

(2.5.12)

One can show that
P_{t}
has the
Feller property
, meaning that it maps
continuous functions into continuous functions. A probability measure
\pi
on

{\mathscr{H}}
is
invariant
if

(\pi P_{t})(A):=\intop\nolimits_{{\mathscr{H}}}\pi(\dd\!\phi)P_{t}(\phi,A)=\pi(A)

(2.5.13)

for every
t\mathbin{\geqslant}0
and every measurable
A\subset{\mathscr{H}}
.

A classical way of establishing the existence of an invariant probability
measure, due to Krylov and Bogoliubov, relies on a tightness argument.

Definition 2.5.4

(Tightness)
.

A family
\{\mu_{t}\}
of probability measures on
{\mathscr{H}}
is
tight
if for
any
\delta>0
, there exists a compact set
K\subset{\mathscr{H}}
such that

\mu_{t}(K)\mathbin{\geqslant}1-\delta
for all
t
.

Indeed one can show (cf.
[
DPZ96
, Corollary 3.1.2]
)
that if the family of ergodic averages

\biggl\{\frac{1}{T}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}P_{t}(\phi_{0},\cdot)\dd\!t\colon T\mathbin{\geqslant}1\biggr\}

(2.5.14)

is tight, then there exists an invariant probability measure. In the present
situation, tightness follows from stochastic stability of
\phi_{t}
, meaning
that for any
\phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{H}}
and any
\delta>0
, there exists
R>0
such that
for all
T\mathbin{\geqslant}1
, one has

\frac{1}{T}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi_{0}}\bigl\{\lVert\phi_{t}\rVert>R\bigr\}\dd\!t\mathbin{\leqslant}\delta\;.

(2.5.15)

See
[
DPZ96
, Theorem 6.1.2]
for details.

In the case of the semilinear SPDE (
2.5.10
), we can
apply
[
DPZ96
, Theorem 6.3.3]
to obtain the following
exponential ergodicity result. The main ingredient of the proof is a suitable
growth bound on
\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi_{0}}\!\left[\phi_{t}\right]
.

Theorem 2.5.5

(Existence of a unique invariant measure and exponential
ergodicity)
.

When
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a unique invariant probability measure
\pi

for (
2.5.10
), which is strongly mixing
2
2

2

Strong mixing
means that
\langle P_{t}f,g\rangle
converges, as
t\to\infty
, to

\langle f,1\rangle\langle 1,g\rangle
, where the inner products are in

L^{2}({\mathscr{H}},\pi)
.
and such that
\nu P_{t}
converges weakly to
\pi
as

t\to\infty
for any probability measure
\nu
on
{\mathscr{H}}
. Furthermore, there
exist constants
C,\beta>0
such that

\bigl|(P_{t}f)(\phi_{0})-\langle\pi,f\rangle\bigr|\mathbin{\leqslant}C\bigl(1+\lVert\phi_{0}\rVert_{{\mathscr{H}}}\bigr)\e^{-\beta t}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm Lip$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm Lip$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm Lip$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm Lip$}}}

(2.5.16)

for all
t\mathbin{\geqslant}0
, all
\phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{H}}
, and all bounded Lipschitz continuous
test functions
f:{\mathscr{H}}\to\mathbb{R}
with Lipschitz constant
\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm Lip$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm Lip$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm Lip$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm Lip$}}}
.

In the particular case
F(\phi)=-\phi
in (
2.5.10
), the
invariant measure is given by
\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}^{(\varepsilon)}(\dd\!\phi)
, the distribution of
the scaled GFF defined in (
2.5.8
).

In the general case, the semilinear equation (
2.5.10
) can
be written in gradient form as

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\bigl[\Delta-{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}\bigr]\phi(t,x)-\nabla\widetilde{V}(\phi(t,x))+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)\;,

(2.5.17)

where

\widetilde{V}(\phi)=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\widetilde{U}(\phi(x))\dd\!x\;,\qquad\widetilde{U}^{\prime}(\phi)=\phi-F(\phi)\;.

(2.5.18)

Then it turns out that one has the following explicit expression for the
invariant measure, which is in fact a natural extension of the Gibbs
measure (
1.3.3
) we encountered in the finite-dimensional case.
Indeed, the following result is a particularisation
of
[
DPZ96
, Theorem 8.6.3]
.

Theorem 2.5.6

(Invariance and reversibility of the Gibbs measure)
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, the unique invariant measure of (
2.5.10
) is
absolutely continuous with respect to the scaled Gaussian free field, with
density
\e^{\widetilde{V}/\varepsilon}
:

\pi(\dd\!\phi)=\frac{1}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{0}}\e^{-\widetilde{V}(\phi)/\varepsilon}\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}^{(\varepsilon)}(\dd\!\phi)\;,

(2.5.19)

where
{\mathscr{Z}}_{0}
is the normalisation.
In addition,
\pi
satisfies the detailed-balance property

\intop\nolimits_{{\mathscr{H}}}f(\phi)(P_{t}g)(\phi)\pi(\dd\!\phi)=\intop\nolimits_{{\mathscr{H}}}g(\phi)(P_{t}f)(\phi)\pi(\dd\!\phi)

(2.5.20)

for all bounded test functions
f,g:{\mathscr{H}}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 2.5.7
.

As in the case of Markov chains, reversibility of a measure implies its
invariance, as can be seen by choosing
f(\phi)=1
and

g(\phi)=\mathbbm{1}_{A}(\phi)
in (
2.5.20
). Thus it is in
fact sufficient to prove that the measure defined in (
2.5.19
)
satisfies detailed balance to prove the theorem. The proof proceeds by using a
finite-dimensional spectral Galerkin approximation of (
2.5.10
) and
passing to the limit.

To see why (in the case
F(\phi)=\phi-\phi^{3}
) this is indeed the Gibbs measure
associated with the potential
V
defined in (
2.1.2
), it
suffices to notice that

V(\phi)-\widetilde{V}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\bigl[\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}+\phi(x)^{2}\bigr]\dd\!x\;,

(2.5.21)

which is exactly the quadratic form associated with the linear
case
F(\phi)=-\phi
. This suggests using the sloppy notation

\pi(\dd\!\phi)=\frac{1}{{\mathscr{Z}}}\e^{-V(\phi)/\varepsilon}\dd\!\phi\;,\qquad{\mathscr{Z}}=\intop\nolimits_{{\mathscr{H}}}\e^{-V(\phi)/\varepsilon}\dd\!\phi\;,

(2.5.22)

but one should not forget that there is no Lebesgue measure on

{\mathscr{H}}=L^{2}(\Lambda)
!

2.6
Large deviations

Large deviation estimates for the SPDE

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+F(\phi(t,x))+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)

(2.6.1)

as
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
have been obtained in a way very similar to the
finite-dimensional situation discussed in
Section

1.4

[
FJL82
,
Fre88
,
CM97
]
. One starts by showing
that scaled space-time white noise
\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)
satisfies an LDP on

[0,T]
with good rate function

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma)=\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\gamma(t,x)\biggr]^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t\;,

(2.6.2)

with the understanding that
{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma)=+\infty
if the integral on
the
right-hand side does not converge. Then one uses a contraction principle to
extend the LDP to the general case.

Theorem 2.6.1

(Large-deviation principle for SPDEs on the one-dimensional
torus)
.

The semilinear SPDE (
2.6.1
) satisfies an LDP on
[0,T]
with good
rate function

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\gamma(t,x)-\Delta\gamma(t,x)-F(\gamma(t,x))\biggr]^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t&\text{if the integral is finite\;, }\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(2.6.3)

From this result, one can for instance deduce information on the exit time and
location from a domain in
{\mathscr{H}}
in exactly the same way as in the
finite-dimensional case.

Exercise 2.6.2
.

Use a similar argument as in (
1.4.8
) to show that the lower
bound

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\phi)\mathbin{\geqslant}2\bigl[V(\phi_{T})-V(\phi_{0})\bigr]

(2.6.4)

holds for any given
T
, where
V
is the potential
given by (
2.1.2
) in the case
F(\phi)=\phi-\phi^{3}
.

2.7
Metastability

We examine now the dynamics of the stochastic Allen–Cahn equation

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)

(2.7.1)

for small positive
\varepsilon
. For simplicity, we concentrate on the case

L<2\pi
, when the associated potential
V
is a double-well potential,
cf.
Proposition

2.1.1
. As in the finite-dimensional case, the
estimate (
2.5.16
) shows that any initial measure will
converge exponentially fast to the invariant measure
\pi
, but it provides no
control on the dependence of the constants
C
and
\beta
on
\varepsilon
.

The large-deviation approach provides similar results as those in

Theorem

1.5.1
, including an Arrhenius law for the first-hitting time

\tau_{+}
of a neighbourhood of
\phi^{*}_{+}
of the form

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\log{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi^{*}_{-}}\bigl[\tau_{+}\bigr]=H\;,

(2.7.2)

where for
L<2\pi
, one has
H=V(\phi^{*}_{0})-V(\phi^{*}_{-})=L/4
.

Remark 2.7.1
.

For
L>2\pi
,
\phi^{*}_{0}
is no longer a saddle with one unstable direction, and
the role of the transition state is played by the family

\{\phi^{*}_{1,\theta}\}
of non-constant stationary solutions that bifurcate
from
\phi^{*}_{0}
at
L=2\pi
. The value of
H
can be expressed in terms of
elliptic integrals, cf.
[
MS01
,
MS03
]
.

The next question is thus whether an analogue of the Eyring–Kramers
formula (
1.5.58
) holds. We first have to check
whether the ratio of determinants of Hessians in that formula makes any
sense in the infinite-dimensional case. Recall that we have shown in

Proposition

2.1.1
that the Hessians are given by
-\Delta-1
and

-\Delta+2
. Though each of them has an infinite determinant, it turns out that
their ratio converges. To see this, let us write this ratio as

\det\Bigl((-\Delta-1)(-\Delta+2)^{-1}\Bigr)=\det\Bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}-3(-\Delta+2)^{-1}\Bigr)\;.

(2.7.3)

This is a so-called
Fredholm determinant
. To see that it indeed
converges, we note that it is equal to
-\smash{\frac{1}{2}}\det\bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}-3(-\Delta_{\perp}+2)^{-1}\bigr)
where
\Delta_{\perp}
acts on mean-zero functions, and
write

\displaystyle\log\det\Bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}-3(-\Delta_{\perp}+2)^{-1}\Bigr)=\Tr\log\Bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}-3(-\Delta_{\perp}+2)^{-1}\Bigr)=-\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}1}\frac{3^{n}}{n}\Tr\Bigl((-\Delta_{\perp}+2)^{-n}\Bigr)\;.

(2.7.4)

The series converges because
(-\Delta_{\perp}+2)^{-1}
is trace class (and
therefore all its powers as well), and its eigenvalues decrease like

((2k\pi/L)^{2}+2)^{-1}
.

Remark 2.7.2
.

The ratio (
2.7.3
) can also be computed explicitly, writing it as

\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\nu_{k}}{\mu_{k}}=\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\Bigl(\frac{2k\pi}{L}\Bigr)^{2}+2}{\Bigl(\frac{2k\pi}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-1}=-2\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1+2\Bigl(\frac{L}{2k\pi}\Bigr)^{2}}{1-\Bigl(\frac{L}{2k\pi}\Bigr)^{2}}\right)^{2}=-\frac{\sinh^{2}(L/\sqrt{2})}{\sin^{2}(L/2)}\;,

(2.7.5)

where we used two of Euler’s infinite product identities in the last step.
However, the interpretation in terms of Fredholm determinant will be more
useful in higher dimensions.

Exercise 2.7.3
.

By working in Fourier space, show that

\det\Bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}+c(-\Delta+a)^{-1}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{\det\Bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}-c(-\Delta+b)^{-1}\Bigr)}

(2.7.6)

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
such that
a+c=b
and the Fredholm determinants make
sense.

In order to prove an Eyring–Kramers law, we will approximate the
infinite-dimensional system by a finite-dimensional one and pass to the limit.
One way of doing this is to take the limit
N\to\infty
the lattice
system (
1.0.1
). While this is possible, we prefer the slightly easier
approach of using a spectral Galerkin approximation, which has the advantage
that the relevant eigenvalues are independent of
N
. For

\phi\in L^{2}(\Lambda)
, we define its Galerkin projection as

(\Pi_{N}\phi)(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N\end{subarray}}\langle\phi,e_{k}\rangle e_{k}(x)\;,

(2.7.7)

where the
e_{k}
denote the Fourier basis functions (
2.3.11
).
We then consider the projected equation

\partial_{t}\phi_{N}(t,x)=\Delta\phi_{N}(t,x)+\phi_{N}(t,x)-\Pi_{N}\phi_{N}(t,x)^{3}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\bigl(\Pi_{N}\xi\bigr)(t,x)

(2.7.8)

on the finite-dimensional subspace
E_{N}
of
L^{2}(\Lambda)
spanned by the
e_{k}

with
\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N
(we have used the fact that
\Pi_{N}
and
\Delta

commute). This is equivalent to the finite-dimensional SDE for Fourier
coefficients

\dd\!\hat{\phi}_{t}=-\nabla\widehat{V}(\hat{\phi}_{t})\dd\!t+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dd\!W_{t}\;,

(2.7.9)

where
\widehat{V}
is simply the potential (
2.1.2
)
expressed in Fourier coordinates. This form is suitable for the
potential-theoretic approach described in
Section

1.5.2

One easily checks that for symmetric sets
B=-A\subset E_{N}

Lemma

1.5.12
still applies here, so that it is essentially
sufficient
to estimate the partition function
{\mathscr{Z}}
and the capacity
\capacity(A,B)

uniformly in the cut-off
N
.

As in the finite-dimensional case, it turns out to be useful to perform the
change of variables

\phi_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\phi_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{\perp}(x)\;,\qquad\phi_{0}=\langle e_{0},\phi_{N}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{N}(x)\dd\!x\;,

(2.7.10)

where
\phi_{\perp}
has zero mean. Inserting this in the
potential (
2.1.2
) yields

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V\biggl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\phi_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{\perp}(x)\biggr)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V_{0}(\phi_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})\phi_{\perp}\rangle+R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})\;,

(2.7.11)

where

\displaystyle V_{0}(\phi_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{L}{4}\biggl(\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}}{L}-1\biggr)^{2}\;,

(2.7.12)

\displaystyle Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{\perp}-1+\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{L}\;,

(2.7.13)

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{L}}\phi_{0}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{3}\dd\!x+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{4}\dd\!x\;.

(2.7.14)

Here the Laplacian
\Delta_{\perp}=\Delta_{\perp,N}
acts on
E_{\perp,N}
, the
subspace of zero-mean functions in
E_{N}
. Therefore, the eigenvalues of

Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})
are given by
\mu_{k}+3\phi_{0}^{2}/L\mathbin{\geqslant}2\pi/L-1>0
, where
the
\mu_{k}=\lambda_{k}-1
are the eigenvalues of
-\Delta_{\perp}-1
. We will denote
by
g_{N}(\phi_{0})
the Gaussian measure with covariance
Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})^{-1}
on

E_{\perp,N}
, called a (truncated) Gaussian free field with mass

m=(3\phi_{0}^{2}/L)^{1/2}
. A crucial estimate is the following one.

Lemma 2.7.4
.

There exist constants
C_{n}
, uniform in
N
and
\phi_{0}
, such that the bound

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\biggl[\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2n}\dd\!x\biggr]\mathbin{\leqslant}C_{n}

(2.7.15)

holds for all even moments of
\phi_{\perp}
, while the odd moments vanish.

Proof:
.

Let
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\colon k\neq 0,\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N\}
. Then we can represent

\phi_{\perp}
as

\phi_{\perp}(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{Z_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu_{k}+m^{2}}}e_{k}(x)\;,

(2.7.16)

where the
Z_{k}
are i.i.d. standard normal random variables. Therefore

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\biggl[\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2n}\dd\!x\biggr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{2n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[Z_{k_{1}}\dots Z_{k_{2n}}]}{\sqrt{\mu_{k_{1}}+m^{2}}\dots\sqrt{\mu_{k_{2n}}+m^{2}}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}e_{k_{1}}(x)^{2}\dots e_{k_{2n}}(x)^{2}\dd\!x\;.

(2.7.17)

The expectation vanishes unless the
k_{i}
are pairwise equal. Therefore, we
obtain

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\biggl[\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2n}\dd\!x\biggr]\lesssim\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{(\mu_{k_{1}}+m^{2})\dots(\mu_{k_{n}}+m^{2})}=\Biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}+m^{2}}\Biggr)^{n}\;,

(2.7.18)

which is bounded uniformly in
N
and
m
. Odd moments vanish by symmetry.
∎

We will see in the next chapter that this bound can be substantially improved
using Wick calculus. For now, it will be sufficient to obtain the following key
estimate.

Proposition 2.7.5
.

We have

\e^{-c\varepsilon}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}1+\e^{C(1+\phi_{0}^{2})}\varepsilon\;,

(2.7.19)

where
c
and
C
are uniform in
N
.

Proof:
.

By
Lemma

2.7.4
,
\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\!\left[R_{\varepsilon}\right]=c_{N}\varepsilon
, where

c_{N}\mathbin{\leqslant}c
is bounded uniformly in
N
and does not depend on
\phi_{0}
.
Therefore the lower bound follows directly from Jensen’s inequality, which
yields

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\bigr]\mathbin{\geqslant}\exp\Bigl\{-{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)\bigr]\Bigr\}\mathbin{\geqslant}\exp\bigl\{-c\varepsilon\bigr\}\;.

(2.7.20)

For the other direction, we first derive a rough bound on a slightly higher
exponential moment, using a change of mass in the Gaussian measure. For
any
p\mathbin{\geqslant}1
, we have

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-pR_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\bigr]=\sqrt{\frac{\det Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})}{\det Q_{\perp}(0)}}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(0)}\Biggl[\exp\biggl\{-\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{2L}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2}\dd\!x-pR_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)\biggr\}\Biggr]\;.

(2.7.21)

For the ratio of determinants, we note that

\log\Biggl(\frac{\det Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})}{\det Q_{\perp}(0)}\Biggr)=\log\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\Biggl(1+\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{L\mu_{k}}\Biggr)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\log\Biggl(1+\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{L\mu_{k}}\Biggr)\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{L}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}=:2C_{N}\phi_{0}^{2}\;,

(2.7.22)

where
C_{N}
is bounded uniformly in
N
.
Furthermore, using completion of squares, we get

\displaystyle\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{2L}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2}\dd\!x+pR_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2}\Biggl[\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{2L}+p\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{L}}\phi_{0}\phi_{\perp}(x)+p\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2}\Biggr]\dd\!x

(2.7.23)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2}\Biggl[\Biggl(\frac{2\sqrt{p}\phi_{0}}{\sqrt{L}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon p}\phi_{\perp}(x)\Biggr)^{2}+\frac{2(3-2p)\phi_{0}^{2}}{L}\Biggr]\dd\!x\;,

(2.7.24)

which is positive for
p\mathbin{\leqslant}3/2
. Therefore

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-(3/2)R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}\e^{C_{N}\phi_{0}^{2}}
. We now decompose

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{R_{\varepsilon}\mathbin{\geqslant}0\}}\bigr]+{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{R_{\varepsilon}<0\}}\bigr]

(2.7.25)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl\{R_{\varepsilon}\mathbin{\geqslant}0\bigr\}+{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[(1-R_{\varepsilon}+\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}-1+R_{\varepsilon})\mathbbm{1}_{\{R_{\varepsilon}<0\}}\bigr]

(2.7.26)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl\{R_{\varepsilon}\mathbin{\geqslant}0\bigr\}+{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl\{R_{\varepsilon}<0\bigr\}-{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}\bigr]+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}^{2}\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]

(2.7.27)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}1-{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}\bigr]+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}^{6}\bigr]^{1/3}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-(3/2)R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]^{2/3}\;,

(2.7.28)

where we have used the fact that
\e^{-x}-1+x\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{1}{2}x^{2}\e^{-x}
for

x<0
and Hölder’s inequality. Since by
Lemma

2.7.4
,

\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\!\left[\smash{R_{\varepsilon}^{6}}\right]={\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{3}\phi_{0}^{6})
, the
result follows from the bound on

\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\!\left[\smash{\e^{-(3/2)R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}}\right]
for an
appropriate choice of
C
.
∎

We can now estimate the partition function and capacity, in a similar way as we
did in the finite-dimensional case. We define the sets
A
and
B
as

A=-B=\bigl\{y\colon\lvert\phi_{0}+\sqrt{L}\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}\delta,\phi_{\perp}\in D_{\perp}\bigr\},

(2.7.29)

with
D_{\perp}
containing a ball of radius
\sqrt{\log(\varepsilon^{-1})}
in

H^{s}(\Lambda)
for some
s<\frac{1}{2}
. Then we have the following result.

Proposition 2.7.6
.

The partition function and the capacity satisfy

\displaystyle{\mathscr{Z}}_{N}:=\intop\nolimits_{E_{N}}\e^{-V(\phi)/\varepsilon}\dd\!\phi

\displaystyle=2\frac{(2\pi\varepsilon)^{N+1/2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{0}\det(-\Delta_{\perp}+2)}}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;,

(2.7.30)

\displaystyle\capacity(A,B)

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi\varepsilon)^{N+1/2}\sqrt{\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert}\e^{-V_{0}(0)/\varepsilon}}{2\pi\sqrt{\det(-\Delta_{\perp}-1)}}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;,

(2.7.31)

where
\Delta_{\perp}=\Delta_{\perp,N}
,
\nu_{0}=2
,
\mu_{0}=-1
, and the error
terms are uniform in the cut-off
N
.

Proof:
.

The partition function can be written as

{\mathscr{Z}}_{N}=\varepsilon^{N}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{-V_{0}(\phi_{0})/\varepsilon}\frac{(2\pi)^{N}}{\sqrt{\det Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})}}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\bigr]\dd\!\phi_{0}\;.

(2.7.32)

The result then follows from
Proposition

2.7.5
, using Laplace asymptotics
for the integral over
\phi_{0}
, which is dominated by values of
\phi_{0}
near

\pm\sqrt{L}
. The error term
\varepsilon\e^{C(1+\phi_{0}^{2})}
only yields an error of
order
\varepsilon
, because it is dominated by the factor
\e^{-V_{0}(\phi_{0})/\varepsilon}
.

For the upper bound on the capacity, we use as before the Dirichlet principle,
with a test function

h(\phi)=h_{0}(\phi_{0})=\frac{1}{c_{0}}\intop\nolimits_{\phi_{0}}^{a}\e^{V_{0}(\xi)/\varepsilon}\dd\!\xi\;,\qquad c_{0}=\intop\nolimits_{-a}^{a}\e^{V_{0}(\xi)/\varepsilon}\dd\!\xi\;.

(2.7.33)

Writing
{\mathscr{E}}(h)
in terms of

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mug_{N}(\phi_{0})}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\bigr]
yields the upper bound
in a similar way as for the partition function. For the lower bound on the
capacity, we use the Thomson principle. Here it is useful to decompose the
potential as

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V\biggl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\phi_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{\perp}(x)\biggr)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V_{0}(\phi_{0})+V_{\perp}(\phi_{\perp})+V_{+}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})\;,

(2.7.34)

where

\displaystyle V_{\perp}(\phi_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(0)\phi_{\perp}\rangle+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{4}\dd\!x\;,

(2.7.35)

\displaystyle V_{+}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{2L}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2}\dd\!x+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{0}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{3}\dd\!x\;.

(2.7.36)

We define the divergence-free unit flow

\varphi(\phi)=\frac{1}{K}\mathbbm{1}_{D_{\perp}}(\phi_{\perp})\e^{-V_{\perp}(\phi_{\perp})}e_{0}\;,\qquad K=\varepsilon^{N}\intop\nolimits_{D_{\perp}}\e^{-V_{\perp}(\phi_{\perp})}\dd\!\phi_{\perp}\;.

(2.7.37)

Substituting in the quadratic form (
1.5.33
) yields

{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon K^{2}}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{V_{0}(\phi_{0})/\varepsilon}\frac{(2\pi\varepsilon)^{N}}{\sqrt{\det Q_{\perp}(0)}}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{N}(0)}\Biggl[\mathbbm{1}_{D_{\perp}}\exp\biggl\{-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{4}\dd\!x+V_{+}(\phi_{0},\cdot)\biggr\}\Biggr]\dd\!\phi_{0}\;.

(2.7.38)

Proceeding as in
Proposition

2.7.5
, one checks that the gaussian
expectation is again equal to
1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
, despite the different sign of
the cubic term and the extra quadratic term. A similar analysis can be
performed for
K
. The lower bound then follows from Thomson’s principle.
∎

Proposition

2.7.6
is one of the essential building blocks of the
following theorem, which is the main result of this section.

Theorem 2.7.7

(Eyring-Kramers law for the one-dimensional Allen–Cahn SPDE)
.

Assume that
L<2\pi
and
A
and
B
are as in (
2.7.29
). Then

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi^{*}_{-}}\bigl[\tau_{B}\bigr]=\frac{2\pi}{\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert}\frac{\e^{[V(\phi^{*}_{0})-V(\phi^{*}_{-})]/\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\bigl|\det\bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}+3(-\Delta-1)^{-1}\bigr)\bigr|}}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;,

(2.7.39)

where
V(\phi^{*}_{0})-V(\phi^{*}_{-})=L/4
and
the determinant is to be interpreted as the inverse of the Fredholm
determinant (
2.7.3
) (cf.
2.7.3
).

Sketch of proof:
.

For finite cut-off
N
, and when starting in the equilibrium distribution on

\partial A
, the result follows from
Proposition

2.7.6
and

Theorem

1.5.6
. To extend this to the infinite-dimensional equation,
we have to check two more things. The first one is that first-hitting times of

B
in the finite-dimensional system converge to those of the
infinite-dimensional one. This can be done by combining an a priori estimate on

\mathbb{E}[\tau_{B}^{2}]
with a sample-path approximation argument given
in
[
BJ13
, Theorem 3.1]
, cf.
[
BG13
, Theorem 3.1
and Proposition 3.4]
. The second thing to be done is to prove that one
can replace the initial distribution on
\partial A
by a start in
\phi^{*}_{-}
.
This can be achieved by using the coupling argument given
in
[
MOS89
, Corollary 3.1]
, cf.
[
BG13
, Theorem 3.5
and Proposition 3.6]
.
∎

2.8
Bibliographical notes

The deterministic PDE commonly called Allen–Cahn equation was introduced
in
[
CI75
]
and
[
AC79
]
. Detailed descriptions of its
dynamics can be found for instance in
[
CP89
,
Che04
]
.

The definitions of parabolic Hölder spaces and related Schauder estimates
have been taken from
[
Hai14
]
and
[
CW17
]
, while
properties of Gaussian fields, in particular in relation with fractional
Sobolev spaces, can be found in
[
Hai09
]
.

Existence and uniqueness of solutions of the Allen–Cahn SPDE have been first
established in
[
FJL82
]
. Regarding the general existence and
uniqueness theory, as well as properties of invariant measures, we have mainly
followed
[
DPZ96
]
with some input
from
[
Hai09
]
. The sharper bounds on the decay of supremum norms
are from
[
Cer99
]
.

The large-deviation principle for the stochastic Allen–Cahn equation has been
first obtained in
[
FJL82
]
, and later extended
in
[
Fre88
,
Sow92
,
CM97
]
.

Eyring–Kramers laws for the one-dimensional Allen–Cahn equation have been
obtained in
[
BG13
]
(using spectral Galerkin approximations) and
in
[
Bar15
]
(using lattice discretisations). Results in these papers are
much more general than those presented here, as they apply to equations with a
general confining nonlinearity, with some smoothness assumptions. Results
in
[
BG13
]
include the bifurcation regime and the dynamics for
L>2\pi

for periodic boundary conditions, in which the transition states are
degenerate.
The proof presented here is somewhat different from the proofs given in those
works, which did not use Gaussian measure theory and the Thomson principle, but
were instead based on Hausdorff–Young inequalities and direct approximations
of Dirichlet forms, in the spirit of
[
BEGK04
]
.

Chapter 3
Allen–Cahn SPDE in two space dimensions

The particle system considered in
Chapter

1
makes also sense in two
dimensions, that is, for a two-dimensional lattice of
N^{2}
particles, each one
interacting with its
4
nearest neighbours. Proceeding as in
Chapter

2
,
we obtain formally a continuum limit given by the SPDE

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)\;,

(3.0.1)

where
x
now belongs to the two-dimensional torus
\Lambda=(\mathbb{R}/(L\mathbb{Z}))^{2}
, and
the Laplacian
\Delta=\partial_{x_{1}x_{1}}+\partial_{x_{2}x_{2}}
acts on both
components of
x
. The PDE obtained in the deterministic case
\varepsilon=0
is
perfectly well-defined.

Exercise 3.0.1
.

Compute the eigenvalues of the Laplacian
\Delta
for periodic boundary
conditions on
\Lambda
, and use it to determine the stability of the constant
stationary solutions of the deterministic PDE. Argue that for
L<2\pi
, we are
again in the double-well situation, as there can be no non-constant stationary
solutions. Prove this fact, using

W(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}\dd\!x

(3.0.2)

as a Lyapunov function.

It turns out, however, that the SPDE (
3.0.1
) is not well-posed. This
is related to the fact that space-time white noise is more irregular in
dimension
2
than in dimension
1
. Before discussing a cure for this problem,
we first have to understand in more detail where the problem comes from.

3.1
The need for renormalisation

Space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda
can be defined exactly as in

Definition

2.2.1
, except that
\Lambda
now denotes the
two-dimensional torus. The scaling operator in (
2.2.9
) now
takes the form

({\mathscr{S}}^{\tau,\lambda}\varphi)(t,x):=\frac{1}{\tau\lambda^{2}}\varphi\biggl(\frac{t}{\tau},\frac{x}{\lambda}\biggr)\;,

(3.1.1)

and the scaling property of
Proposition

2.2.5
becomes

\xi_{\tau,\lambda}\eqinlaw\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau}\lambda}\xi\;.

(3.1.2)

The parabolic Hölder spaces are defined as before, with the parabolic norm
defined by

\lVert(t,x)-(s,y)\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}=\lvert t-s\rvert^{1/2}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{2}\lvert x_{i}-y_{i}\rvert\;,

(3.1.3)

and the scaling operator in (
2.2.17
) replaced by

({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{t,x}\varphi)(s,y):=\frac{1}{\lambda^{4}}\varphi\biggl(\frac{s-t}{\lambda^{2}},\frac{y-x}{\lambda}\biggr)\;.

(3.1.4)

Making the necessary changes in the proof of

Theorem

2.2.8
, one now finds that

\xi\in{\mathscr{C}}^{-2-\kappa}_{\mathfrak{s}}\qquad\text{for any $\kappa>0$\;.}

(3.1.5)

Exercise 3.1.1
.

Prove (
3.1.5
) by adapting the argument used in

Theorem

2.2.8
.

The Schauder estimate stated in
Theorem

2.3.9
remains true in the present
setting. The problem is, however, that the stochastic convolution
P*\xi

belongs to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
for any
\alpha<0
only, instead any

\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
in the one-dimensional case. Therefore, the stochastic
convolution is only a distribution, albeit rather close to being a function.
Since there is no canonical way of multiplying two distributions, the
fixed-point equation (
2.4.2
) becomes ill-defined, as it requires
the nonlinear function
F
to be applied to the stochastic convolution.

As the stochastic convolution is a Gaussian process, its singularity can be
traced to the behaviour of the covariance, which diverges near the diagonal

y=x
(see also (
2.5.5
)). Therefore, a regularisation becomes
possible by subtracting an “infinite constant” from the right-hand
side of (
3.0.1
), a process known as
renormalisation
. An
important tool to understand how it works is Wick calculus for Gaussian random
variables.

3.2
Wick calculus

3.2.1
Isserlis’ theorem

Consider a Gaussian random vector
X
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with covariance matrix
C
.
Recall that this means that for any test function
F
, one has

\mathbb{E}\bigl[F(X)\bigr]=\frac{1}{{\mathscr{Z}}}\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F(x)\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\dd\!x\;,

(3.2.1)

where
C
has entries
C_{ij}=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]
, and
{\mathscr{Z}}
is such that

\mathbb{E}[1]=1
. Moments of
X
can easily be computed by the following
integration-by-parts formula, which is a finite-dimensional version of the
Schwinger–Dyson equations from Quantum Field Theory.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

For any
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
we have

\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{i}F(X)\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}C_{ij}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\partial_{j}F(X)\bigr]

(3.2.2)

for all real-valued differentiable
F
such that both sides of the equality are
well-defined.

Proof:
.

Leibniz’ rule yields

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\Bigl(F(x)\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\Bigr)=\biggl[\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{j}}(x)-\frac{1}{2}F(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle\biggr]\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\;.

(3.2.3)

Since we have

\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}C_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle=\sumop\displaylimits_{j,k=1}^{N}C_{ij}C^{-1}_{jk}x_{k}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{ik}x_{k}=x_{i}\;,

(3.2.4)

it follows that

x_{i}F(x)\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}C_{ij}\biggl[-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\Bigl(F(x)\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\Bigr)+\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{j}}\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\biggr]\;.

(3.2.5)

Integrating over the whole space, we see that the boundary terms vanish, and
the result follows at once.
∎

An immediate consequence of this result is the following theorem, due to
Leon Isserlis.

Theorem 3.2.2

(Isserlis)
.

For any
1\mathbin{\leqslant}n\mathbin{\leqslant}N/2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}\dots X_{2n}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\text{pairings ${\mathscr{P}}$ of $\{1,\dots,2n\}$}}\;\prodop\displaylimits_{\{i,j\}\in{\mathscr{P}}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{i}X_{j}\bigr]\;,

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}\dots X_{2n-1}\bigr]

\displaystyle=0\;,

(3.2.7)

where a
pairing
of
\{1,\dots,2n\}
is a partition

{\mathscr{P}}=\{\{i_{1},j_{1}\},\dots,\{i_{n},j_{n}\}\}
of this set into disjoint subsets
of two elements.

Proof:
.

By induction on
n
, applying (
3.2.2
) with
i=1
and
F(X)
of the form

X_{2}\dots X_{m}
.
∎

Remark 3.2.3
.

An alternative proof relies on the identity

\mathbb{E}[\e^{\icx\langle\ell,X\rangle}]=\e^{-\langle\ell,C\ell\rangle/2}
, expanding
both sides in powers of
\ell=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})^{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm T$}}}
and
identifying coefficients.

For instance, in the case
2n=4
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}X_{4}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{2}\bigr]\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{3}X_{4}\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{3}\bigr]\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{2}X_{4}\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{4}\bigr]\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{2}X_{3}\bigr]\;.

(3.2.8)

A convenient graphical way of representing this relation is the following:

\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}X_{4}\bigr]=\raisebox{-25.0pt}{\hbox to56.91pt{\vbox to56.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.22638pt\lower-42.67914pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(3.2.9)

Exercise 3.2.4
.

Determine the number of pairings of
\{1,\dots,2n\}
. Use this information to
compute
\mathbb{E}[X^{2n}]
when
X
follows a standard normal distribution, and
prove (
2.2.21
).

3.2.2
Hermite polynomials

Consider the one-dimensional Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process, solving the SDE

\dd\!X_{t}=-X_{t}\dd\!t+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dd\!W_{t}\;.

(3.2.10)

We know from
Section

1.3
that its invariant distribution
\pi
has
density
\e^{-x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}/\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon}
with respect to Lebesgue measure,
while its generator

{\mathscr{L}}=\varepsilon\e^{x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\e^{-x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}

(3.2.11)

is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\pi(\dd\!x))
. Furthermore, one easily checks that the
operators

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\e^{x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}\biggl(x+\varepsilon\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\biggr)\e^{-x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}=\varepsilon\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\;,

(3.2.12)

\displaystyle a^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=-\e^{x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}\varepsilon\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\e^{-x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}=x-\varepsilon\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}

(3.2.13)

are mutually adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\pi(\dd\!x))
, and satisfy the relations

a^{\dagger}a=-\varepsilon{\mathscr{L}}\;,\qquad aa^{\dagger}-a^{\dagger}a=\varepsilon\;.

(3.2.14)

These relations are useful to find eigenfunctions of
{\mathscr{L}}
. Indeed, we
obviously have
{\mathscr{L}}H_{0}=0
, where
H_{0}(x)=1
is constant. Now if
H
is an
eigenfunction of
{\mathscr{L}}
with eigenvalue
-\lambda
, we have

-{\mathscr{L}}(a^{\dagger}H)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}a^{\dagger}aa^{\dagger}H=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}a^{\dagger}(a^{\dagger}a+\varepsilon)H=(\lambda+1)a^{\dagger}H\;,

(3.2.15)

showing that
a^{\dagger}H
is an eigenfunction of
{\mathscr{L}}
with eigenvalue

-(\lambda+1)
. We conclude that the functions in the family
(H_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

defined recursively by

H_{n+1}(x;\varepsilon)=(a^{\dagger}H_{n})(x;\varepsilon)=xH_{n}(x;\varepsilon)-\varepsilon\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}H_{n}(x;\varepsilon)

(3.2.16)

are orthogonal, and satisfy

{\mathscr{L}}H_{n}(x;\varepsilon)=-nH_{n}(x;\varepsilon)\;,\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\;.

(3.2.17)

The corresponding eigenfunctions of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\dagger}
are simply given by

\e^{-x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}H_{n}(x;\varepsilon)
.

Remark 3.2.5
.

The operators
a^{\dagger}
and
a
behave in the same way as creation and
annihilation operators for the quantum harmonic oscillator. In fact, the
generator
{\mathscr{L}}
is conjugated to the Hamiltonian of the harmonic oscillator via
the transformation
{\mathscr{L}}\mapsto\e^{-x^{2}/(4\varepsilon)}{\mathscr{L}}\e^{x^{2}/(4\varepsilon)}
, and the
operators
a^{\dagger}
and
a
are conjugated in the same way to those of the
harmonic oscillator.

In fact, one can show that the family
(H_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
forms a complete
orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\pi(\dd\!x))
. See for
instance
[
Nua06
, Lemma 1.1.2]
.

Definition 3.2.6

(Hermite polynomials)
.

The polynomial

H_{n}(x;\varepsilon)=\bigl(a^{\dagger}\bigr)^{n}H_{0}(x;\varepsilon)=(-\varepsilon)^{n}\e^{x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}\frac{\dd\!^{n}}{\dd\!x^{n}}\e^{-x^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}

(3.2.18)

is called the
n
th Hermite polynomial
with variance
\varepsilon
.

Hermite polynomials can easily be computed by the recurrence
relation (
3.2.16
). The first few of them are given by

\displaystyle H_{0}(x;\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=1

(3.2.19)

\displaystyle H_{1}(x;\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=x

(3.2.20)

\displaystyle H_{2}(x;\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=x^{2}-\varepsilon

(3.2.21)

\displaystyle H_{3}(x;\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=x^{3}-3\varepsilon x

(3.2.22)

\displaystyle H_{4}(x;\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=x^{4}-6\varepsilon x^{2}+3\varepsilon^{2}\;.

(3.2.23)

A useful alternative expression for Hermite polynomials relies on their
generating function.

Lemma 3.2.7
.

For every
t,x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon\mathbin{\geqslant}0
, one has

G(t,x;\varepsilon):=\e^{tx-\varepsilon t^{2}/2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}H_{n}(x;\varepsilon)\;.

(3.2.24)

Exercise 3.2.8
.

Prove
Lemma

3.2.7
.
Hint:
Take the derivative
with respect to
t
on both sides, and use the recurrence
relation (
3.2.16
).
Another proof (with a slightly different normalisation) can be found
in
[
Nua06
, Lemma 1.1.1]
.

A very important consequence of
Lemma

3.2.7
is the following
identity, which can be seen as an analogue of Isserlis’ theorem for Hermite
polynomials of Gaussian random variables.

Lemma 3.2.9
.

Let
X
and
Y
be jointly Gaussian centred random variables, of respective
variance
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
. Then for any
n,m\mathbin{\geqslant}0
, one has

\mathbb{E}\bigl[H_{n}(X;\varepsilon_{1})H_{m}(Y;\varepsilon_{2})\bigr]=\begin{cases}n!\mathbb{E}\bigl[XY\bigr]^{n}&\text{if $n=m$\;,}\\
0&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(3.2.25)

Proof:
.

Let
C
denote the covariance matrix of
Z=(X,Y)^{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm T$}}}
. Then the
expression

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\e^{\langle\ell,Z\rangle}\Bigr]=\exp\biggl\{\frac{1}{2}\langle\ell,C\ell\rangle\biggr\}

(3.2.26)

for the Laplace transform of
Z
, applied with
\ell=(t,s)^{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm T$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm T$}}}
,
implies that

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\e^{tX-\varepsilon_{1}t^{2}/2}\e^{sY-\varepsilon_{2}s^{2}/2}\Bigr]=\e^{ts\mathbb{E}[XY]}\;.

(3.2.27)

Using (
3.2.24
) and expanding both sides yields

\sumop\displaylimits_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n}s^{m}}{n!m!}\mathbb{E}\bigl[H_{n}(X;\varepsilon_{1})H_{m}(Y;\varepsilon_{2})\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(ts)^{k}}{k!}\mathbb{E}\bigl[XY\bigr]^{k}\;.

(3.2.28)

Identifying coefficients of the power series gives the result.
∎

A convenient graphical way of representing (
3.2.25
), for
instance in the case
n=4
, is

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\raisebox{-5.5pt}{
\hbox to28.45pt{\vbox to17.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.22638pt\lower-8.5359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.91 1.36 C 11.99 5.59 19.51 5.59 28.59 1.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.91 -1.36 C 11.99 -5.59 19.51 -5.59 28.59 -1.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.83 -3.1 C 3.84 -14.34 27.65 -14.34 30.66 -3.1}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\;.

(3.2.29)

Here each diagram with four legs represents a term
H_{4}
, and each edge on the
right-hand side represents a covariance
\mathbb{E}[XY]
. The combinatorial factor

4!
counts the number of ways of pairing the four legs, with the rule that
only legs from different diagrams can be paired. This is the main difference
with Isserlis’ theorem, which allowed for all possible pairings. Similar
identities as (
3.2.25
) can be obtained for products of more
than two Hermite polynomials, by summing over all pairings of legs belonging to
different terms.

Note that if we put
m=0
in (
3.2.25
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}\bigl[H_{n}(X;\varepsilon)\bigr]=\delta_{n0}\;,

(3.2.30)

which shows that Hermite polynomials of Gaussian random variables are centred
for
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
.
Another useful consequence of
Lemma

3.2.7
is the following
binomial formula for Hermite polynomials.

Lemma 3.2.10
.

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\mathbin{\geqslant}0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

H_{n}(x+y,\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})=\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{n}\binom{n}{m}H_{m}(x,\varepsilon_{1})H_{n-m}(y,\varepsilon_{2})\;.

(3.2.31)

Proof:
.

Expanding the identity

\e^{t(x+y)-(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})t^{2}/2}=\e^{tx-\varepsilon_{1}t^{2}/2}\e^{ty-\varepsilon_{2}t^{2}/2}

(3.2.32)

yields

\sumop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}H_{n}(x+y;\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})=\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{m}}{m!}H_{m}(x;\varepsilon_{1})\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}H_{k}(y;\varepsilon_{2})\;.

(3.2.33)

Comparing coefficients of
t^{n}
gives the result.
∎

3.2.3
The two-dimensional Gaussian free field

We define a Fourier basis of
\Lambda=(\mathbb{R}/(L\mathbb{Z}))^{2}
by

e_{k}(x)=e_{k_{1}}(x_{1})e_{k_{2}}(x_{2})\;,\qquad k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\;,

(3.2.34)

where the
e_{k_{i}}
are the one-dimensional basis functions defined
in (
2.3.11
). The slight abuse of notation made by using the
same symbol for one- and two-dimensional basis functions will not matter in
what follows. The
e_{k}
are eigenfunctions of the Laplacian on
\Lambda
with
periodic boundary conditions, satisfying

\Delta e_{k}=-\lambda_{k}e_{k}\;,\qquad\lambda_{k}=\biggl(\frac{2\lVert k\rVert\pi}{L}\biggr)^{2}\;.

(3.2.35)

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define sets of indices
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\colon\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N\}
and
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{+}=\{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}\colon k_{1},k_{2}>0\}
, where

\lvert k\rvert=\lvert k_{1}\rvert+\lvert k_{2}\rvert
. Let
E_{N}
be the space spanned by

\{e_{k}\colon k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}\}
. Note the identities

\displaystyle e_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(x)^{2}+e_{(k_{1},-k_{2})}(x)^{2}+e_{(-k_{1},k_{2})}(x)^{2}+e_{(-k_{1},-k_{2})}(x)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\forall(k_{1},k_{2})\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{+}\;,

(3.2.36)

\displaystyle e_{(k_{1},0)}(x)^{2}+e_{(-k_{1},0)}(x)^{2}=e_{(0,k_{2})}(x)^{2}+e_{(0,-k_{2})}(x)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\forall k_{1},k_{2}\neq 0\;.

(3.2.37)

Definition 3.2.11

(Two-dimensional Gaussian free field)
.

The truncated two-dimensional
Gaussian free field (GFF)
with covariance

(-\Delta_{N}+1)^{-1}
on
\Lambda
is defined as

\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{Z_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}+1}}e_{k}(x)\;,

(3.2.38)

where
\Delta_{N}
is the restriction of
\Delta
to
E_{N}
and
the
Z_{k}
are i.i.d. standard normal random variables.

Thanks to the symmetry relations (
3.2.37
),

\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x)^{2}]
does not depend on
x
, and is thus equal to

\displaystyle C_{N}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x)^{2}]\dd\!x=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}+1}e_{k}(x)^{2}\dd\!x

(3.2.39)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}+1}=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\Tr\bigl[(-\Delta_{N}+1)^{-1}\bigr]\;.

(3.2.40)

Since
k
is two-dimensional and
\lambda_{k}
grows like
\lVert k\rVert^{2}
, this
constant diverges like
\log N
.

Exercise 3.2.12
.

Show that

C_{N}=\frac{\log N}{2\pi}+{\mathcal{O}}(1)

(3.2.41)

as
N\to\infty
.
Hint:
View (
3.2.40
) as a Riemann sum and integrate
using polar coordinates.

Thus we see that the non-truncated two-dimensional GFF has infinite variance at
every point!
Fortunately, since this variance is constant, we can subtract it in
order to get a meaningful object in the limit, a procedure which is akin to an
Itô correction. This leads to the following definition.

Definition 3.2.13

(Wick powers of the GFF)
.

For any integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the
n
th Wick power of the truncated GFF is defined
as

{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}={\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}:=H_{n}(\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x);C_{N})\;,\qquad x\in\Lambda\;.

(3.2.42)

Note in particular that owing to (
3.2.30
), all Wick powers
are centred random variables at any
x\in\Lambda
. Furthermore,

Lemma

3.2.9
implies the following important result, which
is a generalisation of
Lemma

2.7.4
to the two-dimensional case.

Proposition 3.2.14
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\sup_{N\mathbin{\geqslant}1}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\Biggl(\frac{1}{L^{2}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\Biggr)^{2}\Biggr]<\infty\;.

(3.2.43)

Proof:
.

By (
3.2.25
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\Biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\Biggr)^{2}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\mathbb{E}\bigl[{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(y)\mathpunct{:}}\bigr]\dd\!x\dd\!y

(3.2.44)

\displaystyle=n!\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x)\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(y)\bigr]^{n}\dd\!x\dd\!y

(3.2.45)

\displaystyle=n!\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\Biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k,\ell\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[Z_{k}Z_{\ell}]}{\sqrt{(\lambda_{k}+1)(\lambda_{\ell}+1)}}e_{k}(x)e_{\ell}(y)\Biggr)^{n}\dd\!x\dd\!y

(3.2.46)

\displaystyle=n!\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\Biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}+1}e_{k}(x)e_{k}(y)\Biggr)^{n}\dd\!x\dd\!y

(3.2.47)

\displaystyle=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{k_{1}}+1)\dots(\lambda_{k_{n}}+1)}\Biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}e_{k_{1}}(x)\dots e_{k_{n}}(x)\dd\!x\Biggr)^{2}\;.

(3.2.48)

By orthogonality of the eigenfunctions, the integral in the last expression
vanishes unless some linear combination
k_{1}\pm k_{2}\pm\dots\pm k_{n}
is equal
to zero. Therefore, we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\Biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\Biggr)^{2}\Biggr]\lesssim\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}\\
k_{1}+\dots+k_{n}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{k_{1}}+1)\dots(\lambda_{k_{n}}+1)}\;.

(3.2.49)

Thanks to the condition that the sum of the
k_{i}
vanishes, this sum is bounded
uniformly in
N
, by a Young-type inequality, see for
instance
[
ZZ15a
, Lemma 3.10]
.
∎

Remark 3.2.15
.

In the graphical notation introduced in (
3.2.29
), we
have
C_{N}=\raisebox{-5.5pt}{
\hbox to17.07pt{\vbox to20.77pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.5359pt\lower-2.27626pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
