\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}
, since
C_{N}
involves the covariance of
any
\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x)
with itself.

3.2.4
Wick powers of the stochastic convolution

We consider now the stochastic heat equation

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\xi_{N}(t,x)\;,

(3.2.50)

where
\xi_{N}=\Pi_{N}\xi
is a spectral Galerkin approximation of space-time
white noise (cf. (
2.7.7
)). Similarly to (
2.3.8
), the
solution of (
3.2.50
) can be written as

\phi=P\phi_{0}+P*\xi_{N}\;,

(3.2.51)

where
P
denotes the analogue of the heat kernel (
2.3.5
) in
two dimensions, namely

P(t,x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}P_{\,\mathbb{R}^{2}}(t,x-kL)\;,\qquad P_{\,\mathbb{R}^{2}}(t,x):=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\e^{-\lVert x\rVert^{2}/(4t)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>0\}}\;.

(3.2.52)

The stochastic convolution
P*\xi_{N}
can be analysed as in
Section

2.3
. In
particular, subtracting the Brownian motion
\smash{W^{(0)}_{t}}
of the zeroth
Fourier mode, we find that
(P*\xi_{N})(t,x)-\smash{W^{(0)}_{t}}
converges as

t\to\infty
to a Gaussian free field with covariance

(-\Delta_{\perp,N})^{-1}
, where
\Delta_{\perp,N}=\Pi_{N}\Delta_{\perp}
acts on
zero-mean functions. By
Proposition

3.2.14
, defining
C_{N}
as
in (
3.2.40
), all Wick powers
{\mathpunct{:}(P*\xi_{N})^{n}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}
will have a
variance uniformly bounded in
N
, and should have well-defined limits (one
easily checks that adding a constant to the
\lambda_{k}
in (
3.2.40
)
does not change its divergent part).

There is a slightly different way to regularise the stochastic heat equation,
which turns out to be equivalent to (
3.2.50
). Let

\varrho:\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
be smooth, compactly supported and of
integral
1
. Then for any
\delta\in(0,1]
, define the
mollifier

\varrho_{\delta}(t,x)={\mathscr{S}}^{\delta}_{0}\varrho(t,x):=\frac{1}{\delta^{4}}\varrho\biggl(\frac{t}{\delta^{2}},\frac{x}{\delta}\biggr)\;.

(3.2.53)

We can then set
\xi^{\delta}(t,x)=(\varrho_{\delta}*\xi)(t,x)
(interpreted as
testing
\xi
against
\varrho((t,x)-\cdot)
), and consider the regularised
stochastic heat equation

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;,

(3.2.54)

which admits solutions in the classical sense since
\xi^{\delta}
is smooth,
namely

\phi=P\phi_{0}+P*\xi^{\delta}\;,

(3.2.55)

with a stochastic convolution
P*\xi^{\delta}
given by

(P*\xi^{\delta})(t,x)=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P(t-s,x-y)\xi^{\delta}(s,y)\dd\!y\dd\!s=\langle\xi,P*\varrho_{\delta}\rangle(t,x)\;.

(3.2.56)

Since this object and its powers will play a central role in the following, we
introduce the graphical notation (borrowed
from
[
Hai14
,
CW17
]
)

(P*\xi^{\delta})(t,x)=\,\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.3379pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{\delta}(t,x)\;,\qquad(P*\xi^{\delta})(t,x)^{2}=\,\hbox to9.79pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.8945pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.92 8.27 M 6.5 8.27 C 6.5 9.14 5.79 9.84 4.92 9.84 C 4.05 9.84 3.35 9.14 3.35 8.27 C 3.35 7.4 4.05 6.69 4.92 6.69 C 5.79 6.69 6.5 7.4 6.5 8.27 Z M 4.92 8.27}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }

(3.2.57)

Writing
P_{\delta}=P*\varrho_{\delta}
and using the defining
property (
2.2.2
) of space-time white noise, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\,\hbox to9.79pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.8945pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{\delta}(t,x)\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,P_{\delta}\rangle(t,x)^{2}\bigr]=\langle P_{\delta},P_{\delta}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda)}=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P_{\delta}(t,x)^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t=:C_{\delta}\;,

(3.2.58)

which is independent of
(t,x)
.

Proposition 3.2.16
.

Assume that
\varrho(t,x)=\varrho^{(0)}(t)\varrho^{(1)}(x)
where

\varrho^{(1)}
is even. Then the constant
C_{\delta}
has the asymptotic
behaviour

C_{\delta}=\frac{\log(\delta^{-1})}{4\pi}+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\qquad\text{as $\delta\to 0$\;.}

(3.2.59)

Proof:
.

We present a proof which is perhaps not the shortest possible, but will
highlight the link with other object such as the Green function. First note
that the Markov property implies that for any
t,s>0
and
x\in\Lambda
,

\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P(t,x-y)P(s,y)\dd\!y=P(t+s,x)\;.

(3.2.60)

Furthermore, for any
x\in\Lambda
,

\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}P(t,x)\dd\!t=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{\infty}\e^{t\Delta}(x)\dd\!t=-G(x)\;,

(3.2.61)

where
G=\Delta^{-1}
is the Green function of the Laplacian acting on
zero-mean functions, that is, the solution of
(\Delta G)(x)=\delta(x)
. Taking
into account the properties of
\varrho
, one finds that for
\delta>0
, these
relations become

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P_{\delta}(t,x-y)P_{\delta}(s,y)\dd\!y

\displaystyle=\tilde{P}_{\delta}(t+s,x)\;,

(3.2.62)

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}P_{\delta}(t,x)\dd\!t=-G_{\delta}(x)\;,

(3.2.63)

where
G_{\delta}=G*\varrho_{\delta}^{(1)}
and
\tilde{P}_{\delta}=P*(\varrho*\varrho)_{\delta}
is a regularised version of
P
with a
different mollifier. It follows, using that
P
is even in
x
, that

\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P_{\delta}(t,x)^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{P}_{\delta}(2t,0)\dd\!t=-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{G}_{\delta}(0)\;,

(3.2.64)

where again
\tilde{G}_{\delta}
is a regularisation of
G
with a different
mollifier. Using the fact that the Green function of the two-dimensional
Laplacian behaves like
\log{\lVert x\rVert}/(2\pi)
near the origin, one easily
obtains
\tilde{G}_{\delta}(0)=\log(\delta)/(2\pi)+{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, which
proves (
3.2.59
).
∎

Remark 3.2.17
.

Note that the expressions (
3.2.59
) and (
3.2.41
) have
the same divergent behaviour. Furthermore, the divergent part of
C_{\delta}
is
independent of the mollifier
\varrho
. The term of order
1
, however, will
depend on
\varrho
. In other words, there is no canonical choice for the
bounded part of the renormalisation constants.

shows that the Wick powers

\displaystyle{\mathpunct{:}\,\hbox to9.79pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.8945pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

(3.2.67)

have a variance uniformly bounded in
\delta
. In fact, one can show that they
admit well-defined limits
,
and
as
\delta\searrow 0
in
terms of iterated stochastic integrals, which belong to
{\mathscr{C}}^{-\kappa}_{\mathfrak{s}}

for any
\kappa>0
. See
[
CW17
, Appendix A]
,

[
Hai14
, Section 10.1]
and
[
DPD03
, Lemma 3.2]
for
details. The same works of course for higher powers of the stochastic
convolution.

Remark 3.2.18
.

A formal way of writing these limits is to introduce

\displaystyle\xi(\dd\!z_{1})\diamond\xi(\dd\!z_{2})={}

\displaystyle\xi(\dd\!z_{1})\xi(\dd\!z_{2})-\delta(z_{1}-z_{2})\;,

(3.2.68)

\displaystyle\xi(\dd\!z_{1})\diamond\xi(\dd\!z_{2})\diamond\xi(\dd\!z_{3})={}

\displaystyle\xi(\dd\!z_{1})\xi(\dd\!z_{2})\xi(\dd\!z_{3})

(3.2.69)

\displaystyle-\xi(\dd\!z_{1})\delta(z_{2}-z_{3})-\xi(\dd\!z_{2})\delta(z_{3}-z_{1})-\xi(\dd\!z_{3})\delta(z_{1}-z_{2})\;.

(3.2.70)

The quantities on the left-hand side, which define the limiting objects

and
when integrated against a product of
P_{\delta}
, can be given a
rigorous meaning as elements of the Wiener chaos decomposition,
see
[
Nua06
, Section 1.1]
,
and
[
CW17
, Appendix A]
.

3.3
Existence and uniqueness of solutions

The discussion so far suggests that the renormalised version of the
two-dimensional stochastic Allen–Cahn equation we should consider is

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi_{\delta}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\Delta\phi_{\delta}(t,x)+\phi_{\delta}(t,x)-{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\delta}(t,x)^{3}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{\delta}}+\xi^{\delta}(t,x)

(3.3.1)

\displaystyle=\Delta\phi_{\delta}(t,x)+\phi_{\delta}(t,x)-\Bigl(\phi_{\delta}(t,x)^{3}-3C_{\delta}\phi_{\delta}(t,x)\Bigr)+\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;.

(3.3.2)

For
\delta>0
, this is a smooth PDE (for any realisation of the noise), whose
solution with initial condition
\phi_{\delta,0}
satisfies the fixed-point
equation

\phi_{\delta}=P\phi_{\delta,0}+\,\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.3379pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 9.84 M 1.57 9.84 C 1.57 10.71 0.87 11.42 0 11.42 C -0.87 11.42 -1.57 10.71 -1.57 9.84 C -1.57 8.97 -0.87 8.27 0 8.27 C 0.87 8.27 1.57 8.97 1.57 9.84 Z M 0 9.84}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{\delta}+P*\bigl[\phi_{\delta}-\phi_{\delta}^{3}+3C_{\delta}\phi_{\delta}\bigr]\;.

(3.3.3)

The idea exploited by Da Prato and Debussche in
[

]
is
that since the most irregular term on the right-hand side should be the
stochastic convolution
\,\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.3379pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{\delta}
, one can write

\phi_{\delta}=\,\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.3379pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{\delta}+\psi_{\delta}\;,

where

(3.3.7)

The key result allowing to analyse this fixed-point equation is the following,
see
[
Hai14
, Proposition 4.14]
as well
as
[
BCD11
, Section 2.6]
.

Theorem 3.3.1

(Product in Besov spaces)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
\alpha+\beta>0
. Then there exists a bilinear
map
B:{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}\times{\mathscr{C}}^{\beta}_{\mathfrak{s}}\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha\wedge\beta}_{\mathfrak{s}}
with the following properties:

1.

If
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
and
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\beta}_{\mathfrak{s}}
are continuous
functions, then
B(f,g)(z)=f(z)g(z)
.

2.

For arbitrary
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
and
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\beta}_{\mathfrak{s}}
, one
has the bound

\lVert B(f,g)\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha\wedge\beta}_{\mathfrak{s}}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}}\lVert g\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\beta}_{\mathfrak{s}}}\;.

(3.3.8)

If
\alpha+\beta\mathbin{\leqslant}0
, then no bilinear map satisfying these two properties
exists.

We then have the following existence and uniqueness result.

Theorem 3.3.2

(Global existence and uniqueness for the two-dimensional
Allen–Cahn SPDE)
.

Assume
\psi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{-\kappa}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda)
.
For sufficiently small
\kappa>0
, equation (
3.3.7
) with initial
condition
\psi_{0}
admits an almost surely unique global solution in

{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Lambda)
for any
\alpha<2-\kappa
.
Therefore, the solution of (
3.3.2
) with initial condition

\varrho_{\delta}*\psi_{0}
converges almost surely, as
\delta\searrow 0
, to a
stochastic process
(\psi_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
such that
\psi-\,\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.3379pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 9.84 M 1.57 9.84 C 1.57 10.71 0.87 11.42 0 11.42 C -0.87 11.42 -1.57 10.71 -1.57 9.84 C -1.57 8.97 -0.87 8.27 0 8.27 C 0.87 8.27 1.57 8.97 1.57 9.84 Z M 0 9.84}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Lambda)
.

Sketch of proof:
.

We first prove a local existence result, in the spirit
of
Theorem

2.4.2
. We know that

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 9.84 M 1.57 9.84 C 1.57 10.71 0.87 11.42 0 11.42 C -0.87 11.42 -1.57 10.71 -1.57 9.84 C -1.57 8.97 -0.87 8.27 0 8.27 C 0.87 8.27 1.57 8.97 1.57 9.84 Z M 0 9.84}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,\,\hbox to9.79pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.8945pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

(3.3.9)

for some constant
M
. The Schauder estimate shows that the convolution of this
quantity with
P
belongs to
{\mathscr{C}}^{2-\kappa}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\Lambda)
.
This is not sufficient to obtain a contraction, since the norm does not become
small for small
T
. Such a
T
-dependence can, however, be obtained by
sacrificing a bit of regularity. One can indeed show that, for any
\sigma>0
,

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,]\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{2-\kappa-\sigma}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\Lambda)}\lesssim T^{\sigma}\bigl(\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\Lambda)}+M\bigr)^{3}\;.

(3.3.10)

Choosing
\alpha=2-\kappa-\sigma
, we obtain that the right-hand side
of (
3.3.7
) maps a ball into itself for sufficiently small
T
. A
similar argument shows that this map is a contraction in that ball for small

T
, so that the existence of a local solution follows from Banach’s
fixed-point theorem. Since
\sigma
was arbitrary, the fixed point has the
claimed regularity.

Global existence then follows from showing non-explosion, by using an
appropriate Lyapunov function and a Gronwall-type argument.
See
[
DPD03
, p. 1914]

and
[
TW18b
, Section 3.4]
, as well
as
[
MW17b
, Section 9]
for an argument working when
\Lambda

is replaced by
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
∎

Remark 3.3.3
.

This result appears with different variants of function spaces in the
literature. The proof given in
[
DPD03
]
uses spaces mixing
Lebesgue spaces
L^{p}
in the time variable and Besov spaces

{\mathscr{B}}^{\alpha}_{q,r}

in the space variable. In
[
TW18b
, Section 3]
, one finds a
formulation with Hölder regularity in space and continuity in time. The
formulation here follows
[
Hai14
]
. The result remains true for any
polynomial nonlinearity of odd degree and negative leading-order coefficient.

3.4
Invariant measure

We now consider the stochastic Allen–Cahn equation written in the form

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\phi(t,x)-{\mathpunct{:}\phi(t,x)^{3}\mathpunct{:}}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)\;,

(3.4.1)

where we have reintroduced the small parameter in front of the noise for later
use. Here the Wick power is with respect to the constant
\varepsilon\overline{C}_{\delta}=2\varepsilon C_{\delta}
since the variance of the noise has been
multiplied by
2\varepsilon
.

By analogy with (
2.5.19
), a natural candidate for the invariant
measure of (
3.4.1
) is

\pi(\dd\!\phi):=\frac{1}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{0}}\e^{-\widetilde{V}(\phi)/\varepsilon}\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}^{(\varepsilon)}(\dd\!\phi)\;,\qquad\widetilde{V}(\phi):=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{1}{4}{\mathpunct{:}\phi^{4}(x)\mathpunct{:}}-{\mathpunct{:}\phi^{2}(x)\mathpunct{:}}+\frac{1}{4}\biggr]\dd\!x\;,

(3.4.2)

where
\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}^{(\varepsilon)}
is the measure of the Gaussian free field with
covariance
\varepsilon(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
(and the constant term
1/4
plays no role
but will be convenient in computations). Note that the integral over
\Lambda

has finite expectation and variance by
Proposition

3.2.14
.
However, this does not automatically imply that the partition function
{\mathscr{Z}}_{0}

is finite.

In the case of the
\Phi^{4}
model (without the linear term
\phi(t,x)

in (
3.4.1
), cf.
Remark

2.0.1
), this problem is in fact an old
problem in Quantum Field Theory, which has been solved by various methods. A
particularly elegant approach has recently been developed by Barashkov and
Gubinelli
[
BG18
]
. To adapt it to the nonconvex
potential of the Allen–Cahn equation, we start by performing, in a similar way
as in
Section

2.7
, cf. (
2.7.10
), the change of variables

\phi(x)=\frac{1}{L}\phi_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{\perp}(x)\;,

(3.4.3)

where
\phi_{\perp}
has zero mean. Using
Lemma

3.2.10
to
transform Wick powers, we obtain

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{V}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\biggl[V_{0}(\phi_{0})-\frac{1}{2}\phi_{0}^{2}\biggr]+\frac{1}{2}{\mathscr{Q}}_{\perp}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})+R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})\;,

(3.4.4)

where

\displaystyle V_{0}(\phi_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{L^{2}}{4}\biggl(\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}}{L^{2}}-1\biggr)^{2}\;,

(3.4.5)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{Q}}_{\perp}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{L^{2}}-2\biggr)\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\;,

(3.4.6)

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{L}\phi_{0}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{3}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\;.

(3.4.7)

This yields the expression

{\mathscr{Z}}_{0}={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}^{(\varepsilon)}}\bigl[\e^{-\widetilde{V}/\varepsilon}\bigr]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon}}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{-V_{0}(\phi_{0})/\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},\perp}}\Bigl[\e^{-{\mathscr{Q}}_{\perp}(\phi_{0},\cdot)/2-R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\Bigr]\dd\!\phi_{0}

(3.4.8)

for the partition function, where
\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},\perp}
has covariance

(-\Delta_{\perp}+1)^{-1}
.

Our aim is now to bound the expectation in (
3.4.8
). One way of doing
this, which was used in
[
BDGW17
]
, is based on the
so-called Nelson estimate, a refinement of
Proposition

3.2.14
. We outline
here instead the approach used in
[
BG18
]
, which relies on
the Boué–Dupuis formula. The idea is that if
\mu_{\perp}(a)
is a Gaussian
measure with covariance
(-\Delta_{\perp}+a)^{-1}
, it can be regularised in the
following way. Let
\rho:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a compactly supported smooth function
such that
\rho(0)=0
, and set, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

v_{k}(t):=\frac{\sigma_{k}(t)}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}^{2}+a}}\;,\qquad\sigma_{k}(t)^{2}:=\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!t}\rho\biggl(\frac{\lVert k\rVert}{t}\biggr)\;.

(3.4.9)

Then introduce the stochastic process

Y_{t}(x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}v_{k}(s)\dd\!W^{(k)}_{s}e_{k}(x)\;,

(3.4.10)

where the
W^{(k)}_{t}
are independent Wiener processes. Using Itô’s isometry,
we obtain

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle Y_{t},\varphi\rangle\langle Y_{s},\psi\rangle\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t\wedge s}v_{k}(u)^{2}\dd\!u\,\overline{\hat{\varphi}_{k}}\hat{\psi}_{k}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\rho\biggl(\frac{\lVert k\rVert}{t\wedge s}\biggr)\,\overline{\hat{\varphi}_{k}}\hat{\psi}_{k}

(3.4.11)

for any test functions
\varphi
and
\psi
with Fourier coefficients
\hat{\varphi}_{k}

and
\hat{\psi}_{k}
. In particular,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle Y_{t},\varphi\rangle\langle Y_{t},\psi\rangle\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}^{2}+a}\,\overline{\hat{\varphi}_{k}}\hat{\psi}_{k}=\langle\varphi,(-\Delta_{\perp}+a)^{-1}\psi\rangle\;.

(3.4.12)

Then the Boué–Dupuis variational formula, whose proof relies on the Girsanov
transformation
[
BG18
, Section 2]
, reads as follows.

Theorem 3.4.1

(Boué–Dupuis formula,
[
BD98
]
)
.

For any
T>0
and functional
{\mathscr{V}}
, we have

-\log\mathbb{E}\bigl[\e^{-{\mathscr{V}}(Y_{T})}\bigr]=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[{\mathscr{V}}(Y_{T}+Z_{T}(u))+\frac{\lvert\Lambda\rvert}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\dd\!t\Biggr]\;,

(3.4.13)

where

Z_{T}(u,x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}u_{k}(t)\dd\!t\,e_{k}(x)

(3.4.14)

and
\mathbb{H}
is the space of progressively measurable processes which are
almost surely in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Lambda)
.

Note that here the parameter
T
does not represent time, but a continuous
regularisation parameter such that in the limit
T\to\infty
, one recovers the
expectation under the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},\perp}
. This leads to the
following estimate, which extends
Proposition

2.7.5
to the two-dimensional
case (the bounds’
\varepsilon
-dependence can be improved, cf.
Proposition

3.6.3
).

Proposition 3.4.2
.

Assume that
L<2\pi
. Then there exists a constant
c
such that

1\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},\perp}}\Bigl[\e^{-{\mathscr{Q}}_{\perp}(\phi_{0},\cdot)/2-R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\Bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}\e^{c\phi_{0}^{4}}\;.

(3.4.15)

As a consequence, the partition function
{\mathscr{Z}}_{0}
is bounded.

Sketch of proof:
.

The lower bound follows from Jensen’s inequality and the fact that Wick
powers are centred. For the upper bound, we first perform a shift to the
Gaussian measure
\mu_{\perp}
of covariance
(-\Delta_{\perp}-1)^{-1}
(which is
allowed since the eigenvalues of
-\Delta_{\perp}
are greater than
1
for

L<2\pi
). One obtains

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},\perp}}\Bigl[\e^{-{\mathscr{Q}}_{\perp}(\phi_{0},\cdot)/2-R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\Bigr]=K{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\Bigl[\e^{-{\mathscr{V}}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\Bigr]\;,

(3.4.16)

where

{\mathscr{V}}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp}):=R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})+\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{2L^{2}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\;,

(3.4.17)

and
K
is a constant. This is actually a nontrivial point, to which we will
come back in
Section

3.6
, see
Lemma

3.6.1
.
Expanding the Wick powers of
Y_{T}+Z_{T}(u)
with the help of

Proposition

3.2.14
, and inserting this in (
3.4.13
), we get

-\log\mathbb{E}\bigl[\e^{-{\mathscr{V}}(\phi_{0},\cdot)}\bigr]=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[{}_{T}(u)+\Phi_{T}(u)\Bigr]\;,

(3.4.18)

where, since all Wick powers of
Y_{T}
have zero expectation,

\displaystyle{}_{T}(u)={}

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}Z_{T}^{4}(u)\dd\!x+\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{2L^{2}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}Z_{T}^{2}(u)\dd\!x+\frac{\lvert\Lambda\rvert}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\dd\!t\;,

(3.4.19)

\displaystyle\Phi_{T}(u)={}

\displaystyle\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}Y_{T}^{3}\mathpunct{:}}Z_{T}(u)\dd\!x+\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}Y_{T}^{2}\mathpunct{:}}Z_{T}^{2}(u)\dd\!x+\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}Y_{T}Z_{T}^{3}(u)\dd\!x

(3.4.20)

\displaystyle{}+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{L}\phi_{0}\Biggl[3\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}Y_{T}^{2}\mathpunct{:}}Z_{T}(u)\dd\!x+3\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}Y_{T}Z_{T}^{2}(u)\dd\!x+\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}Z_{T}^{3}(u)\dd\!x\Biggr]+\frac{3\phi_{0}^{2}}{2L^{2}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}Y_{T}Z_{T}(u)\dd\!x\;.

(3.4.21)

Here one has to show that for each choice of
u\in\mathbb{H}
, the positive
term

{}_{T}(u)
dominates
\Phi_{T}(u)
uniformly in
T
up to a remainder of order

\phi_{0}^{4}
. This is a purely deterministic argument, which is based on
functional inequalities, cf.
[
BG18
, Section 3]
. The
claim on
{\mathscr{Z}}_{0}
then follows from Laplace asymptotics on (
3.4.8
).
∎

The existence of the Gibbs measure
\pi
being established, it is natural to ask
whether
\pi
is indeed invariant under the dynamics of the Allen–Cahn
equation (
3.4.1
), whether it is the unique invariant measure, and
what are its ergodic properties. This task is not straightforward at all, since
even the fact that solutions of (
3.4.1
) enjoy the Markov property
is not automatic, but requires a proof. All these properties have in fact
been established recently. We summarise them in the following theorem, which
incorporates results
from
[
RZZ17b
,
TW18b
,
RZZ17a
,
HM18
]
.

Theorem 3.4.3

(Ergodic properties of the two-dimensional Allen–Cahn equation)
.

The solution of (
3.4.1
) defines a Markov process

(\phi_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
with transition semi-group
(P_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
with respect
to the filtration
({\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
generated by all random variables

\langle\xi,\varphi\rangle
obtained by testing space-time white noise against functions

\varphi
supported on
[0,t]\times\Lambda
. The semigroup
(P_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}

satisfies the
strong Feller property
, that is, it maps bounded
measurable functions to continuous functions for every
t>0
.
The Gibbs measure
\pi(\dd\!\phi)
is the unique invariant measure of the
dynamics, and is reversible. Finally, the dynamics is exponentially mixing, in
the sense that there exist constants
C,\beta>0
such that

\lVert P_{t}(\phi_{0})-\pi\rVert_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm TV$}}}\mathbin{\leqslant}C\e^{-\beta t}\lVert\delta_{\phi_{0}}-\pi\rVert_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm TV$}}}

(3.4.22)

holds for all
\phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{-\kappa}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda)
and all
t>0
, where

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm TV$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm TV$}}}
denotes the total variation distance.

3.5
Large deviations

Consider the Allen–Cahn equation on the torus
\Lambda
with weak mollified
space-time white noise

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\phi(t,x)-\Bigl(\phi(t,x)^{3}-3\varepsilon\overline{C}_{\delta}\phi(t,x)\Bigr)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;,

(3.5.1)

where
\overline{C}_{\delta}=2C_{\delta}
. Note that we have now two small parameter

\delta
and
\varepsilon
, and that the counterterm
3\varepsilon\overline{C}_{\delta}\phi
has
been scaled accordingly (recall from (
3.2.59
) that

\overline{C}_{\delta}
diverges logarithmically). For fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we know
that this equation admits a well-defined limit as
\delta\searrow 0
. When

\delta,\varepsilon>0
, (
3.5.1
) is a well-posed PDE for any realisation
of the noise.
In
[
HW15
]
, Hairer and Weber have established the
following large-deviation principle.

Theorem 3.5.1

(Large-deviation principle for the renormalised equation)
.

Let
\phi_{\delta,\varepsilon}
denote the solution of (
3.5.1
), and
let
\varepsilon\mapsto\delta(\varepsilon)\mathbin{\geqslant}0
be a function such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\delta(\varepsilon)=0\;.

(3.5.2)

Then the family
(\phi_{\delta(\varepsilon),\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
with fixed initial
condition
\phi_{0}
satisfies an LDP on
[0,T]
with good rate function

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\gamma(t,x)-\Delta\gamma(t,x)-\gamma(t,x)+\gamma(t,x)^{3}\biggr]^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t&\text{if the integral is finite\;, }\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(3.5.3)

The remarkable thing about this result is that the counterterm

3\varepsilon\overline{C}_{\delta}\phi
does not appear in the rate function, which
suggests that the Wick power consisting of cubic term and counterterm is the
object that should really be considered physically relevant.

On the other hand, for
\delta>0
, we may also consider the variant
of (
3.5.1
) without
counterterm

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+C\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;,

(3.5.4)

which does not admit a limit as
\delta\searrow 0
.
Here, one can also obtain a large-deviation result, which reads as follows.

Theorem 3.5.2

(Large-deviation principle for equation without renormalisation)
.

Let
\tilde{\phi}_{\delta,\varepsilon}
denote the solution of (
3.5.4
), and
let
\varepsilon\mapsto\delta(\varepsilon)\mathbin{\geqslant}0
be a function
satisfying (
3.5.2
) as well as

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\log\Bigl(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1}\Bigr)=\lambda\in[0,\infty)\;.

(3.5.5)

Then the family
(\tilde{\phi}_{\delta(\varepsilon),\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
with fixed initial
condition
\phi_{0}
satisfies an LDP on
[0,T]
with good rate function

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\gamma(t,x)-\Delta\gamma(t,x)+C^{(\lambda)}\gamma(t,x)+\gamma(t,x)^{3}\biggr]^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t&\text{if the integral is finite\;, }\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise\;,}\end{cases}

(3.5.6)

where
C^{(\lambda)}=C-3\lambda^{2}/(4\pi)
.

Note that by the asymptotics (
3.2.59
) of
C_{\delta}
, the results
of both theorems indeed overlap if
C
is such that
C_{\lambda}=0
.

3.6
Metastability

We consider now the long-time dynamics of the renormalised
equation (
3.5.1
) for small positive
\varepsilon
, again for simplicity
in the case
L<2\pi
, when the only stationary solutions of the deterministic
equation have constant value
0
or
\pm 1
. As before, we denote these
stationary solutions
\phi^{*}_{0}
and
\phi^{*}_{\pm}
. Note that at first glance, one
might think that the stable stationary solutions of (
3.5.1
) are
located in
\pm[1+3\varepsilon\smash{\overline{C}_{\delta}}]^{1/2}
instead of
\pm 1
.
However, the large-deviation estimate (
3.5.3
) suggests otherwise,
and we shall see that indeed the system behaves in some sense as if the
counterterm were absent.

As in the one-dimensional case, we have no good control on the

\varepsilon
-dependence of the constant
\beta
controlling the speed of convergence
in the exponential mixing result (
3.4.22
). A natural approach
to characterise this speed is thus to establish asymptotic results on the
expected transition time
\tau_{+}
from
\phi^{*}_{-}
to
\phi^{*}_{+}
. The LDP given
in
Theorem

3.5.1
can be used to show that, just as in the one-dimensional
case, this time satisfies the Arrhenius law

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\log{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi^{*}_{-}}\bigl[\tau_{+}\bigr]=H\;,

(3.6.1)

where for
L<2\pi
, one has
H=L^{2}/4
. Note that the fact that the rate
function (
3.5.3
) does not depend on
C_{\delta}
is crucial here.

The next step is to derive Eyring–Kramers asymptotics for

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi^{*}_{-}}\bigl[\tau_{+}\bigr]
. One may indeed wonder whether
there exists an analogue to
Theorem

2.7.7
on the one-dimensional
Allen–Cahn equation. Here, however, we note that the Fredholm determinant

\det\Bigl((-\Delta-1)(-\Delta+2)^{-1}\Bigr)=\det\Bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}-3(-\Delta+2)^{-1}\Bigr)\;.

(3.6.2)

that would appear in the prefactor is
not
well-defined, since

(-\Delta+2)^{-1}
is not trace class in two dimensions. But one should not
forget that the Eyring–Kramers formula also involves a potential difference.
If the potential
V
is defined by

V(\phi)=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{4}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}+\frac{1}{4}\biggr]\dd\!x\;,

(3.6.3)

one can check that

V(\phi^{*}_{0})-V(\phi^{*}_{-})=\frac{L^{2}}{4}+\frac{3}{2}L^{2}\varepsilon\overline{C}_{\delta}\;.

(3.6.4)

It turns out that the extra term in
\overline{C}_{\delta}
exactly compensates
the divergence of the Fredholm determinant, replacing it by a so-called

Carleman–Fredholm
(renormalised) determinant.

To see this, we will work with the spectral Galerkin approximation of the
SPDE given by

\partial_{t}\phi_{N}(t,x)=\Delta\phi_{N}(t,x)+\phi_{N}(t,x)-\Pi_{N}\phi_{N}(t,x)^{3}+3\varepsilon C_{N}\phi_{N}(t,x)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\Pi_{N}\xi(t,x)\;,

(3.6.5)

where
\Pi_{N}
is the projection on Fourier modes with wave vector
k
satisfying

\lvert k\rvert=\lvert k_{1}\rvert+\lvert k_{2}\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N
. As before, we denote by
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}
the
set of these
k
. In order to keep track of the choice of the order-one part of
the renormalisation constant, which is in principle arbitrary, it will be
convenient to choose
C_{N}
such that

L^{2}C_{N}=\Tr\Bigl(\lvert-\Delta_{N}-1\rvert^{-1}\Bigr)+\theta=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lvert\lambda_{k}-1\rvert}+\theta=L^{2}\frac{\log N}{2\pi}+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\;,

(3.6.6)

where
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
is a free parameter. The renormalised potential is then
given by (
3.6.3
) where Wick powers are with respect to
\varepsilon C_{N}
. Using the decomposition (
3.4.3
) of
\phi
into its
mean and oscillatory parts, we obtain

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\phi)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V_{0}(\phi_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi_{\perp},Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})\phi_{\perp}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(1-m^{2}(\phi_{0})\Bigr)L^{2}C_{N}+R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})\;,

(3.6.7)

where
V_{0}
and
R_{\varepsilon}
are as in (
3.4.7
),
Q_{\perp}(\phi_{0})=-\Delta_{\perp}-1+m^{2}(\phi_{0})
and
m^{2}(\phi_{0})=3\phi_{0}^{2}/L^{2}
. Note that
compared to the one-dimensional case,
cf. (
2.7.11
), there is an additional term
proportional to
C_{N}
, which is due to our using Wick powers in the potential.

The key observation here is the following explicit expression for a change of
mass in a Gaussian measure.

Lemma 3.6.1
.

For
a>0
, let
\mu(a)
be the Gaussian measure with covariance

(-\Delta_{N}+a)^{-1}
. Then for any
\mu(a)
-integrable random variable

F(\phi_{N})
and any
b>0
, one has

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu(a)}\bigl[F(\phi_{N})\bigr]=\sqrt{\mathfrak{D}_{N}(a-b;b)}\,{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu(b)}\biggl[\exp\biggl\{-\frac{a-b}{2}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr\}F(\phi_{N})\biggr]\;,

(3.6.8)

where the Wick power is with respect to
\Tr(-\Delta+b)^{-1}/L^{2}
, and for
any
b\notin\spec(\Delta_{N})
, we define

\mathfrak{D}_{N}(c;b)=\det{}_{2}\Bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}+c(-\Delta_{N}+b)^{-1}\Bigr):=\det\Bigl({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}+c(-\Delta_{N}+b)^{-1}\Bigr)\e^{-c\Tr(-\Delta_{N}+b)^{-1}}\;.

(3.6.9)

The modified determinant
\det{}_{2}({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}+{\mathscr{L}})=\det({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}+{\mathscr{L}})\e^{-\Tr{\mathscr{L}}}

of a linear operator
{\mathscr{L}}
, as appearing in (
3.6.9
), is
called its
Carleman–Fredholm determinant
, and is convergent whenever

{\mathscr{L}}
is
Hilbert–Schmidt
, meaning that only
{\mathscr{L}}^{\dagger}{\mathscr{L}}
needs to
be trace class, see
[
Sim05
, Chapter 5]
.

Exercise 3.6.2
.

Use a Fourier basis
\{e_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}
in order to

•

show that there is a constant
M
uniform in
N
,
b
and
c
such
that
\e^{-Mc^{2}/b^{2}}\mathbin{\leqslant}\mathfrak{D}_{N}(c;b)\mathbin{\leqslant}\e^{Mc^{2}/b^{2}}
;

•

show that
\mathfrak{D}_{N}(a-b;b)^{-1}=\mathfrak{D}_{N}(b-a;a)\exp\Bigl\{(a-b)^{2}\Tr[(-\Delta+a)^{-1}(-\Delta+b)^{-1}]\Bigr\}
;

•

prove
Lemma

3.6.1
, using Parseval’s identity.

As in the one-dimensional case, the Galerkin
approximation (
3.6.5
) is equivalent to a finite-dimensional
Itô SDE

\dd\!\hat{\phi}_{t}=-\nabla\widehat{V}_{N}(\hat{\phi}_{t})\dd\!t+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dd\!W_{t}\;,

(3.6.10)

with a potential
\widehat{V}_{N}
depending now explicitly on
C_{N}
. We can thus
apply as before the potential-theoretic approach. To be able to apply the
symmetry argument of
Lemma

1.5.12
, we choose again symmetric sets

A=-B=\bigl\{y\colon\lvert\phi_{0}+L\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}\delta,\phi_{\perp}\in D_{\perp}\bigr\},

(3.6.11)

with
D_{\perp}
containing this time a ball of radius
\sqrt{\log(\varepsilon^{-1})}

in
H^{s}(\Lambda)
for some
s<0
. Then we have the following
result
[
BDGW17
]
.

Proposition 3.6.3
.

The partition function and capacity satisfy

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{N}}{2\capacity(A,B)}=\frac{2\pi}{\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert}\frac{\e^{-3\theta/2}}{\sqrt{\lvert\mathfrak{D}_{N}(3;-1))\rvert}}\e^{V_{0}(0)/\varepsilon}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;,

(3.6.12)

where
\nu_{0}=2
,
\mu_{0}=-1
, and the
error term is uniform in the cut-off
N
.

Sketch of proof:
.

We write
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}={\mathscr{K}}_{N}\setminus\{0\}
, and define
\mathfrak{D}_{N}^{\perp}(c;b)
as
in (
3.6.9
), with
\Delta_{N}
replaced by

\Delta_{\perp,N}
. Interpreting as before the integral over
\phi_{\perp}
as an
expectation under the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\perp}(-1+m^{2}(\phi_{0}))
, which is
well-defined since the smallest eigenvalue of
\Delta_{\perp,N}
is

(2\pi/L)^{2}
, we find for the partition function

\displaystyle{\mathscr{Z}}_{N}

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{E_{N}}\e^{-V(\phi)/\varepsilon}\dd\!\phi

(3.6.13)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{-V_{0}(\phi_{0})/\varepsilon}\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\varepsilon}{\lambda_{k}-1+m^{2}(\phi_{0})}}\e^{(1-m^{2}(\phi_{0}))L^{2}C_{N}/2}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}(-1+m^{2}(\phi_{0}))}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]\dd\!\phi_{0}

(3.6.14)

\displaystyle=\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\varepsilon}{\lambda_{k}-1}}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{-V_{0}(\phi_{0})/\varepsilon}\sqrt{\frac{\e^{L^{2}C_{N}}\e^{-m^{2}(\phi_{0})(\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert^{-1}+\theta)}}{\mathfrak{D}_{N}^{\perp}(m^{2}(\phi_{0});-1)}}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}(-1+m^{2}(\phi_{0}))}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]\dd\!\phi_{0}\;.

(3.6.15)

Proceeding as in
Proposition

2.7.5
, one can show that

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}(-1+m^{2}(\phi_{0}))}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]=1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon\e^{\phi_{0}^{4}})
. As in that proposition, the argument actually
requires Gaussian changes of mass, using here
Lemma

3.6.1
, to
obtain the required estimate,
cf.
[
BDGW17
, Proposition 5.7]
. When performing the
integral over
\phi_{0}
, we note that
-V_{0}(\phi_{0})
is maximal at
\phi_{0}=\pm L^{2}
, where
m^{2}(\phi_{0})=3
, so that by Laplace asymptotics we obtain

{\mathscr{Z}}_{N}=2\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\varepsilon\e^{L^{2}C_{N}-3(\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert^{-1}+\theta)}}{\nu_{0}\mathfrak{D}_{N}^{\perp}(3;-1)}}\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\varepsilon}{\lambda_{k}-1}}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;.

(3.6.16)

For the capacity, using the Dirichlet and Thomson principles, we find in a
similar way

\capacity(A,B)=\frac{\varepsilon}{c_{0}^{2}}\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\varepsilon}{\lambda_{k}-1}}\intop\nolimits_{-a}^{a}\e^{V_{0}(\phi_{0})/\varepsilon}\sqrt{\frac{\e^{L^{2}C_{N}}\e^{-m^{2}(\phi_{0})(\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert^{-1}+\theta)}}{\mathfrak{D}_{N}^{\perp}(m^{2}(\phi_{0});-1)}}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}(-1+m^{2}(\phi_{0}))}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]\dd\!\phi_{0}\;,

(3.6.17)

where
c_{0}
is as in (
2.7.33
). This time, the integral over
\phi_{0}

is dominated by
\phi_{0}
near
0
, where
m^{2}=0
, and the result is

\capacity(A,B)=\frac{\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{\e^{L^{2}C_{N}}}{\mathfrak{D}_{N}^{\perp}(0;-1)}}\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\varepsilon}{\lambda_{k}-1}}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;.

(3.6.18)

The result follows, using the facts that
c_{0}=\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert^{-1}}\e^{V_{0}(0)/\varepsilon}[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)]
, that

\mathfrak{D}_{N}^{\perp}(0;-1)=1
, and that
\nu_{0}\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert^{-1}\mathfrak{D}_{N}^{\perp}(3;-1)=\e^{-3\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert^{-1}}\lvert\mathfrak{D}_{N}(3;-1)\rvert
.
∎

To turn
Proposition

3.6.3
into a limiting result, one needs to perform
some post-processing, as discussed in the proof of
Theorem

2.7.7
. Namely, one
has to show that the expected transition times of the spectral Galerkin
approximation converge to the one of the limiting system, and derive a coupling
result to get rid of the equilibrium distribution on
\partial A
. This has been
achieved in
[
TW18a
]
, with the following result.

Theorem 3.6.4

(Eyring-Kramers law for the two-dimensional Allen–Cahn SPDE)
.

Assume that
L<2\pi
and
A
and
B
are as in (
3.6.11
). Then, for
the choice (
3.6.6
) of regularisation
C_{N}
, one has

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\phi^{*}_{-}}\bigl[\tau_{B}\bigr]=\frac{2\pi}{\lvert\mu_{0}\rvert}\frac{\e^{-3\theta/2}\e^{L^{2}/(4\varepsilon)}}{\sqrt{\bigl|\det_{2}[{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}+3(-\Delta-1)^{-1}]\bigr|}}\bigl[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)\bigr]\;,

(3.6.19)

where the determinant is to be interpreted as the Carleman–Fredholm
determinant (
3.6.9
).

3.7
Bibliographical notes

Besides the system of coupled diffusions, the Allen–Cahn SPDE also describes
particular scaling limits of Ising–Kac models, in which each spin interacts
with a large number of other spins
[
MW17a
]
.

Background on Wick calculus can be found
in
[
Nua06
, Chapter 1]
. The graphical notation for Wick
powers has been introduced in
[
Hai14
]
, and we adopted the version used
in
[
CW17
]
for their regularisation.

Construction of solutions to stochastic quantisation equations was investigated
in
[
AR91
]
, using the theory of Dirichlet forms. Existence
and uniqueness of strong solutions to stochastic quantisation equations on the
two-dimensional torus, as well as the absence of explosion, was first proved
in
[
DPD03
]
. The result was extended to the whole plane
in
[
MW17b
]
.

Connections between the Gibbs measure and the stochastic quantisation equation
were investigated in
[
JLM85
]
. The idea to use the
Boué–Dupuis formula to bound the partition function was introduced
in
[
BG18
]
. The fact that solutions to stochastic
quantisation equations satisfy the Markov property and are reversible with
respect to the Gibbs measure was proved in
[
RZZ17b
]
using
Dirichlet forms, while uniqueness of the Gibbs measure and convergence to it
were obtained in
[
RZZ17a
]
. The strong Feller property, as well
as the Markov property and exponential mixing were proved
in
[
TW18b
]
using a dissipative bound, while the strong Feller
property was also proved (for more general equations)
in
[
HM18
]
, using the theory of regularity structures.

The large-deviation results are from
[
HW15
]
. The
uniform-in-
N
Eyring–Kramers asymptotics on expected transition times were
obtained in
[
BDGW17
]
, and extended to a full proof of
the Eyring–Kramers formula in
[
TW18a
]
.

Chapter 4
Allen–Cahn SPDE in three space dimensions

We finally turn to the analysis of the three-dimensional Allen–Cahn equation,
whose non-renormalised version is formally written as

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi(t,x)\;,

(4.0.1)

where
x
now belongs to the torus
\Lambda=(\mathbb{R}/(L\mathbb{Z}))^{3}
. The deterministic PDE
obtained for
\varepsilon=0
is again well-defined. However, we will see that the
stochastic PDE remains ill-posed even when the nonlinear term
\phi(t,x)^{3}
is
replaced by its Wick power
{\mathpunct{:}\phi(t,x)^{3}\mathpunct{:}}
. This is a consequence of
space-time white noise being even more singular in three than in two space
dimensions.

There exist by now several methods to make sense of the singular
equation (
4.0.1
) after proper renormalisation, namely
regularity
structures

[
Hai14
]
,
paracontrolled
calculus

[
GIP15
,
CC18
]
and a
Wilsonian renormalisation group approach
[
Kup16
]
. In this chapter,
we will describe the approach based on regularity structures. First, however,
we need to understand the reason for the ill-posedness in more detail.

4.1
Failure of the previous approaches

The definition of space-time white noise
\xi
in one time and three space
dimensions proceeds as before, with some straightforward adjustments. In
particular,
\xi
has now the scaling behaviour

\xi_{\tau,\lambda}\eqinlaw\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau\lambda^{3}}}\xi\;.

(4.1.1)

With the parabolic norm

\lVert(t,x)-(s,y)\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}=\lvert t-s\rvert^{1/2}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{3}\lvert x_{i}-y_{i}\rvert\;,

(4.1.2)

and the scaling operator

({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{t,x}\varphi)(s,y):=\frac{1}{\lambda^{5}}\varphi\biggl(\frac{s-t}{\lambda^{2}},\frac{y-x}{\lambda}\biggr)\;,

(4.1.3)

Theorem

2.2.8
becomes

\xi\in{\mathscr{C}}^{-5/2-\kappa}_{\mathfrak{s}}\qquad\text{for any $\kappa>0$\;.}

(4.1.4)

The Schauder estimate stated in
Theorem

2.3.9
remains true, showing that
the stochastic convolution
P*\xi
now belongs to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
for

\alpha<-\frac{1}{2}
only. It is thus much more singular than in dimension two,
where
it was very close to being a function.

In analogy with what we did in dimension two, it seems natural
to consider the regularised equation (for
2\varepsilon=1
)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi_{\delta}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\Delta\phi_{\delta}(t,x)+\phi_{\delta}(t,x)-{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\delta}(t,x)^{3}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{\delta}}+\xi^{\delta}(t,x)

(4.1.5)

\displaystyle=\Delta\phi_{\delta}(t,x)+\phi_{\delta}(t,x)-\Bigl(\phi_{\delta}(t,x)^{3}-3C_{\delta}\phi_{\delta}(t,x)\Bigr)+\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;,

(4.1.6)

where
\xi^{\delta}
denotes a mollified noise defined by

\xi^{\delta}(t,x)=(\varrho_{\delta}*\xi)(t,x)\;,\qquad\varrho_{\delta}(t,x)=\frac{1}{\delta^{5}}\varrho\biggl(\frac{t}{\delta^{2}},\frac{x}{\delta}\biggr)

(4.1.7)

for a compactly supported smooth function
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
of
integral
1
. The renormalisation constant
C_{\delta}
defining the Wick power
in (
4.1.6
) is given as in two dimensions by

C_{\delta}:=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}P_{\delta}(t,x)^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t\;,

(4.1.8)

where
P_{\delta}=P*\varrho_{\delta}
, except that
P
is now the three-dimensional
heat kernel

P(t,x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}P_{\,\mathbb{R}^{3}}(t,x-kL)\;,\qquad P_{\,\mathbb{R}^{3}}(t,x):=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{3/2}}\e^{-\lVert x\rVert^{2}/(4t)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>0\}}\;.

(4.1.9)

Exercise 4.1.1
.

Use the fact that the Green function of the three-dimensional Laplacian is
the periodicised version of

G_{\,\mathbb{R}^{3}}(x)=-\frac{1}{4\pi\lVert x\rVert}

(4.1.10)

to show that
C_{\delta}
diverges like
\delta^{-1}
as
\delta\to 0
.

Recall
Proposition

3.2.16
.

(4.1.11)

The term with the worst regularity on the right-hand side should be

P*\,\hbox to9.79pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.8945pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

(4.1.14)

The problem now is that the product

is not well-defined, since the
regularity of the two factors is slightly less than
-1
and
\frac{1}{2}

respectively.
This is a first indication that a second renormalisation constant will be
needed. It further suggests that one has to rethink the way solutions are
represented as linear combinations of iterated stochastic integrals.

4.2
Perturbation theory for the Gibbs measure

One way to understand the need for a further renormalisation constant is to
consider, as in
Section

3.6
, the Wick-renormalised potential

V(\phi):=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{4}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}+\frac{1}{4}\biggr]\dd\!x\;,

(4.2.1)

where we use a spectral Galerkin approximation of order
N
for
\phi
, and
Wick powers are with respect to
\varepsilon C_{N}
. Here we choose

L^{2}C_{N}=\Tr\Bigl(\bigl(-\Delta_{\perp,N}-1\bigr)^{-1}\Bigr)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}-1}={\mathcal{O}}(N)\;,

(4.2.2)

where, like before,
\lambda_{k}=(2\lVert k\rVert\pi/L)^{2}
and
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\colon 0<\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N\}
with
\lvert k\rvert=\lvert k_{1}\rvert+\lvert k_{2}\rvert+\lvert k_{3}\rvert
.

To lighten the notation, we assume from now on that
L=1
.
As we have seen, e.g., in the proof of
Proposition

3.6.3
, showing that
the potential (
4.2.1
) is well-defined involves computing
the expectation of
\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}
under the Gaussian measure

\mu_{\perp}=\mu_{\perp}(\phi_{0})
having covariance

(-\Delta_{\perp,N}-1+3\phi_{0}^{2})^{-1}
, where

R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp}):=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{0}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{3}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\;.

(4.2.3)

We will represent this graphically as

R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\phi_{0}\raisebox{-5.5pt}{

(4.2.4)

We can compute
{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]
perturbatively in

\varepsilon
by using the
cumulant expansion

\displaystyle-\log{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[\e^{-R_{\varepsilon}}\bigr]={}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}\bigr]

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle{}+\frac{1}{2!}\Bigl({\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}\bigr]^{2}-{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigr]\Bigr)

(4.2.6)

\displaystyle{}+\frac{1}{3!}\Bigl(2{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}\bigr]^{3}-3{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}\bigr]{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigr]+{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[R_{\varepsilon}^{3}\bigr]\Bigr)

(4.2.7)

\displaystyle{}+\dots

(4.2.8)

In order to compute these expectations, we recall
(see (
2.5.5
)) that
1
1

1

We are going to be slightly sloppy
here, by ignoring the fact that the covariance of the Gaussian measure

\mu_{\perp}
depends on
\phi_{0}
, which affects
G_{N}
and
C_{N}
. However, this
results only in negligible extra terms, essentially thanks to

Lemma

3.6.1
.

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[\phi_{\perp}(x)\phi_{\perp}(y)\bigr]=G_{N}(x-y)\;,

(4.2.9)

where
G_{N}
is the Green function of
\Delta_{\perp,N}-1
.
In a Fourier basis
\{e_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}}
, it is given by

G_{N}(x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}^{*}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}-1}e_{k}(x)

(4.2.10)

(note that we recover the fact that
G_{N}(0)=C_{N}
).

Consider first the case
\phi_{0}=0
, when only the quartic term is present in

R_{\varepsilon}
. We know that the fourth Wick power has zero expectation, while
according to
Lemma

3.2.9
, its variance is given by

\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{\perp}}\bigl[{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\perp}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\perp}(y)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\bigr]\dd\!x\dd\!y=4!\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}G_{N}(x)^{4}\dd\!x

(4.2.11)

(we have used translation invariance of the Green function and the fact that

L=1
). We denote this graphically (cf. (
3.2.29

}:=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}G_{N}(x)^{2}G_{N}(y)^{2}G_{N}(x-y)^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!y\;.

(4.2.14)

The graphs occurring in (
4.2.12
) and (
4.2.14
) are special
cases of Feynman diagrams, called
vacuum diagrams
.

Exercise 4.2.1
.

Prove the bounds

\displaystyle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}G_{N}(x)^{n}\dd\!x\lesssim\begin{cases}1&\text{if $n=2$\;,}\\
\log N&\text{if $n=3$\;,}\\
N&\text{if $n=4$\;,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}G_{N}(x)^{2}G_{N}(y)^{2}G_{N}(x-y)^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!y\lesssim\log(N)\;.

Hint:
One may use the Young-type inequality

\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\\
k_{1}+k_{2}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lVert k_{1}\rVert^{n}\lVert k_{2}\rVert^{m}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lVert k\rVert^{n+m-d}}

(4.2.15)

valid whenever
0<n,m<d
and
n+m>d
, see for
instance
[
ZZ15a
, Lemma 3.10]
.

Remark 4.2.2
.

Equivalent estimates hold for the Green function
G_{\delta}=G*\varrho_{\delta}
mollified on scale
\delta=1/N
, where
\varrho_{\delta}(x)=\delta^{-3}\varrho(\delta^{-1}x)
. In particular, we have
G_{\delta}(x)={\mathcal{O}}((\lVert x\rVert+\delta)^{-1})
, see for
instance
[
Hai14
, Lemma 10.17]
.

Inserting (
4.2.12
) and (
4.2.13
) in the cumulant
expansion (
4.2.8
), we obtain the asymptotic expansion

}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{4})\;,

(4.2.16)

where the two vacuum diagrams diverge, respectively, like
N
and like

\log N
.
This suggests that for
\phi_{0}=0
, the potential (
4.2.1
) can
be renormalised by subtracting two terms of respective order
\varepsilon^{3}N
and

\varepsilon^{4}\log(N)
, called
energy renormalisation
terms. Of
course, at this point we can tell nothing about the
N
-dependence
of the higher-order terms in
\varepsilon
.

If
\phi_{0}\neq 0
, the variance of the third Wick power in
R_{\varepsilon}
will
add a new divergent term of order
\varepsilon\phi_{0}^{3}\log N
to the cumulant
expansion. This is a symptom of the fact that the Gibbs measures obtained for
different values of
\phi_{0}
are not absolutely continuous with respect to one
another. We cannot merely subtract this
\phi_{0}
-dependent term from the
potential, as this would not result from a decomposition into mean and
oscillating part. However, we can modify the potential (
4.2.1
) by
adding a renormalisation term to the coefficient of the second Wick power, that
is,

V(\phi)=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{4}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}-\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(1-\varepsilon^{2}C_{N}^{(2)}\Bigr){\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}+\frac{1}{4}\biggr]\dd\!x\;.

(4.2.17)

This procedure, called
mass renormalisation
, results in the expression

R_{\varepsilon}(\phi_{0},\phi_{\perp})=\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon C_{N}^{(2)}\phi_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}C_{N}^{(2)}\raisebox{-5.5pt}{
\hbox to28.45pt{\vbox to17.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.22638pt\lower-8.5359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.94 0 C 2.94 1.62 1.62 2.94 0 2.94 C -1.62 2.94 -2.94 1.62 -2.94 0 C -2.94 -1.62 -1.62 -2.94 0 -2.94 C 1.62 -2.94 2.94 -1.62 2.94 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}
{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.21 0 L -19.69 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}
{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.21 0 L 19.69 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(4.2.22)

allows to kill the divergent term of order
\varepsilon
. Pushing the expansion one
step further, one can show that the divergent part of the term of order

\varepsilon^{3}
is the same as in (
4.2.16
).

What this purely formal computation suggests, is that by choosing the
renormalised potential of the form

V(\phi)=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{4}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}_{\varepsilon C_{N}^{(1)}}-\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(1-\varepsilon^{2}C_{N}^{(2)}\Bigr){\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}_{\varepsilon C_{N}^{(1)}}+\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon^{3}C_{N}^{(3)}-\varepsilon^{4}C_{N}^{(4)}\biggr]\dd\!x\;,

(4.2.23)

the limit
N\to\infty
may be well-defined, provided we choose the counterterms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.83 -3.1 C 3.84 -14.34 27.65 -14.34 30.66 -3.1}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}={\mathcal{O}}(N)\;,

(4.2.26)

\displaystyle C_{N}^{(4)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{2^{3}}{3!4^{3}}\binom{4}{2}^{3}\raisebox{-10.5pt}{
\hbox to28.45pt{\vbox to27.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84544pt\lower-12.80365pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}={\mathcal{O}}(\log N)\;.

(4.2.27)

Of course, this perturbative computation up to order
\varepsilon^{3}
does not imply
that no other counterterms of higher order in
\varepsilon
are needed. The fact that
this is not the case is a highly nontrivial fact, which makes the static

\Phi^{4}_{3}
model one of the important solved cases of (Euclidean) Quantum Field
Theory. See
Section

4.8
for some historical notes. A recent proof, which
is relatively compact, of the well-posedness of the Gibbs measure is provided
in
[
BG18
]
.

4.3
Regularity structures: introduction

4.3.1
Hölder spaces revisited

One of the key ideas of the theory of regularity structures is to replace
expansions as appearing in the fixed-point equation (
4.1.11
) by
more general expansions, which separate the roles of the coefficients, such as

\psi(t,x)
, and of the singular distributions, such as
. These objects
can be viewed as a kind of generalised Taylor expansion.

Consider for simplicity the case of a function
f_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2+\alpha}(I)
, where

I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. One way of defining

{\mathscr{C}}^{2+\alpha}(I)
is to require that
f_{0}
is twice continuously
differentiable, with a second derivative in
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(I)
,
cf.
2.3.8
. An alternative definition, which is more in
line with
Definition

2.3.7
, is to require the existence of a triple

(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2})
of functions from
I
to
\mathbb{R}
satisfying for all
x,y\in I
the
bounds on Taylor expansions

\displaystyle\bigl|f_{0}(y)-f_{0}(x)-(y-x)f_{1}(x)-\tfrac{1}{2}(y-x)^{2}f_{2}(x)\bigr|

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}C\lvert x-y\rvert^{2+\alpha}\;,

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle\bigl|f_{1}(y)-f_{1}(x)-(y-x)f_{2}(x)\bigr|

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}C\lvert x-y\rvert^{1+\alpha}\;,

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle\bigl|f_{2}(y)-f_{2}(x))\bigr|

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}C\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha}\;.

(4.3.3)

Note that this definition, while it seems redundant, completely avoids the use
of derivatives, though of course we necessarily have
f_{1}(x)=f_{0}^{\prime}(x)
and
f_{2}(x)=f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)
.

In the theory of regularity structures, one uses the abstract notation

f(x)=f_{0}(x)\mathbf{1}+f_{1}(x)\mathbf{X}+\tfrac{1}{2}f_{2}(x)\mathbf{X}^{2}\;,

(4.3.4)

where the symbols
\mathbf{1}
,
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{X}^{2}
are basis vectors of a vector space

T
of dimension
3
, called a
model space
, and
f:I\to T
is considered
as a map from
I
to
T
. We will denote the collection of basis vectors by

{\mathscr{F}}=\{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{2}\}
.

In order to be able to evaluate the abstract object (
4.3.4
) at a
particular point, we introduce the notion of
model
. This is given by a
collection of maps
\{\Pi_{x}\tau\colon x\in I,\tau\in{\mathscr{F}}\}
defined for all

z\in I
by

(\Pi_{x}\mathbf{1})(z):=1\;,\qquad(\Pi_{x}\mathbf{X})(z):=z-x\;,\qquad(\Pi_{x}\mathbf{X}^{2})(z):=(z-x)^{2}\;.

(4.3.5)

In this way, we have

\displaystyle(\Pi_{x}f(x))(z)

\displaystyle:=f_{0}(x)(\Pi_{x}\mathbf{1})(z)+f_{1}(x)(\Pi_{x}\mathbf{X})(z)+\tfrac{1}{2}f_{2}(x)(\Pi_{x}\mathbf{X}^{2})(z)

(4.3.6)

\displaystyle=f_{0}(x)+f_{1}(x)(z-x)+\tfrac{1}{2}f_{2}(x)(z-x)^{2}\;,

(4.3.7)

which is the second-order Taylor expansion of
f
at
x
. In particular, we
can recover
f_{0}
thanks to the
diagonal identity

(\Pi_{x}f(x))(x)=f_{0}(x)\;.

(4.3.8)

However,
f
contains more information than just the value of
f_{0}
at any
point
x
, since the higher-order terms of the Taylor expansion provide some
description of how
f
behaves in the vicinity of
x
.

Remark 4.3.1
.

An aspect of the theory that may be confusing at first is that it is
possible to define the quantity
(\Pi_{x}f(y))(z)
which depends on three
different arguments. This is not really of interest in the case of polynomial
models considered here, but will play a role later on.

It will also be important to encode how the model
\Pi_{x}
depends on the base
point
x
. For this purpose, note that if we define a collection

\{\Gamma_{xy}\colon x,y\in I\}
of linear maps from
T
to
T
by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{xy}\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{1}\;,

(4.3.9)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{xy}\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{X}+(x-y)\mathbf{1}\;,

(4.3.10)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{xy}\mathbf{X}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\bigl[\mathbf{X}+(x-y)\mathbf{1}\bigr]^{2}:=\mathbf{X}^{2}+2(x-y)\mathbf{X}+(x-y)^{2}\mathbf{1}\;,

(4.3.11)

then we have the relations

\displaystyle\Pi_{y}\tau

\displaystyle=\Pi_{x}\Gamma_{xy}\tau

\displaystyle\forall\tau\in{\mathscr{F}}\;,\forall x,y\in I\;,

(4.3.12)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{xy}\Gamma_{yz}\tau

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{xz}\tau

\displaystyle\forall\tau\in{\mathscr{F}}\;,\forall x,y,z\in I\;.

(4.3.13)

Exercise 4.3.2
.

Check that the relations (
4.3.13
) hold. What is the natural
generalisation of (
4.3.5
) and (
4.3.11
) to general
monomials
\mathbf{X}^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
? Check the analogue of (
4.3.13
) for
these general values of
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
as well.

The interest of this framework is that by combining (
4.3.4
)
and (
4.3.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle f(y)-\Gamma_{yx}f(x)={}

\displaystyle\bigl[f_{0}(y)-f_{0}(x)-(y-x)f_{1}-x)-\tfrac{1}{2}(y-x)^{2}f_{2}(x)\bigr]\mathbf{1}

(4.3.14)

\displaystyle{}+\bigl[f_{1}(y)-f_{1}(x)-(y-x)f_{2}(x)\bigr]\mathbf{X}

(4.3.15)

\displaystyle{}+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl[f_{2}(y)-f_{2}(x)\bigr]\mathbf{X}^{2}\;.

(4.3.16)

In view of (
4.3.3
), we see that if
{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}
denotes the
projection on the subspace of
T
spanned by
\mathbf{X}^{k}
, then we have

f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2+\alpha}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\bigl|{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}\bigl[f(y)-\Gamma_{yx}f(x)\bigr]\bigr|\lesssim\lvert x-y\rvert^{2+\alpha-k}\qquad\forall k\in\{0,1,2\}\;.

(4.3.17)

A central idea in the theory of regularity structures is that this
characterisation of regularity can be extended to situations in which the
monomials
\mathbf{X}^{k}
are complemented by more singular objects, such as the
stochastic convolution
and its powers.

4.3.2
Overview of the approach

Our primary aim will be to construct solutions for the regularised Allen–Cahn
SPDE on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Lambda
given by

\partial_{t}\phi_{\delta}(t,x)=\Delta\phi_{\delta}(t,x)+\phi_{\delta}(t,x)-\phi_{\delta}(t,x)^{3}+\bigl[3C_{\delta}^{(1)}-9C_{\delta}^{(2)}\bigr]\phi_{\delta}(t,x)+\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;,

(4.3.18)

where the renormalisation constants
\smash{C_{\delta}^{(1)}}={\mathcal{O}}(\delta^{-1})
and
\smash{C_{\delta}^{(2)}}={\mathcal{O}}(\log(\delta^{-1}))

are analogues of the first two constants in (
4.2.27
), while

\xi^{\delta}=\varrho_{\delta}*\xi
is a mollified version of space-time white
noise. Indeed, the right-hand side corresponds to the derivative of the
renormalised potential (
4.2.23
), which does not see the
constant terms.

The general idea of the method developed in
[
Hai14
]
can be described
by the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{0},Z^{\delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{0},\xi^{\delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}}

(4.3.19)

The maps appearing in this diagram are as follows:

•

The
classical solution map

\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
takes an initial
condition
\phi_{0}
and a realisation
\xi^{\delta}
of mollified space-time white
noise, and associates with them the solution
(\phi_{\delta}(t,x))_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
of
the smooth PDE (
4.3.18
). This solution exists globally in
time for any positive
\delta
and almost every realisation of the noise,
because the PDE is smooth with a confining nonlinearity.

•

The lift
\Psi
maps
\xi^{\delta}
to a so-called
canonical model

Z^{\delta}=(\Pi^{\delta},\Gamma^{\delta})
, which extends the collections of maps
introduced in (
4.3.5
) and (
4.3.11
) to the stochastic
convolution, its powers, and similar data. We will explain the construction of

Z^{\delta}
in
Section

4.5.1
.

•

The solution map
{\mathscr{S}}
associates with the initial data and the model
an element
\Phi
of an abstract space
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
of so-called
modelled
distributions
of regularity
\gamma
. This space can be thought of as an
abstract analogue of the Hölder space
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
on the level of
coefficients of Taylor series. We will describe it in

Section

4.5.2
. The element
\Phi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
is
obtained as the fixed point of a contracting map on
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
.

•

The
reconstruction operator

{\mathscr{R}}
takes a modelled distribution

\Phi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
and maps it to a distribution in a Hölder space

{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
. We will describe it in
Section

4.5.3
.

The point of the whole procedure is that we have the relation

\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}={\mathscr{R}}\circ{\mathscr{S}}\circ\Psi\;,

(4.3.20)

meaning that the fixed-point problems defining the classical solution

\phi_{\delta}
and the modelled distribution
\Phi
are equivalent for any

\delta>0
. In addition, both maps
{\mathscr{S}}
and
{\mathscr{R}}
are continuous in an
appropriate topology. We explain this part of the theory in more detail in

Section

4.5.4
.

Renormalisation comes into play when one modifies the lift
\Psi
. This will be
an extension of the transformation (
3.2.66
) from powers of
stochastic convolutions to their Wick powers, which removes divergencies. One
can then show that under appropriate conditions, the renormalised models

\widehat{Z}^{\delta}={}_{\delta}\xi^{\delta}
converge, as
\delta\to 0
, to some
limiting model
\widehat{Z}
. The image of this limit under the map
{\mathscr{R}}\circ{\mathscr{S}}

is then defined as the solution of the limiting stochastic PDE. We give more
details on this procedure in
Section

4.5.5
.

4.4
Regularity structures: algebraic aspects

At the core of the theory lies the notion of a
regularity structure
,
which is the abstract Banach space
T
spanned by the basis elements of
generalised Taylor expansions. This space is equipped with two additional
algebraic structures:

•

the
structure group

{\mathscr{G}}
, which allows to represent the link
between expansions around different base points;

•

a
renormalisation group
, which allows to encode the
renormalisation procedure.

We point out that while the model space is by definition infinite-dimensional,
in practice only a finite-dimensional subspace of
T
will matter. This is in
contrast with Feynman diagram expansions, which are in principle infinite.
Both the structure group and renormalisation group will be idempotent (a
sufficiently high power of any group element is the identity), which
implies that the algebraic structures remain reasonably simple.

4.4.1
The model space

The starting point of the construction is the following definition,
cf.
[
Hai14
, Definition 2.1]
.

Definition 4.4.1

(Regularity structure)
.

A
regularity structure
is a triple
(A,T,{\mathscr{G}})
consisting of

1.

an
index set

A\subset\mathbb{R}
, containing
0
, which is bounded
from below and locally finite;

2.

a
model space

T
, which is a graded vector space

T=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in A}T_{\alpha}
, where each
T_{\alpha}
is a Banach space;
the space
T_{0}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
and its unit is denoted
\mathbf{1}
;

3.

a
structure group

{\mathscr{G}}
of linear operators
acting on
T
, such that

\Gamma\tau-\tau\in\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{\beta<\alpha}T_{\beta}=:T_{\alpha}^{-}

(4.4.1)

holds for every
\Gamma\in{\mathscr{G}}
, every
\alpha\in A
and every
\tau\in T_{\alpha}
;
furthermore,
\Gamma\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
for every
\Gamma\in{\mathscr{G}}
.

We have already encountered an example of regularity structure in

Section

4.3.1
:

Example 4.4.2

(Polynomal regularity structure on
\mathbb{R}
)
.

This regularity structure is defined as follows:

•

The index set
A=\mathbb{N}_{0}
is the set of all degrees of monomials on

\mathbb{R}
.

•

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
T_{k}
is the one-dimensional real vector space
spanned by a basis vector denoted
\mathbf{X}^{k}
if
k>0
and
\mathbf{1}
if
k=0
. This is
indeed a Banach space for the norm
\lvert\cdot\rvert
.

•

The structure group is the group
{\mathscr{G}}=\{\Gamma_{h}\colon h\in\mathbb{R}\}

defined by

\Gamma_{h}\mathbf{X}^{k}:=(\mathbf{X}-h)^{k}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}(-h)^{\ell}\mathbf{X}^{k-\ell}\;.

(4.4.2)

Note that the relation (
4.4.1
) is indeed satisfied, and that

{\mathscr{G}}
is isomorphic to the group of translations on
\mathbb{R}
via

\Gamma_{h_{1}}\circ\Gamma_{h_{2}}=\Gamma_{h_{1}+h_{2}}
.

The model space
T
is naturally equipped with the commutative product
defined by
\mathbf{X}^{k}\mathbf{X}^{\ell}=\mathbf{X}^{k+\ell}
, with neutral element
\mathbf{1}
.

This construction can be easily extended to higher dimensions.

Example 4.4.3

(Polynomal regularity structure on
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
)
.

Fix a scaling
\mathfrak{s}=(\mathfrak{s}_{0},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
. Then this
regularity structure is defined in the following way:

•

The index set is again
A=\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

•

The model space is now the direct sum of
T_{\ell}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, where
each
T_{\ell}
is spanned by monomials
\mathbf{X}^{k}=\mathbf{X}_{0}^{k_{0}}\dots\mathbf{X}_{d}^{k_{d}}
of
scaled degree
\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathfrak{s}_{0}k_{0}+\dots+\mathfrak{s}_{d}k_{d}=\ell
.

•

The structure group is again defined by (
4.4.2
), where the
binomial coefficients are defined via
k!=k_{0}!\dots k_{d}!
.

Again,
T
is naturally equipped with a commutative product, and
{\mathscr{G}}
is
now isomorphic to the group of translations on
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
.

The
degree
of a monomial
\mathbf{X}^{k}
is by definition the quantity

\lvert\mathbf{X}^{k}\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}=\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}
, which simply tells us to which
T_{\ell}

the monomial belongs.

In the case of the three-dimensional Allen–Cahn equation, we take
d=3
and
the parabolic scaling
\mathfrak{s}=(2,1,1,1)
. The polynomial regularity structure
now has to be enriched with additional elements representing the noise, the
stochastic convolution, its powers and so on. We start by introducing a symbol

\Xi
representing space-time white noise. In view of (
4.1.4
),
we declare its degree to be given by
\lvert\Xi\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\alpha_{0}
, where
\alpha_{0}:=-\frac{5}{2}-\kappa
for a fixed
\kappa>0
that we can take as small as we
like.

The next step is to introduce an operator
{\mathcal{I}}
creating new symbols
{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)

from existing ones, which represent their convolution with the heat kernel. In
order to avoid redundancies, we declare that
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{X}^{k})=0
for elements of the
polynomial structure. All these symbols can be multiplied, in a commutative
way, yielding still more symbols. Their degree is defined by the two rules

\lvert{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\lvert\tau\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}+2\;,\qquad\lvert\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\lvert\tau_{1}\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}+\lvert\tau_{2}\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}\;.

(4.4.3)

It will also be convenient to use graphical notations such as

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,
. Unlike in (
4.1.14
), this last object is
just a new symbol instead of an undefined product of distributions.

At this point, we realise that there may be a problem with the requirement that
the index set
A
be bounded below, since multiplying symbols of negative degree
allows to generate terms whose degree is arbitrarily negative. The solution is
to set
T:=\vspan({\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}})
, where
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}
is the restriction of the set
{\mathscr{F}}
of
all symbols to the set of those which can be generated by the map

\tau\mapsto{\mathcal{I}}(\Xi+\tau^{3})+\sumop\displaylimits_{k}\mathbf{X}^{k}\;,

(4.4.4)

which is an abstract representation of the fixed-point map (
3.3.3
).

\tau

Symbol

\lvert\tau\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}

\Xi

{\mathcal{I}}(\Xi)^{2}

{\mathcal{I}}({\mathcal{I}}(\Xi)^{3}){\mathcal{I}}(\Xi)^{2}

{\mathcal{I}}(\Xi)

{\mathcal{I}}({\mathcal{I}}(\Xi)^{3}){\mathcal{I}}(\Xi)
