Table 4.1:
Elements of
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}
of degree up to
\frac{3}{2}
. Here
\mathbf{X}_{i}
denotes
any element of the form
\mathbf{X}^{k}
where
k=e_{i}
is a canonical basis vector.

When iterating this map, starting with the empty set, one can check that indeed
all symbols in
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}
have degree at least
\alpha_{0}
. We have listed all
symbols of degree up to
\frac{3}{2}
in
Table

4.1
. The fact
that the degree remains bounded below is a consequence of
local
subcriticality
, also called
super-renormalisability
in Physics,
cf.
[
Hai14
, Assumption 8.3 and Lemma 8.10]
. To define this property,
consider the formal Allen–Cahn equation

\partial_{t}\phi=\Delta\phi+\phi-\phi^{3}+\xi

(4.4.5)

on the
d
-dimensional torus, and perform a scaling

\tilde{\phi}(t,x)=\lambda^{\alpha}\phi(\lambda^{\beta}t,\lambda^{\gamma}x)
,
ignoring boundary conditions. This yields the rescaled equation

\partial_{t}\tilde{\phi}=\lambda^{\beta-2\gamma}\Delta\tilde{\phi}+\lambda^{\beta}\tilde{\phi}-\lambda^{\beta-2\alpha}\tilde{\phi}^{3}+\lambda^{\alpha+\beta}\xi_{\lambda^{\beta},\lambda^{\gamma}}\;.

(4.4.6)

The scaling property of space-time white noise shows that
\tilde{\xi}=\lambda^{(\beta+\gamma d)/2}\xi_{\lambda^{\beta},\lambda^{\gamma}}
has the same
law as
\xi
(cf.
Proposition

2.2.5
). Choosing
\gamma=1
,
\beta=2
and

\alpha=d/2-1
thus yields

\partial_{t}\tilde{\phi}=\Delta\tilde{\phi}+\lambda^{2}\tilde{\phi}-\lambda^{4-d}\tilde{\phi}^{3}+\tilde{\xi}\;.

(4.4.7)

The equation is called
locally subcritical
if the coefficient of the
nonlinear term vanishes in the limit
\lambda\to 0
, which corresponds to
zooming in on small scales. This is the case if and only if
d<4
, which is why
the Allen–Cahn equation can be renormalised in dimensions two and three, but
not in higher dimension.

Exercise 4.4.4
.

Determine the degree of the symbols listed in
Table

4.1
in the
case of the two-dimensional Allen–Cahn equation, when
\Xi
has degree

\alpha_{0}=-\frac{3}{2}-\kappa
. What happens in the four-dimensional case?

4.4.2
The structure group

We have already defined the structure group for the polynomial part
\overline{T}=\vspan\{\mathbf{X}^{k}\colon k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}\}
of the model space as being given by
all maps
\Gamma_{h}
acting as

\Gamma_{h}\mathbf{X}^{k}=(\mathbf{X}-h)^{k}\;,\qquad h\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\;.

(4.4.8)

Extending the structure group to the non-polynomial elements is a somewhat
tricky point, since it has to reflect the way Taylor expansions change under
translations of the base point. Since space-time white noise is
translation-invariant, it seems reasonable to set

\Gamma\Xi=\Xi\qquad\forall\Gamma\in{\mathscr{G}}\;.

(4.4.9)

It turns out that the same holds for the element
. This is related to the
fact that the stochastic convolution has negative regularity, so that there is
no need to subtract any polynomial terms when testing it against a scaled test
function. We have encountered this situation in (
2.3.28
)
in the proof of Schauder’s
Theorem

2.3.9
. Therefore, we set

\Gamma\,\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.3379pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

(4.4.10)

The situation is different, however, for elements such as
. Indeed,

represents a function with positive regularity, from which one has to
subtract a term before testing it, as in (
2.3.33
), a
procedure known as
recentering
. Therefore, the structure group cannot
act trivially on such elements.

To extend the structure group to all elements of
T
, it is helpful to take a
look at the structure group of
\overline{T}
in a more algebraic way. The dual

\overline{T}^{\,*}
can be identified with the space

\{\DD^{k}\colon k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}\}
of differential operators with constant
coefficients via

\langle\DD^{k},\mathbf{X}^{\ell}\rangle:=\frac{\partial\mathbf{X}^{\ell}}{\partial\mathbf{X}^{k}}\biggr|_{\mathbf{X}=0}=k!\delta_{k\ell}\;.

(4.4.11)

We can define an action
(\DD^{\ell},\tau)\mapsto\Gamma_{\DD^{\ell}}\tau
of

\overline{T}^{\,*}
onto
\overline{T}
by

\langle\DD^{k},\Gamma_{\DD^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}^{m}\rangle:=\langle\DD^{k}\DD^{\ell},\mathbf{X}^{m}\rangle=m!\delta_{k+\ell,m}\qquad\forall\DD^{k}\in\overline{T}^{\,*},\forall\mathbf{X}^{m}\in\overline{T}\;,

(4.4.12)

which by (
4.4.11
) is equivalent to

\Gamma_{\DD^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}^{m}=\frac{m!}{(m-\ell)!}\mathbf{X}^{m-\ell}\;,

(4.4.13)

with the convention that the right-hand side vanishes unless
\ell_{i}\mathbin{\leqslant}m_{i}

for each
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, which we write for short
\ell\mathbin{\leqslant}m
.
Note that we have the semigroup property
\Gamma_{\DD^{\ell}\DD^{m}}=\Gamma_{\DD^{\ell}}\Gamma_{\DD^{m}}
, since for every

\smash{\DD^{k}\in\overline{T}^{\,*}}
and
\mathbf{X}^{n}\in\overline{T}
,

\langle\DD^{k},\Gamma_{\DD^{\ell}\DD^{m}}\mathbf{X}^{n}\rangle=\langle\DD^{k}\DD^{\ell}\DD^{m},\mathbf{X}^{n}\rangle=\langle\DD^{k}\DD^{\ell},\Gamma_{\DD^{m}}\mathbf{X}^{n}\rangle=\langle\DD^{k},\Gamma_{\DD^{\ell}}\Gamma_{\DD^{m}}\mathbf{X}^{n}\rangle\;.

(4.4.14)

However, not all
\Gamma_{\DD^{k}}
are invertible, so that the set

\{\Gamma_{g}\colon g\in\overline{T}^{\,*}\}
does not form a group. We thus define

{\mathscr{G}}
as the set of
\smash{g\in\overline{T}^{\,*}}
which are
group-like
,
meaning that they satisfy

\langle g,\mathbf{X}^{\ell}\mathbf{X}^{m}\rangle=\langle g,\mathbf{X}^{\ell}\rangle\langle g,\mathbf{X}^{m}\rangle\qquad\forall\mathbf{X}^{\ell},\mathbf{X}^{m}\in\overline{T}\;.

(4.4.15)

One can show (cf.
[
Hai14
, Section 4.3]
) that
{\mathscr{G}}
is exactly the Lie
group generated by the Lie algebra of first-order differential operators.
Indeed, if for
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}
we set

\langle h,\DD\rangle:=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{3}h_{i}\DD^{e_{i}}\in\overline{T}^{\,*}\;,\qquad g=\e^{-\langle h,\DD\rangle}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}}\frac{\bigl(-\langle h,\DD\rangle\bigr)^{k}}{k!}\;,

(4.4.16)

then we have

\Gamma_{g}(\mathbf{X}^{\ell})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}}\frac{(-h)^{k}}{k!}\Gamma_{\DD^{k}}(\mathbf{X}^{\ell})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\leqslant}\ell}\frac{(-h)^{k}}{k!}\frac{\ell!}{(\ell-k)!}\mathbf{X}^{\ell-k}=\bigl(\mathbf{X}-h\bigr)^{\ell}\;,

(4.4.17)

showing that the group action
\Gamma
of
{\mathscr{G}}
onto
\overline{T}
is
indeed equivalent to the action of the structure group of the polynomial
regularity structure. This is related to the fact that first-order derivatives
generate the group of translations.

Exercise 4.4.5
.

Prove that the element
g
defined by (
4.4.16
) is group-like in the
sense of (
4.4.15
) in the case where the indices
k
belong to

\mathbb{N}_{0}
instead of
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}
.

A useful way of encoding the Leibniz rule is to define a bilinear operator

\Delta^{+}:\overline{T}\to\overline{T}\otimes\overline{T}
by

\Delta^{+}(\mathbf{1}):=\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\;,\qquad\Delta^{+}(\mathbf{X}_{i}):=\mathbf{X}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{X}_{i}\;,

(4.4.18)

which is then extended to all of
\overline{T}
by requiring that

\Delta^{+}(\mathbf{X}^{k}\mathbf{X}^{\ell}):=\Delta^{+}(\mathbf{X}^{k})\Delta^{+}(\mathbf{X}^{\ell})\qquad\forall\mathbf{X}^{k},\mathbf{X}^{\ell}\in\overline{T}\;.

(4.4.19)

The operator
\Delta^{+}
is called a
coproduct
.
One can indeed check that
\Delta^{+}
is
coassociative
, meaning that

(\Id\otimes\Delta^{+})\Delta^{+}\mathbf{X}^{k}=(\Delta^{+}\otimes\Id)\Delta^{+}\mathbf{X}^{k}\qquad\forall\mathbf{X}^{k}\in\overline{T}\;,

(4.4.20)

which provides
\overline{T}
with a
Hopf algebra
structure
2
2

2

In
addition to a product and coproduct and associated neutral elements, a Hopf
algebra is characterised by a linear map
{\mathscr{A}}:\overline{T}\to\overline{T}
called the

antipode
. It should satisfy the identity
M({\mathscr{A}}\otimes\Id)\Delta^{+}=M(\Id\otimes{\mathscr{A}})\Delta^{+}
, where
M
is the multiplication map defined by

M(\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2})=\tau_{1}\tau_{2}
. In this setting, it is given by

\smash{{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbf{X}^{k})=(-1)^{k}\mathbf{X}^{k}}
.
. Furthermore, we have the duality property

\langle\DD^{k}\DD^{\ell},\mathbf{X}^{m}\rangle=\langle\DD^{k}\otimes\DD^{\ell},\Delta^{+}\mathbf{X}^{m}\rangle\;,

(4.4.21)

where by definition

\langle\DD^{k}\otimes\DD^{\ell},Y^{(1)}\otimes Y^{(2)}\rangle:=\langle\DD^{k},Y^{(1)}\rangle\langle\DD^{\ell},Y^{(2)}\rangle\;.

(4.4.22)

Note that in general,
\Delta^{+}\mathbf{X}^{m}
is a sum of several terms

Y_{i}^{(1)}\otimes Y_{i}^{(2)}
, but it is convenient to use
Sweedler’s
notation
suppressing the sum. The point is that by
combining (
4.4.21
) with (
4.4.12
), one obtains
the relation

\Gamma_{\DD^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}^{m}=(\Id\otimes\DD^{\ell})\Delta^{+}\mathbf{X}^{m}\;,

(4.4.23)

where by definition,
(\Id\otimes\DD^{\ell})Y^{(1)}\otimes Y^{(2)}:=Y^{(1)}\langle\DD^{\ell},Y^{(2)}\rangle
.

The advantage of this rather formal approach, which may seem like overkill in
the polynomial case, is that it allows to define the structure group in the
general case. Define a set of formal expressions

{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}^{+}=\Biggl\{\mathbf{X}^{k}\prodop\displaylimits_{j}{\mathcal{J}}_{k_{i}}\tau_{j}\colon k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4},\;\tau_{j}\in{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}},\;\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}+2-\lvert k_{j}\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}>0\Biggr\}\;.

(4.4.24)

Here the
{\mathcal{J}}_{k}\tau
are new symbols of degree
\lvert\tau\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}+2-\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
which is strictly positive by assumption. Their meaning will become clearer
later on. By convention,
\smash{{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}^{+}}
also contains the unit
\mathbf{1}
and
all monomials
\mathbf{X}^{k}
, corresponding to an empty product.

\tau

\Delta^{+}(\tau)

\Gamma_{g}\tau

Table 4.2:
Elements of
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}
of degree up to
\frac{3}{2}
on which the structure
group has a non-trivial effect and their coproducts. Repeated indices
i
imply
summation over all values of
i
, and we have written
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
instead of

{\mathcal{J}}_{e_{i}}
.

The coproduct
\Delta^{+}
is extended from
\overline{T}
to
T=\vspan({\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}})
by
requiring that, in addition to (
4.4.18
) and multiplicativity as
in (
4.4.19
), it satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}(\Xi)

\displaystyle:=\Xi\otimes\mathbf{1}\;,

(4.4.25)

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}({\mathcal{I}}\tau)

\displaystyle:=({\mathcal{I}}\otimes\Id)\Delta^{+}(\tau)+\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,m}\frac{\mathbf{X}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{X}^{m}}{m!}{\mathcal{J}}_{\ell+m}\tau\;.

(4.4.26)

The sum in the last equation is necessarily finite, because of the limitation
on the degree in (
4.4.24
). Denoting by
T_{+}^{*}
the set of
linear maps
\tau\mapsto\langle h,\tau\rangle
from
T_{+}=\vspan({\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}^{+})
to
\mathbb{R}
, the
structure group
{\mathscr{G}}
is given by the set of group-like elements in
T^{*}
,
acting on
T
as

(g,\tau)\mapsto\Gamma_{g}\tau:=(\Id\otimes g)\Delta^{+}\tau\;.

(4.4.27)

Note that since
\langle g,\mathbf{1}\rangle=1
for any group-like
g
,
(
4.4.25
) yields
\Gamma_{g}(\Xi)=\Xi
, so that this definition is
compatible with the invariance of
\Xi
required in (
4.4.9
).
Furthermore, the invariance of
,
and
required
in (
4.4.10
) is satisfied, because

\Delta^{+}(\,\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.3379pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

Exercise 4.4.6
.

Check the expressions for
\Delta^{+}\tau
and
\Gamma_{g}\tau
listed in

Table

4.2
. What is the matrix representing
\Gamma_{g}
in a

Remark 4.4.7
.

If
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}^{+}
were equal to
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}
, the coproduct
\Delta^{+}
would again endow

T
with a Hopf-algebra structure. Since these sets are different,
T
is now a
comodule over
T_{+}=\vspan({\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}^{+})
. However,
T_{+}
can also be endowed with a
Hopf algebra structure — see
[
Hai14
, Section 8.1]
. Strictly
speaking, since
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}
and
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm AC$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm AC$}}}^{+}
are not stable under multiplication, the
comodule should rather be defined on
\vspan({\mathscr{F}})
and its analogue

\vspan({\mathscr{F}}^{+})
, but this has no influence on
the theory.

4.4.3
The renormalisation group

The renormalisation group also acts on the model space
T
, but has a different
purpose than the structure group. In the two-dimensional case, we have seen
that Wick renormalisation, as appearing in (
3.2.66
), allows
to convert divergent stochastic integrals into convergent ones by subtracting
appropriate terms. Here we will perform the same subtractions, but as we have
seen in
Section

4.2
and in (
4.3.18
), it is necessary to
introduce a second renormalisation constant.

The second renormalisation constant
C_{\delta}^{(2)}
is in fact related to the
symbol
. This is because similarly to (
3.2.56
),
this symbol represents the stochastic integral

\begin{split}\Bigl(P*(P_{\delta}*\xi)^{2}(P_{\delta}*\xi)^{2}\Bigr)(z):=\idotsintop\nolimits P(z-z_{0})P_{\delta}(z_{0}-z_{1})\xi(\dd\!z_{1})P_{\delta}(z_{0}-z_{2})\xi(\dd\!z_{2})\\
\times P_{\delta}(z-z_{3})\xi(\dd\!z_{3})P_{\delta}(z-z_{4})\xi(\dd\!z_{4})\dd\!z_{0}\;.\end{split}

By Isserlis’ theorem, we formally have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\xi(\dd\!z_{1})\xi(\dd\!z_{2})\xi(\dd\!z_{3})\xi(\dd\!z_{4})\bigr]={}

\displaystyle\delta(z_{1}-z_{2})\delta(z_{3}-z_{4})\dd\!z_{1}\dd\!z_{3}

(4.4.31)

\displaystyle{}+\delta(z_{1}-z_{3})\delta(z_{2}-z_{4})\dd\!z_{1}\dd\!z_{2}

(4.4.32)

\displaystyle{}+\delta(z_{1}-z_{4})\delta(z_{2}-z_{3})\dd\!z_{1}\dd\!z_{2}\;.

(4.4.33)

The expectation of (
4.4.3
) is thus given by the sum of three
integrals. The first one actually vanishes for symmetry reasons, while the last
two integrals are equal, and given by

\iiintop\nolimits P(z-z_{0})P_{\delta}(z_{0}-z_{1})P_{\delta}(z-z_{1})P_{\delta}(z_{0}-z_{2})P_{\delta}(z-z_{2})\dd\!z_{0}\dd\!z_{1}\dd\!z_{2}\;.

(4.4.34)

We can then use the identities (
3.2.63
) from the proof of

Proposition

3.2.16
to obtain

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits P_{\delta}(z_{0}-z_{1})P_{\delta}(z-z_{1})\dd\!z_{1}

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P_{\delta}(t_{0}-t_{1},x_{0}-x_{1})P_{\delta}(t-t_{1},x-x_{1})\dd\!x_{1}\dd\!t_{1}

(4.4.35)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{P}_{\delta}(t_{0}+t-2t_{1},x_{0}-x)\dd\!t_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{G}_{\delta}(x_{0}-x)\;.

(4.4.36)

Substituting in (
4.4.34
) and changing variables from
x-x_{0}
to

-x_{0}
, we thus obtain

C_{\delta}^{(2)}=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\,\hbox to14.06pt{\vbox to10.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.02837pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 7.09}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.87 5.12}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -7.87 5.12}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.09 L -4.92 12.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.09 L 4.92 12.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 5.12 M 9.45 5.12 C 9.45 5.99 8.74 6.69 7.87 6.69 C 7 6.69 6.3 5.99 6.3 5.12 C 6.3 4.25 7 3.54 7.87 3.54 C 8.74 3.54 9.45 4.25 9.45 5.12 Z M 7.87 5.12}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 5.12 M -6.3 5.12 C -6.3 5.99 -7 6.69 -7.87 6.69 C -8.74 6.69 -9.45 5.99 -9.45 5.12 C -9.45 4.25 -8.74 3.54 -7.87 3.54 C -7 3.54 -6.3 4.25 -6.3 5.12 Z M -7.87 5.12}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.92 12.6 M -3.35 12.6 C -3.35 13.47 -4.05 14.17 -4.92 14.17 C -5.79 14.17 -6.5 13.47 -6.5 12.6 C -6.5 11.73 -5.79 11.02 -4.92 11.02 C -4.05 11.02 -3.35 11.73 -3.35 12.6 Z M -4.92 12.6}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.92 12.6 M 6.5 12.6 C 6.5 13.47 5.79 14.17 4.92 14.17 C 4.05 14.17 3.35 13.47 3.35 12.6 C 3.35 11.73 4.05 11.02 4.92 11.02 C 5.79 11.02 6.5 11.73 6.5 12.6 Z M 4.92 12.6}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{\delta}\Bigr]=2\biggl(-\frac{1}{2}\biggr)^{2}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}P(t-t_{0},-x_{0})\tilde{G}_{\delta}(-x_{0})^{2}\dd\!x_{0}\dd\!t_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\tilde{G}_{\delta}(x_{0})^{3}\dd\!x_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\raisebox{-5.5pt}{
\hbox to28.45pt{\vbox to17.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84544pt\lower-8.5359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(4.4.38)

Formally, an element
M^{\delta}
of the renormalisation group is defined as

M^{\delta}:=\exp\Bigl\{-C_{\delta}^{(1)}L_{1}-C_{\delta}^{(2)}L_{2}\Bigr\}\;,

(4.4.39)

where
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
represent the substitutions

L_{1}:\,\hbox to9.79pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.8945pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -4.92 8.27}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

(4.4.46)

Observe that now, the idempotency results from the fact that all additional
terms have a
higher
degree than the original term.

Remark 4.4.8
.

The renormalisation group can also be introduced in a more abstract way, via a
coproduct
\Delta^{-}
and an associated Hopf algebra. See in
particular
[
BHZ19
,
CH16
]
. In some sense, this
indicates that recentering and renormalisation are “dual” procedures.

4.5
Regularity structures: analytic aspects

Now that the abstract algebraic framework is in place, we have to complete it
with analytic objects, describing the different maps in
the commutative diagram (
4.3.19
).

4.5.1
Models

We describe in this section the map denoted
\Psi
in the
diagram (
4.3.19
), which associates to a realisation of (mollified)
space-time white noise a collection
Z^{\delta}=(\Pi^{\delta},\Gamma^{\delta})

of objects called a
model
. The following definition is

[
Hai14
, Definition 2.17]
particularised to the parabolic scaling

\mathfrak{s}=(2,1,1,1)
, where we denote space-time points by

z=(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

Definition 4.5.1

(Model)
.

A model for a regularity structure
(A,T,{\mathscr{G}})
is
a pair
Z=(\Pi,\Gamma)
, defined by a collection

\{\Pi_{z}:T\to{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\}_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{4}}
of continuous linear maps and a
map
\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}\to{\mathscr{G}}
with the following properties.

1.

\Gamma_{zz}=\Id
is the identity of

{\mathscr{G}}
and

\Gamma_{zz^{\prime}}\Gamma_{z^{\prime}z^{\prime\prime}}=\Gamma_{zz^{\prime\prime}}
for
all
z,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

2.

\Pi_{z^{\prime}}=\Pi_{z}\Gamma_{zz^{\prime}}
for all
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

3.

Let
r=\lceil-\inf A\rceil
.
For any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and any compact set

\mathfrak{K}\subset{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
, one has

\lVert\Pi\rVert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}:=\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{K}}~\sup_{\alpha<\gamma}~\sup_{\tau\in T_{\alpha}}~\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}~\sup_{0<\lambda\mathbin{\leqslant}1}\frac{\bigl|\langle\Pi_{z}\tau,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle\bigr|}{\lambda^{\alpha}\lVert\tau\rVert_{\alpha}}<\infty\;.

(4.5.1)

4.

For any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and any compact set

\mathfrak{K}\subset{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
, one has

\lVert\Gamma\rVert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}:=\sup_{z,z^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}}~\sup_{\alpha<\gamma}~\sup_{\beta<\alpha}~\sup_{\tau\in T_{\alpha}}\frac{\lVert\Gamma_{zz^{\prime}}\tau\rVert_{\beta}}{\lVert\tau\rVert_{\alpha}\,\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert^{\alpha-\beta}_{\mathfrak{s}}}<\infty\;.

(4.5.2)

We recall that the set
B_{r}
occurring in (
4.5.1
) is the set of
smooth test functions supported in the unit
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}
-ball and of
unit
{\mathscr{C}}^{r}
-norm, already encountered in
Definition

2.2.7
. The norm

\lVert\tau\rVert_{\alpha}
in (
4.5.1
) and (
4.5.2
) is
any norm on the finite-dimensional vector space
T_{\alpha}
.

The requirement (
4.5.1
) essentially asks that
\Pi_{z}\tau
should
belong to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
whenever
\tau\in T_{\alpha}
. There is a slight
difference, however, since the bound is only required to hold when zooming in
at the particular point
z
where the model is evaluated.

An important feature of the theory is that the definition allows for different
models for a given regularity structure. This will become important when
considering renormalisation. A particular role is played by the
canonical
model

Z^{\delta}=(\Pi^{\delta},\Gamma^{\delta})
for mollified noise
\xi^{\delta}=\varrho^{\delta}*\xi
, which is defined by

\displaystyle(\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\Xi)(\bar{z})

\displaystyle:=\xi^{\delta}(\bar{z})\;,

(4.5.3)

\displaystyle(\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\mathbf{X}^{k})(\bar{z})

\displaystyle:=(\bar{z}-z)^{k}

\displaystyle\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}\;,

(4.5.4)

\displaystyle(\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})(\bar{z})

\displaystyle:=(\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\tau_{1})(\bar{z})(\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\tau_{2})(\bar{z})

\displaystyle\forall\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T\;.

(4.5.5)

Note that for polynomial symbols
\mathbf{X}^{k}
, this definition is exactly as
in (
4.3.5
).

The tricky part is again to extend this to elements of the form
{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)
.
This is done in
[
Hai14
, Section 5]
via a decomposition

P(t,x)=K(t,x)+R(t,x)

(4.5.6)

of the heat kernel
P
into a smooth part
R
, and a singular part
K
with
special algebraic properties. These properties are as follows:

•

K
is supported in the set
\{\lvert x\rvert^{2}+\lvert t\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}1\}
where

\lvert x\rvert=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{3}\lvert x_{j}\rvert
;

•

K(t,x)=0
for
t\mathbin{\leqslant}0
and
K(t,-x)=K(t,x)
for all
(t,x)
;

•

we have

K(t,x)=\frac{1}{\lvert 4\pi t\rvert^{3/2}}\e^{-\lvert x\rvert^{2}/(4t)}\qquad\text{for $\lvert x\rvert^{2}+\lvert t\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{1}{2}$}

(4.5.7)

and
K(t,x)
is smooth for
\lvert x\rvert^{2}+\lvert t\rvert>\frac{1}{2}
;

•

finally, the integral of
K
against any polynomial of parabolic
degree less or equal some fixed
\zeta\mathbin{\geqslant}2
vanishes; this last condition is
for compatibility with the non-redundancy condition
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{X}^{k})=0
.

See
[
Hai14
, Lemma 5.5]
for a proof that such a decomposition is
indeed possible. This lemma also shows that
K
can be decomposed, as
in (
2.3.25
), as a sum of kernels
K_{n}
, where each
K_{n}
is supported
in a ball of radius
2^{-n}
, and has
k
th derivatives bounded by

C2^{(1+\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}})n}
, as in (
2.3.26
).
Then the construction of the canonical model is completed by requiring that

(\Pi^{\delta}_{z}{\mathcal{I}}\tau)(\bar{z}):=\langle\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\tau,K(\bar{z}-\cdot)\rangle+\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\frac{(\bar{z}-z)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\langle f^{\delta}_{z},{\mathcal{J}}_{\ell}\tau\rangle\qquad\forall\tau\in T\;,

(4.5.8)

where the
f^{\delta}_{z}\in T_{+}^{*}
are linear forms defined in (
4.5.12
)
below. This definition makes sense, even if
\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\tau
is replaced by a
genuine distribution
\Pi_{z}\tau
, by interpreting (
4.5.8
) as a sum
over all
K_{n}
, each of which is tested against
\Pi_{z}\tau
.

As suggested by the construction of the structure group in

Section

4.4.2
, the linear forms
f^{\delta}_{z}
will be used to
construct the elements
\Gamma^{\delta}_{zz^{\prime}}
of the model. They are defined by

\displaystyle\langle f^{\delta}_{z},\mathbf{1}\rangle

\displaystyle:=1\;,

(4.5.9)

\displaystyle\langle f^{\delta}_{z},\mathbf{X}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle:=-z_{i}\;,

(4.5.10)

\displaystyle\langle f^{\delta}_{z},\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\langle f^{\delta}_{z},\tau_{1}\rangle\langle f^{\delta}_{z},\bar{\tau}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\forall\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T\;,

(4.5.11)

\displaystyle\langle f^{\delta}_{z},{\mathcal{J}}_{\ell}\tau\rangle

\displaystyle:=-\langle\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\tau,\DD^{\ell}K(z-\cdot)\rangle

\displaystyle\forall\tau\in T\;.

(4.5.12)

The maps
\Gamma^{\delta}_{zz^{\prime}}
of the canonical model are then defined, as
in (
4.4.27
), by

\Gamma^{\delta}_{zz^{\prime}}:=(F^{\delta}_{z})^{-1}F^{\delta}_{z^{\prime}}\qquad\text{where
$F^{\delta}_{z}:=\Gamma_{f^{\delta}_{z}}=(\Id\otimes f^{\delta}_{z})\Delta^{+}$\;.}

(4.5.13)

Note that the first property of
Definition

4.5.1
is automatically satisfied.
To check the second property, let
\gamma_{zz^{\prime}}
denote the element of the
structure group such that
\Gamma_{zz^{\prime}}=\Gamma_{\gamma_{zz^{\prime}}}
.
Writing
\Delta^{+}\tau=\smash{\tau^{(1)}\otimes\tau^{(2)}}
in Sweedler’s
notation, we have by (
4.4.27
)

\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\Gamma^{\delta}_{zz^{\prime}}\tau=\Pi^{\delta}_{z}(\Id\otimes\gamma^{\delta}_{zz^{\prime}})\Delta^{+}\tau=\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\tau^{(1)}\langle\gamma^{\delta}_{zz^{\prime}},\tau^{(2)}\rangle\;.

(4.5.14)

The second property of
Definition

4.5.1
thus amounts to the relation

\Pi^{\delta}_{z^{\prime}}\tau=\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\tau^{(1)}\langle\gamma^{\delta}_{z\bar{z}},\tau^{(2)}\rangle\;,

(4.5.15)

which provides some intuition for the meaning of
\Delta^{+}
. The fact that

(\Pi^{\delta},\Gamma^{\delta})
is indeed a model is proved
in
[
Hai14
, Proposition 8.27]
.

Exercise 4.5.2
.

•

Check that in the case
\tau=\mathbf{X}_{i}
, the definition (
4.5.12
) is
compatible with the expressions (
4.3.11
) of the
\Gamma_{xy}

introduced in the one-dimensional polynomial case.

•

Compute

(4.5.16)

What is the associated expression for
\gamma^{\delta}_{zz^{\prime}}
? Compare
with (
4.4.30
).

Remark 4.5.3
.

The only rule that we are going to bend for general models is the product
rule (
4.5.5
). For instance, in the case of Wick
renormalisation, the model for
is different from the product of two
models for
, owing to the subtraction a counterterm.

4.5.2
Modelled distributions

In this section, we describe the space in which the fixed-point equation
defining the solution map
{\mathscr{S}}
lives, which appears in the upper part of the
commutative diagram (
4.3.19
). The following definition
is a particularisation of
[
Hai14
, Definition 3.1]
.

Definition 4.5.4

(Modelled distributions)
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.
The space of modelled distributions
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
consists of all function

f:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to T_{\gamma}^{-}
such that for every compact set

\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
one has

|\!|\!|f|\!|\!|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}:=\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{K}}~\sup_{\beta<\gamma}\lVert f(z)\rVert_{\beta}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,z^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}\\
\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbin{\leqslant}1\end{subarray}}\sup_{\beta<\gamma}\frac{\lVert f(z)-\Gamma_{zz^{\prime}}f(z^{\prime})\rVert_{\beta}}{\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert^{\gamma-\beta}_{\mathfrak{s}}}<\infty\;.

(4.5.17)

Here
\lVert f(z)\rVert_{\beta}
denotes the norm of the projection of
f(z)
on

T_{\beta}
. A modelled distribution can thus be written as a Taylor-series-like
expression

f(z)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha<\gamma}\sumop\displaylimits_{\tau\in T_{\alpha}}f_{\tau}(z)\tau\;,

(4.5.18)

where the projection of
f(z)-\Gamma_{zz^{\prime}}f(z^{\prime})
on
T_{\beta}
satisfies a
Hölder condition of exponent
\gamma-\beta
. This is indeed a
generalisation of (
4.3.4
), while the condition (
4.5.17
) is
of the same form as the condition (
4.3.17
) that ensured that
f

belong to a given Hölder space. One important difference between expansions of
the form (
4.5.18
) and usual Taylor expansions is that the “basis vectors”
\tau
in (
4.5.18
) can be associated with
very irregular distributions, while Taylor expansions use monomials which are
analytic functions. One should thus distinguish between the
regularity

\alpha_{0}=\inf A
of the expansion (
4.5.18
), and the Hölder
exponent
\gamma
, which in applications will always be larger than
\alpha_{0}

(and, in fact, positive).

It may happen that the smallest value of
\alpha
occurring in the
expansion (
4.5.18
) is some degree
\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{0}
. In that
case, we say that
f
has regularity
\alpha_{1}
(or that it is defined on a

sector

V\subset T
of regularity
\alpha_{1}
). This is important, in
particular, for the following result, which corresponds
to
[
Hai14
, Theorem 4.7]
.

Theorem 4.5.5

(Multiplication of modelled distributions)
.

Let
f_{1}\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma_{1}}
and
f_{2}\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma_{2}}
be modelled distributions
of respective regularity
\alpha_{1}\mathbin{\leqslant}0
and
\alpha_{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}0
, and let

\gamma=(\gamma_{1}+\alpha_{1})\wedge(\gamma_{2}+\alpha_{2})
. Then one can define a
modelled distribution
f_{1}f_{2}\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
of regularity

\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
, which satisfies

|\!|\!|f_{1}f_{2}|\!|\!|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}\lesssim|\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!|_{\gamma_{1};\mathfrak{K}}|\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!|_{\gamma_{2};\mathfrak{K}}\Bigl(1+\lVert\Gamma_{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2};\mathfrak{K}}\rVert\Bigr)^{2}\;.

(4.5.19)

for every compact set
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
The product
f_{1}f_{2}
is obtained by multiplying formally the expansions of

f_{1}
and
f_{2}
and truncating them to order
\gamma
.

Note that in
Definition

4.5.4
,
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
depends on the
\Gamma
of the
chosen model, so that it should be written
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}(\Gamma)
in case one
works with several models, as will be the case when dealing with
renormalisation. In order to compare elements
f\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}(\Gamma)
and

\bar{f}\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}(\overline{\Gamma})
, we introduce the quantities

\displaystyle\lVert f-\bar{f}\rVert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{K}}~\sup_{\beta<\gamma}~\lVert f(z)-\bar{f}(z)\rVert_{\beta}\;,

(4.5.20)

\displaystyle|\!|\!|f\,\mathord{;}\,\bar{f}|\!|\!|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}

\displaystyle:=\lVert f-\bar{f}\rVert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,z^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}\\
\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}\mathbin{\leqslant}1\end{subarray}}\sup_{\beta<\gamma}\frac{\lVert f(z)-\bar{f}(z)-\Gamma_{zz^{\prime}}f(z^{\prime})+\overline{\Gamma}_{zz^{\prime}}\bar{f}(z^{\prime})\rVert_{\beta}}{\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\gamma-\beta}}\;.

(4.5.21)

The reason for the semicolon in the last expression is that it is not a
function of
f-\bar{f}
, and hence is not symmetric under exchange of
f
and

\bar{f}
. The quantity
|\!|\!|\cdot\,\mathord{;}\,\cdot|\!|\!|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}
is thus
not strictly speaking a norm, though it has similar properties for all
practical purposes.

4.5.3
The reconstruction theorem

A key result of the theory of regularity structures is the reconstruction
theorem, which states the existence of a map
{\mathscr{R}}
from a space
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
of
modelled distributions to a “classical” Hölder space. This map
appears on the right-hand side of the commutative
diagram (
4.3.19
).
The following result is part of
[
Hai14
, Theorem 3.10]
.

Theorem 4.5.6

(Reconstruction theorem)
.

Let
(A,T,{\mathscr{G}})
be a regularity structure, let
\alpha_{0}=\inf A
, and
fix
r>\lvert\alpha_{0}\rvert
as well as a model
(\Pi,\Gamma)
. Then for every

\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a continuous map

{\mathscr{R}}:{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha_{0}}_{\mathfrak{s}}
such that for every compact set

\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
,

\bigl|\langle{\mathscr{R}}f-\Pi_{z}f(z),{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle\bigr|\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}\lVert\Pi\rVert_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}|\!|\!|f|\!|\!|_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}

(4.5.22)

holds uniformly over all test functions
\varphi\in B_{r}
, all
\lambda\in(0,1]
, all

f\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
and all
z\in\mathfrak{K}
. Here
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
is the
1
-fattening of

\mathfrak{K}
, that is, the set of points at
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathfrak{s}}
-distance at most

1
from
\mathfrak{K}
.

Furthermore, if
\gamma>0
, then
{\mathscr{R}}f
is unique.

If
f(z)
is written as in (
4.5.18
), then the term
\Pi_{z}f(z)

in (
4.5.22
) has the expression

\Pi_{z}f(z)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha<\gamma}\sumop\displaylimits_{\tau\in T_{\alpha}}f_{\tau}(z)\Pi_{z}\tau\;,

(4.5.23)

which is in general a distribution. In the particular case where
f
has
non-negative regularity, one has in fact

{\mathscr{R}}f(z)=\bigl(\Pi_{z}f(z)\bigr)(z)=\langle\mathbf{1},f(z)\rangle:=f_{\mathbf{1}}(z)\;,

(4.5.24)

cf.
[
Hai14
, Proposition 3.28]
, which is a generalisation
of (
4.3.8
). More generally, if every
\Pi_{z}\tau
happens to belong
to a Hölder space
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}
with
\alpha>0
, then one has

{\mathscr{R}}f(z)=\bigl(\Pi_{z}f(z)\bigr)(z)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha<\gamma}\sumop\displaylimits_{\tau\in T_{\alpha}}f_{\tau}(z)\bigl(\Pi_{z}\tau\bigr)(z)\;,

(4.5.25)

see
[
Hai14
, Remark 3.15]
. This holds in particular for the
canonical model for mollified noise
\Pi^{\delta}
constructed in the previous
section. In general,
{\mathscr{R}}f
and
\Pi_{z}f(z)
may differ by a
remainder term of degree
\gamma
, which is small if
\gamma
is large.

The proof of
Theorem

4.5.6
given in
[
Hai14
]
is based on
wavelet analysis. There exists by now an alternative proof, presented
in
[
OW19
]
, which is based on smoothing properties of the heat
kernel.

4.5.4
Multilevel Schauder estimates

The last missing piece needed to lift the classical solution map
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}

to the space of modelled distributions, and defining the map
{\mathscr{S}}
making the
diagram (
4.3.19
) commute, is an operator lifting the operation of
convolution with the heat kernel
P
. Owing to the
decomposition (
4.5.6
) of
P
into a singular part
K
and a smooth
part
R
, this problem can be decomposed into two separate problems, which are
to make the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha_{0}}_{\mathfrak{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha_{0}+2}_{\mathfrak{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K*}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha_{0}}_{\mathfrak{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha_{0}+2}_{\mathfrak{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\gamma}\circ{\mathscr{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R*}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\gamma}}

(4.5.26)

The smooth part
R
is actually comparatively easy to deal with. Indeed, one
can define an operator
R_{\gamma}
, acting on distributions

\zeta\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha_{0}}_{\mathfrak{s}}
via the Taylor-series-like expression

(R_{\gamma}\zeta)(z):=\sumop\displaylimits_{\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{\mathbf{X}^{k}}{k!}\langle\zeta,\DD^{k}R(z-\cdot)\rangle\;.

(4.5.27)

The fact that the right-hand side belongs to
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma+2}
is shown
in
[
Hai14
, Lemma 7.3]
. Since
R_{\gamma}\zeta
has positive regularity,
it follows from (
4.5.24
) that
({\mathscr{R}}R_{\gamma}\zeta)(z)
is just the convolution of
\zeta
with
R(z-\cdot)
, so that for

\zeta={\mathscr{R}}f
we have indeed

{\mathscr{R}}R_{\gamma}{\mathscr{R}}f=R*{\mathscr{R}}f

(4.5.28)

as required.

The construction of the operator
{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}
lifting the convolution with the
singular part
K
of the heat kernel is substantially more involved. It turns
out that
{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}
has to be defined as follows. For any
f\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
,
it is composed of three parts, that is

({\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}f)(z):={\mathcal{I}}f(z)+{\mathscr{J}}(z)f(z)+({\mathscr{N}}_{\gamma}f)(z)\;.

(4.5.29)

The first term
{\mathcal{I}}f(z)
is simply given by applying the abstract integration
operator
{\mathcal{I}}
to each symbol
\tau
in the expansion (
4.5.18
) of

f(z)
. The second term, which is again associated with recentering, is
obtained by setting, for each
\tau\in T_{\alpha}
,

{\mathscr{J}}(z)\tau:=\sumop\displaylimits_{\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}<\alpha+2}\frac{\mathbf{X}^{k}}{k!}\langle\Pi_{z}\tau,\DD^{k}K(z-\cdot)\rangle\;.

(4.5.30)

Note the similarity with (
4.5.12
), which is no accident!
The last part of
{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}
is a nonlocal operator given by

({\mathscr{N}}_{\gamma}f)(z):=\sumop\displaylimits_{\lvert k\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2}\frac{\mathbf{X}^{k}}{k!}\langle{\mathscr{R}}f-\Pi_{z}f(z),\DD^{k}K(z-\cdot)\rangle\;.

(4.5.31)

As before, both operators (
4.5.30
) and (
4.5.31
)
actually are to be interpreted by replacing
K
by a sum of
K_{n}
.
Note that they both have values in
\overline{T}
, the polynomial
part of the regularity structure.

With all these objects in place, we have the following far-reaching
generalisation of the Schauder estimate of
Theorem

2.3.9
, which is an
instance of
[
Hai14
, Theorem 5.12]
.

Theorem 4.5.7

(Multilevel Schauder estimate)
.

If
\gamma+2\notin\mathbb{N}
, then the operator
{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}
defined
in (
4.5.29
) maps
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
into
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma+2}
, and satisfies

{\mathscr{R}}{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}f=K*{\mathscr{R}}f

(4.5.32)

for any
f\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
. Furthermore, if
\overline{Z}=(\overline{\Pi},\overline{\Gamma})
is a second model, then one has

|\!|\!|{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}f\,\mathord{;}\,{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}\bar{f}|\!|\!|_{\gamma+2;\mathfrak{K}}\lesssim|\!|\!|f\,\mathord{;}\,\bar{f}|\!|\!|_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}+\lVert\Pi-\overline{\Pi}\rVert_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}+\lVert\Gamma-\overline{\Gamma}\rVert_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}

(4.5.33)

for any
\bar{f}\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}(\overline{\Gamma})
, where
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
is the

1
-fattening of
\mathfrak{K}
.

The proof of this result is similar in spirit to the proof we have given of

Theorem

2.3.9
, but is significantly more involved because of the required
bookkeeping of all the polynomial terms. In fact, bounding components of

{\mathscr{K}}_{\gamma}f
in
T_{\alpha}
of non-integer degree
\alpha
is relatively
straightforward, and all the difficulty lies in integer values of
\alpha
.

4.5.5
Convergence of renormalised models

The tools introduced so far suffice to set up a fixed-point equation in a space

{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
, associated with the canonical model

Z^{\delta}=(\Pi^{\delta},\Gamma^{\delta})
for mollified noise
\xi^{\delta}
, built in

Section

4.5.1
. In order to be able to take the limit
\delta\searrow 0
,
one has to incorporate the renormalisation procedure.

We already introduced a group of renormalisation transformations
M^{\delta}
in

Section

4.4.3
, see in particular (
4.4.46
). The
M^{\delta}

are linear maps from the model space
T
into itself. It is now necessary to
translate this to a transformation from the model
Z^{\delta}
to a renormalised
model
\widehat{Z}^{\delta}=(\widehat{\Pi}^{\delta},\widehat{\Gamma}^{\delta})
.

A useful remark here is that the second property in

Definition

4.5.1
of models and (
4.5.13
) imply that for all

z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,

\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\bigl(F^{\delta}_{z}\bigr)^{-1}=\Pi^{\delta}_{z^{\prime}}\bigl(F^{\delta}_{z^{\prime}}\bigr)^{-1}=:\mathbf{\Pi}^{\delta}\;,

(4.5.34)

where
\mathbf{\Pi}^{\delta}
is independent of
z
. Therefore, the model can also
be specified by the pair
(\mathbf{\Pi}^{\delta},f^{\delta}_{z})
, and

(\Pi^{\delta},\Gamma^{\delta})
can be recovered via

\Pi^{\delta}_{z}=\mathbf{\Pi}^{\delta}\,\Gamma_{f^{\delta}_{z}}\;,\qquad\Gamma^{\delta}_{zz^{\prime}}=\Gamma_{f^{\delta}_{z}}^{-1}\Gamma_{f^{\delta}_{z^{\prime}}}\;.

(4.5.35)

The renormalised model associated with the renormalisation transformation

M^{\delta}
will thus be defined in such a way that

\widehat{\mathbf{\Pi}}^{\delta}\tau=\mathbf{\Pi}^{\delta}M^{\delta}\tau\qquad\forall\tau\in T\;.

(4.5.36)

While it is possible to compute the renormalised model “by hand”,
there exists a more efficient way of doing this algebraically, which is
described in
[
Hai14
, Section 8.3]
. Here we just illustrate the result
by giving the expressions of
\widehat{\Pi}^{\delta}_{z}\tau
for the negative-degree
elements of
{\mathscr{F}}_{-}
:

(4.5.42)

Note that the first three relations are compatible with Wick renormalisation as
introduced in (
3.2.66
), while the last three relations are
specific to the three-dimensional case.

The question that arises now is whether this sequence of models converges to a
meaningful limit as
\delta\searrow 0
. Here the key result is the following
one, see
[
Hai14
, Theorem 10.7]
.

Theorem 4.5.8

(Convergence criterion for renormalised models)
.

Assume that there exists
\kappa>0
such that, for every test function
\varphi\in B_{r}
, every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
and every
\tau\in T
of negative degree, there exists a
random variable
\langle\widehat{\Pi}_{z}\tau,\varphi\rangle
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\lvert\langle\widehat{\Pi}_{z}\tau,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle\rvert^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2\lvert\tau\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}+\kappa}\;,

(4.5.43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\lvert\langle\widehat{\Pi}_{z}\tau-\widehat{\Pi}_{z}^{\delta}\tau,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{z}\varphi\rangle\rvert^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{2\theta}\lambda^{2\lvert\tau\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}+\kappa}

(4.5.44)

holds for some
\theta>0
. Then there exists a unique model
\widehat{Z}=(\widehat{\Pi},\widehat{\Gamma})
such that

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[|\!|\!|\widehat{Z}|\!|\!|^{p}_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}\Bigr]\lesssim 1\;,\qquad\mathbb{E}\Bigl[|\!|\!|\widehat{Z}\,\mathord{;}\,\widehat{Z}^{\delta}|\!|\!|^{p}_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}\Bigr]\lesssim\delta^{\theta p}

(4.5.45)

holds for every compact
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
and every
p\mathbin{\geqslant}1
. Here the
quantities
|\!|\!|Z|\!|\!|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}=\lVert\Pi\rVert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}+\lVert\Gamma\rVert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}
and
\smash{|\!|\!|Z\,\mathord{;}\,\overline{Z}|\!|\!|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}=\lVert\Pi-\overline{\Pi}\rVert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}+\lVert\Gamma-\overline{\Gamma}\rVert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}}

measure the magnitude of models and their difference.

To be more precise, each random variable
\langle\widehat{\Pi}_{z}\tau,\varphi\rangle

should belong to a specific Wiener chaos, namely the
n
th inhomogeneous Wiener
chaos if
\tau
contains
n
symbols
\Xi
. This means that it can be
written as an integral involving
n
space-time white noise variables, just as
in (
4.4.3
) which is an instance with
n=4
.
The conditions (
4.5.44
) can then be reformulated in terms of
bounds on the Wiener chaos expansion coefficients,
(cf.
[
Hai14
, Proposition 10.11]
), which can be represented by Feynman
diagrams similar to those we encountered in
Section

4.2
.

4.6
Existence and uniqueness of solutions

We return now to our aim of constructing limits of solutions of regularised
Allen–Cahn SPDEs

\partial_{t}\phi_{\delta}(t,x)=\Delta\phi_{\delta}(t,x)+\phi_{\delta}(t,x)-\phi_{\delta}(t,x)^{3}+\bigl[3C_{\delta}^{(1)}-9C_{\delta}^{(2)}\bigr]\phi_{\delta}(t,x)+\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;,

(4.6.1)

with renormalisation constants
\smash{C_{\delta}^{(1)}}={\mathcal{O}}(\delta^{-1})
and
\smash{C_{\delta}^{(2)}}={\mathcal{O}}(\log(\delta^{-1}))

defined as in (
3.2.58
) and (
4.4.37
).
One of the main results of
[
Hai14
]
is the following local existence
theorem (which was formulated for the
\Phi^{4}
model, but the proof extends
trivially to the Allen–Cahn equation).

Theorem 4.6.1

(Local existence of solutions for the three-dimensional
Allen–Cahn equation)
.

Assume
\phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\eta}_{\mathfrak{s}}
for some

\eta>-\frac{2}{3}
. Then the Allen–Cahn equation (
4.6.1
) with
initial condition
\phi_{0}
admits a sequence
\phi_{\delta}
of local solutions
converging in probability, as
\delta\searrow 0
, to a limit
\phi
. This limit
is independent of the choice of mollifier
\varrho
.

Sketch of proof:
.

We will only outline the main steps of the proof, ignoring some technical
subtleties, which are discussed in detail in
[
Hai14
, Sections 9.4 and
10.5]
.

In a first step, we ignore the counterterms
C_{\delta}^{(i)}

in (
4.6.1
). Given
\gamma>\bar{\gamma}>0
, consider the
fixed-point equation

\Phi=P\phi_{0}+({\mathscr{K}}_{\bar{\gamma}}+{\mathscr{R}}R_{\gamma}{\mathscr{R}})\bigl[\Xi+\Phi-\Phi^{3}\bigr]\;,

(4.6.2)

where we make a slight abuse of notation by writing
P\phi_{0}
also for
the lift of
P\phi_{0}
to
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
, defined as in (
4.5.27
). First
we want to show that solving (
4.6.2
) is indeed equivalent
to solving the regularised equation (
4.6.1
) without the
counterterms. Applying the reconstruction operator
{\mathscr{R}}
for the canonical
model
(\Pi^{\delta},\Gamma^{\delta})
to both sides of (
4.6.2
) and
using (
4.5.32
) and (
4.5.28
), we obtain

\phi:={\mathscr{R}}\Phi=P\phi_{0}+P*{\mathscr{R}}\bigl[\Xi+\Phi-\Phi^{3}\bigr]\;.

(4.6.3)

Since we work with the canonical model, we can identify
P*{\mathscr{R}}\Xi
with
the stochastic convolution
P*\xi^{\delta}
. It thus remains to show that

{\mathscr{R}}(\Phi^{3})
is equal to
({\mathscr{R}}\Phi)^{3}=\phi^{3}
. To this end, we
rewrite (
4.6.2
), by separating non-polynomial from polynomial
terms, as

\Phi={\mathcal{I}}\bigl[\Xi+\Phi-\Phi^{3}\bigr]+\sumop\displaylimits_{k}\Phi_{\mathbf{X}^{k}}\mathbf{X}^{k}\;.

(4.6.4)

Iterating this map, it is not hard to see that any fixed point
of (
4.6.4
) should have the expression

\lxSVG@closescope }

(4.6.5)

and thus

({\mathscr{R}}\Phi)(z)=\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\,\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to8.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.3379pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

(4.6.6)

since all other terms in (

) have strictly positive

(4.6.8)

However, all these terms are equal to zero. For instance,

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\bigr)(z)\bigl(\Pi^{\delta}_{z}\,\hbox to9.79pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.8945pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -4.92 8.27}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.92 8.27}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.92 8.27 M -3.35 8.27 C -3.35 9.14 -4.05 9.84 -4.92 9.84 C -5.79 9.84 -6.5 9.14 -6.5 8.27 C -6.5 7.4 -5.79 6.69 -4.92 6.69 C -4.05 6.69 -3.35 7.4 -3.35 8.27 Z M -4.92 8.27}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.92 8.27 M 6.5 8.27 C 6.5 9.14 5.79 9.84 4.92 9.84 C 4.05 9.84 3.35 9.14 3.35 8.27 C 3.35 7.4 4.05 6.69 4.92 6.69 C 5.79 6.69 6.5 7.4 6.5 8.27 Z M 4.92 8.27}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\bigr)(z)
, where
the first term vanishes as shown in
4.5.2
. We conclude that indeed

\phi={\mathscr{R}}\Phi
solves the regularised equation without counterterms.

The second step is to show that (
4.6.2
) admits indeed a unique
fixed point. First we determine admissible values of
\gamma
and
\bar{\gamma}
.
Assume that
\Phi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
has regularity
\alpha\mathbin{\leqslant}0
. Then

Theorem

4.5.5
shows that
\Phi^{3}\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma+2\alpha}
, and has
regularity
3\alpha
. The multilevel Schauder estimate of

Theorem

4.5.7
shows that the right-hand side
of (
4.6.2
) is in
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma+2\alpha+2}
and has regularity

(-\frac{1}{2}-\kappa)\wedge(3\alpha+2)
, provided
\bar{\gamma}\mathbin{\geqslant}\gamma+2\alpha
.
We can thus choose
\alpha=-\frac{1}{2}-\kappa
, which is the regularity of the
stochastic convolution,
\gamma>1+2\kappa
and
\bar{\gamma}=\gamma-1-2\kappa
,
to ensure that both sides of (
4.6.2
) belong to the same spaces.

Here we encounter a new difficulty, which is that the term
P\phi_{0}
is not
necessarily small in
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
near
t=0
. The solution is to modify

Definition

4.5.4
of the spaces of modelled distributions
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
in order
to allow coefficients that diverge near
t=0
in a way controlled by a power

\eta
. This yields new spaces
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma,\eta}
with similar results on
multiplication, reconstruction and Schauder estimates as for
{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}
,
discussed in
[
Hai14
, Section 6]
. By slightly changing the parameter

\eta
, one can also obtain bounds which are small for small times, as required
to show that the fixed-point map is a contraction for small enough times.

The third step is to incorporate the counterterms
C_{\delta}^{(i)}
and
take the limit
\delta\searrow 0
. This implies that we replace the commutative
diagram (
4.3.19
) by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{0},\widehat{Z}^{\delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{M^{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{0},\xi^{\delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}}_{\,\,M^{\delta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi\circ M^{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{\!\!\!M^{\delta}}}

(4.6.9)

where
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{\!\!\!M^{\delta}}
is the solution map of the renormalised
equation (
4.6.1
),
\widehat{Z}^{\delta}=Z^{\delta}M^{\delta}
is
the renormalised model described in (
4.5.42
), and

\smash{\widehat{\mathscr{R}}}
is the corresponding reconstruction operator. The map

{\mathscr{S}}_{\,\,M^{\delta}}
is defined by solving the fixed-point
equation (
4.6.2
), with
{\mathscr{R}}
replaced by
\smash{\widehat{\mathscr{R}}}
.
Then a similar computation as above, taking into account the additional terms in
the model (
4.5.42
), shows that the
diagram (
4.6.9
) indeed commutes.

Finally, we have to check that the renormalised model satisfies the
convergence conditions in
Theorem

4.5.8
. This is done
in
[
Hai14
, Section 10.5]
via a lengthy computation involving Feynman
diagrams. However, recent results
in
[
CH16
,
BHZ19
,
BCCH17
]

based on so-called BPHZ renormalisation allow to make the procedure much more
systematic.
∎

Remark 4.6.2
.

One of the technicalities we have glossed over is that the term in brackets on
the right-hand side of (
4.6.2
) has to be multiplied by an
indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>0\}}
. The resulting objects have then to be
shown to belong to the right spaces of distributions.

4.7
Large deviations

We finally consider the weak-noise variant of (
4.6.1
),
given by

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}+\bigl[(2\varepsilon)3C_{\delta}^{(1)}-(2\varepsilon)^{2}9C_{\delta}^{(2)}\bigr]\phi(t,x)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;.

(4.7.1)

The work
[
HW15
]
also contains the following three-dimensional
analogue of
Theorem

3.5.1
.

Theorem 4.7.1

(Large-deviation principle for the renormalised equation)
.

Let
\phi_{\delta,\varepsilon}
denote the solution of (
4.7.1
), and
let
\varepsilon\mapsto\delta(\varepsilon)\mathbin{\geqslant}0
be a function such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\delta(\varepsilon)=0\;.

(4.7.2)

Then the family
(\phi_{\delta(\varepsilon),\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
with fixed initial
condition
\phi_{0}
satisfies an LDP on
[0,T]
with good rate function

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\gamma(t,x)-\Delta\gamma(t,x)-\gamma(t,x)+\gamma(t,x)^{3}\biggr]^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t&\text{if the integral is finite\;, }\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(4.7.3)

Note that just as in the two-dimensional case, the counterterms are absent from
the large-deviation rate function.

As in
Section

3.5
, for
\delta>0
, we may also consider the variant
of (
4.7.1
) without counterterms

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=\Delta\phi(t,x)+C\phi(t,x)-\phi(t,x)^{3}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\xi^{\delta}(t,x)\;,

(4.7.4)

which does not admit a limit as
\delta\searrow 0
.
Here, the large-deviation result reads as follows.

Theorem 4.7.2

(Large-deviation principle for equation without renormalisation)
.

Let
\tilde{\phi}_{\delta,\varepsilon}
denote the solution of (
4.7.4
), and
let
\varepsilon\mapsto\delta(\varepsilon)\mathbin{\geqslant}0
be a function
satisfying (
4.7.2
) as well as

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1}=\lambda\in[0,\infty)\;.

(4.7.5)

Then the family
(\tilde{\phi}_{\delta(\varepsilon),\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
with fixed initial
condition
\phi_{0}
satisfies an LDP on
[0,T]
with good rate function

{\mathscr{I}}_{[0,T]}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{T}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\gamma(t,x)-\Delta\gamma(t,x)+C^{(\lambda)}\gamma(t,x)+\gamma(t,x)^{3}\biggr]^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!t&\text{if the integral is finite\;, }\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise\;,}\end{cases}

(4.7.6)

where
C^{(\lambda)}=C-3\lambda^{2}C_{\delta}^{(1)}
.

4.8
Bibliographical notes

Perturbation theory for the Gibbs measure of the
\Phi^{4}
model in three
dimensions is an important problem in Euclidean Quantum Field theory that has
been studied for a long time with various methods. The earliest works by Glimm
and Jaffe and by Feldman approached the problem via a detailed combinatorial
analysis of Feynman
diagrams
[
GJ68
,
GJ73
,
Fel74
,
GJ81
]
. The
works
[
BCG+78
,
BCG+80
]
introduced the idea of using a renormalisation
group approach, consisting in a decomposition of the covariance of the Gaussian
field into scales, which then allows to integrate sucessively over one scale
after the other. This method was further perfected
in
[
BDH95
]
, using polymers to control error terms, an
approach based on ideas from Statistical Physics
[
GK71
]
.

In another direction, the approach provided
in
[
BFS83a
,
BFS83b
]
allows
to bound correlation functions without having to compute the partition
function explicitly, by using it as a generating function. This involves the
derivation of skeleton inequalities, which were obtained up to third order
in
[
BFS83a
]
, and later extended to all orders
in
[
BF84
]
. A relatively compact
derivation of bounds on the partition function
based on the Boué–Dupuis formula was recently
obtained in
[
BG18
]
.

The solution theory for the three-dimensional
\Phi^{4}
or Allen–Cahn equation
we presented is based on the theory of regularity structures developed
in
[
Hai14
]
. Alternative approaches include paracontrolled
calculus
[
GIP15
,
CC18
]
and a
Wilsonian renormalisation group
[
Kup16
]
. The large-deviation
results are from
[
HW15
]
.

Regularity structures have been successfully applied to several other
equations, including the KPZ equation
[
Hai13
,
HS17
]
and its
generalisations to polynomial nonlinearities
[
HQ18
]
,
the continuum parabolic Anderson model
[
HL15
]
,
the Navier–Stokes equation
[
ZZ15b
]
,
the motion of a random string on a curved surface
[
Hai16
]
,
the FitzHugh–Nagumo SPDE
[
BK16
]
,
the dynamical Sine–Gordon model
[
HS16
]
,
the heat equation driven by space-time fractional noise
[
Dey16
]
,
reaction-diffusion equations with a fractional Laplacian
[
BK17
]
, and
the multiplicative stochastic heat equation
[
HL18
]
.
Recent progress on the theory includes a systematic approach to
renormalisation
[
CH16
,
BHZ19
,
BCCH17
]
based on
BPHZ-renormalisation, see
also
[
Hai18
,
BGHZ19
]
.

Chapter A
Potential theory for reversible Markov chains

A.1
First-hitting time and capacity

Consider a reversible Markov chain on a finite set
{\mathcal{X}}
, with transition
probabilities
p(x,y)
and invariant probability measure
\pi
. Reversibility
means that

\pi(x)p(x,y)=\pi(y)p(y,x)\qquad\forall x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}\;,

(A.1.1)

which is equivalent to the transition matrix
P=(p(x,y)_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}})
being
self-adjoint with respect to the
L^{2}({\mathcal{X}},\pi)
inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\pi}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)f(x)g(x)\;.

(A.1.2)

The
generator
of the chain is given by

(Lf)(x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}p(x,y)[f(y)-f(x)]\;,

(A.1.3)

and is also self-adjoint in
L^{2}({\mathcal{X}},\pi)
.

Fix a nonempty set
A\subset{\mathcal{X}}
and let

w_{A}(x):=\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mux}\!\left[\tau_{A}\right]\;,\qquad\tau_{A}=\inf\{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0\colon X_{n}\in A\}

(A.1.4)

be the expected first-hitting time of
A
. It satisfies the Poisson boundary
value problem

\begin{cases}(Lw_{A})(x)=-1&\qquad x\in A^{c}\;,\\
w_{A}(x)=0&\qquad x\in A\;.\end{cases}

(A.1.5)

The solution of this problem can be written as

w_{A}(x)=-\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in A^{c}}G_{A^{c}}(x,y)\;,

(A.1.6)

where the Green function (or fundamental matrix)
G_{A^{c}}=L_{A^{c}}^{-1}
is
the inverse of the restriction of the generator
L
to
A^{c}
.

Fix now two disjoint nonempty sets
A,B\subset{\mathcal{X}}
and let

h_{AB}(x):={\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mux}\bigl\{\tau_{A}<\tau_{B}\bigr\}

(A.1.7)

be the
committor function
(or
equilibrium potential
), where

\tau_{A}
is the first-hitting time of
A
. It satisfies the Dirichlet boundary
value
problem

\begin{cases}(Lh_{AB})(x)=0&\qquad x\in(A\cup B)^{c}\;,\\
h_{AB}(x)=1&\qquad x\in A\;,\\
h_{AB}(x)=0&\qquad x\in B\;.\end{cases}

(A.1.8)

The
equilibrium measure
is defined by

e_{AB}(x):=-(Lh_{AB})(x)\qquad\forall x\in A\;.

(A.1.9)

This measure also has a probabilistic interpretation, namely

e_{AB}(x)={\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mux}\bigl\{\tau^{+}_{A}<\tau^{+}_{B}\bigr\}\;,

(A.1.10)

where
\tau^{+}_{A}=\inf\{n\mathbin{\geqslant}1\colon X_{n}\in A\}
denotes the first-return
time to
A
.

Proposition A.1.1
.

The committor has the representation

h_{AB}(x)=-\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in A}G_{B^{c}}(x,y)e_{AB}(y)\qquad\forall x\in(A\cup B)^{c}\;.

(A.1.11)

Proof:
.

The restriction of
Lh_{AB}
to
B^{c}
satisfies

L_{B^{c}}\begin{pmatrix}h_{AB}\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-e_{AB}\end{pmatrix}

(A.1.12)

where the upper part of the vectors represents elements of
(A\cup B)^{c}
, and
the lower part elements of
A
. Multiplying on the left by
G_{B^{c}}=L_{B^{c}}^{-1}
yields the result.
∎

The
capacity
is defined by

\capacity(A,B):=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(x)e_{AB}(x)\;.

(A.1.13)

As a consequence,
\nu_{AB}(x)=\pi(x)e_{AB}(x)/\capacity(A,B)
defines a
probability measure on
A
.

Lemma A.1.2
.

The capacity admits the two equivalent representations

\displaystyle\capacity(A,B)

\displaystyle=\langle h_{AB},-Lh_{AB}\rangle_{\pi}\;,

(A.1.14)

\displaystyle\capacity(A,B)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)p(x,y)\bigl[h_{AB}(x)-h_{AB}(y)\bigr]^{2}\;.

(A.1.15)

Proof:
.

For the first identity, we write

\langle h_{AB},-Lh_{AB}\rangle_{\pi}=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A\cup B\cup(A\cup B)^{c}}\pi(x)h_{AB}(x)(-Lh_{AB})(x)\;.

(A.1.16)

Since
h_{AB}(x)=1
in
A
, while
h_{AB}(x)=0
in
B
and
Lh_{AB}(x)=0
in

(A\cup B)^{c}
, we recover (
A.1.13
).
The second identity is a consequence of summation by parts (or discrete Green’s
identity). Indeed,

\displaystyle\langle h_{AB},-Lh_{AB}\rangle_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)p(x,y)h_{AB}(x)\bigl[h_{AB}(x)-h_{AB}(y)\bigr]

(A.1.17)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)p(x,y)\bigl[\frac{1}{2}h_{AB}(x)^{2}-h_{AB}(x)h_{AB}(y)+\frac{1}{2}h_{AB}(x)^{2}\bigr]

(A.1.18)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)p(x,y)\bigl[\frac{1}{2}h_{AB}(x)^{2}-h_{AB}(x)h_{AB}(y)+\frac{1}{2}h_{AB}(y)^{2}\bigr]\;,

(A.1.19)

where we have used reversibility and a permutation of summation indices to
replace
x
by
y
in the last line.
∎

The main result making the potential-theoretic approach work is the following
link between expected hitting times and capacity.

Theorem A.1.3
.

For any disjoint sets
A,B\subset{\mathcal{X}}
, one has

\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mu\nu_{AB}}\!\left[\tau_{B}\right]:=\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\nu_{AB}(x)w_{B}(x)=\frac{1}{\capacity(A,B)}\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in B^{c}}\pi(x)h_{AB}(x)\;.

(A.1.20)

Proof:
.

We have the identity

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(x)e_{AB}(x)w_{B}(x)

\displaystyle=-\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in B^{c}}\pi(x)G_{B^{c}}(x,y)e_{AB}(x)

(A.1.21)

\displaystyle=-\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in B^{c}}\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(y)G_{B^{c}}(y,x)e_{AB}(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in B^{c}}\pi(y)h_{AB}(y)\;.

(A.1.22)

Dividing on both sides by the capacity yields the result.
∎

A more probabilistic proof of this result can be found in
[
Slo12b
]
.

A.2
Dirichlet principle

Define the
Dirichlet form
(or energy) by

{\mathscr{E}}(f):=\langle f,-Lf\rangle_{\pi}=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)p(x,y)\bigl[f(x)-f(y)\bigr]^{2}\;.

(A.2.1)

We associate with it the bilinear form

{\mathscr{E}}(f,g):=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)p(x,y)\bigl[f(x)-f(y)\bigr]\bigl[g(x)-g(y)\bigr]\;.

(A.2.2)

Note that the polarisation identity and self-adjointness yield

{\mathscr{E}}(f,g)=\frac{1}{4}\bigl({\mathscr{E}}(f+g)-{\mathscr{E}}(f-g)\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\langle f,-Lg\rangle_{\pi}+\langle g,-Lf\rangle_{\pi}\bigr)=\langle f,-Lg\rangle_{\pi}\;.

(A.2.3)

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (and reversibility), we have

{\mathscr{E}}(f,g)^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{E}}(f){\mathscr{E}}(g)\;.

(A.2.4)

Furthermore, Lemma
A.1.2
shows that

\capacity(A,B)={\mathscr{E}}(h_{AB})\;.

(A.2.5)

Proposition A.2.1

(Dirichlet principle)
.

We have

\capacity(A,B)=\min_{h\in{\mathscr{H}}_{AB}}{\mathscr{E}}(h)\;,

(A.2.6)

where
{\mathscr{H}}_{AB}=\{h:{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,1]\colon h|_{A}=1,h|_{B}=0\}
.
The minimum is reached in
h=h_{AB}
.

Proof:
.

Pick any
h\in{\mathscr{H}}_{AB}
and observe that

\displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}(h,h_{AB})=\langle h,-Lh_{AB}\rangle_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)h(x)(-Lh_{AB})(x)

(A.2.7)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(x)(-Lh_{AB})(x)

(A.2.8)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(x)e_{AB}(x)

(A.2.9)

\displaystyle=\capacity(A,B)\;,

(A.2.10)

where we have used the boundary conditions for
h
and the fact that
h_{AB}

vanishes in
(A\cup B)^{c}
to obtain the second line.
The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (
A.2.4
) yields

\capacity(A,B)^{2}={\mathscr{E}}(h,h_{AB})^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{E}}(h){\mathscr{E}}(h_{AB})={\mathscr{E}}(h)\capacity(A,B)

(A.2.11)

and hence
\capacity(A,B)\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{E}}(h)
. We already know that equality is
reached for
h=h_{AB}
.
∎

A.3
Thomson principle

The
Thomson principle
provides a complementary variational principle for
the capacity. It comes in two versions.

Proposition A.3.1

(Thomson principle, super-harmonic version)
.

We have

\capacity(A,B)=\max_{h\in\mathfrak{H}_{AB}}\frac{\bigl(\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(x)(-Lh)(x)\bigr)^{2}}{{\mathscr{E}}(h)}\;,

(A.3.1)

where
\mathfrak{H}_{AB}=\{h:{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,1]\colon(Lh)(x)\mathbin{\leqslant}0\;\forall x\in B^{c}\}
. The maximum is reached for
h=h_{AB}
.

Proof:
.

Pick
h\in\mathfrak{H}_{AB}
. We have

{\mathscr{E}}(h,h_{AB})=\langle-Lh,h_{AB}\rangle_{\pi}=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)(-Lh)(x)h_{AB}(x)\;.

(A.3.2)

Since
\pi
,
-Lh
and
h_{AB}
are non-negative, we obtain

{\mathscr{E}}(h,h_{AB})\mathbin{\geqslant}\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(x)(-Lh)(x)\mathbin{\geqslant}0\;.

(A.3.3)

It follows from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality that

\Bigl(\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(x)(-Lh)(x)\Bigr)^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{E}}(h,h_{AB})^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{E}}(h)\capacity(A,B)\;.

(A.3.4)

This proves the inequality, while (
A.1.13
) shows that equality is
again reached for
h=h_{AB}
.
∎

The second version involves the notion of (discrete) unit flow.

Definition A.3.2

(Unit
AB
-flow)
.

A unit
AB
-flow is a map
\varphi:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

•

antisymmetry:
\varphi(x,y)=-\varphi(y,x)
for all
x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}
;

•

compatibility with
p
: if
p(x,y)=0
, then
\varphi(x,y)=0
;

•

Kirchhoff’s law (divergence-freeness):

(\divergence\varphi)(x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\varphi(x,y)=0\qquad\forall x\in(A\cup B)^{c}\;;

(A.3.5)

•

unit intensity:

\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}(\divergence\varphi)(x)=1=-\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in B}(\divergence\varphi)(x)\;.

(A.3.6)

We denote by
\mathfrak{U}^{1}_{AB}
the set of unit
AB
-flows.

One should think of
\varphi
as being defined on the edges of the graph associated
with
P
(i.e., the
(x,y)
on which
p(x,y)>0
).

A special role is played by the
harmonic unit flow

\varphi_{AB}(x,y):=\frac{\pi(x)p(x,y)}{\capacity(A,B)}\bigl[h_{AB}(x)-h_{AB}(y)\bigr]\;.

(A.3.7)

Lemma A.3.3
.

\varphi_{AB}
is a unit
AB
-flow.

Proof:
.

\varphi_{AB}
is clearly antisymmetric and compatible with
p
. It is
divergence-free, since for all
x\in(A\cup B)^{c}
,

(\divergence\varphi_{AB})(x)=\frac{\pi(x)}{\capacity(A,B)}\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}p(x,y)\bigl[h_{AB}(x)-h_{AB}(y)\bigr]=\frac{\pi(x)}{\capacity(A,B)}(-Lh_{AB})(x)=0\;.

(A.3.8)

Finally, it has unit intensity, because

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}(\divergence\varphi_{AB})(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\capacity(A,B)}\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\pi(x)(-Lh_{AB})(x)

(A.3.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\capacity(A,B)}\sqrt{\capacity(A,B){\mathscr{E}}(h_{AB})}=1

(A.3.10)

by Proposition
A.3.1
. The analogous statement for
B
holds by
a discrete version of the divergence theorem.
∎

We now define a bilinear form
{\mathscr{D}}
on the set of unit
AB
-flows by

{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi,\psi):=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\frac{1}{\pi(x)p(x,y)}\varphi(x,y)\psi(x,y)\;,

(A.3.11)

and introduce the shortcut
{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi,\varphi)=:{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)
.
Again, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality applies, yielding

{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi,\psi)^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi){\mathscr{D}}(\psi)\;.

(A.3.12)

Proposition A.3.4

(Thomson principle, unit flow version)
.

We have

\capacity(A,B)=\max_{\varphi\in\mathfrak{U}^{1}_{AB}}\frac{1}{{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)}\;.

(A.3.13)

The maximum is reached for
\varphi=\varphi_{AB}
.

Proof:
.

For a function
h:{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
, define the flow

{}_{h}(x,y):=\pi(x)p(x,y)\bigl[h(x)-h(y)\bigr]\;.

(A.3.14)

Then
\varphi_{AB}={}_{h_{AB}}/\capacity(A,B)
. Thus
{}_{h_{AB}}
is a
(non-unit)
AB
-flow, and we find

\displaystyle{\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\frac{1}{\pi(x)p(x,y)}{}_{h_{AB}}(x,y)^{2}

(A.3.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\pi(x)p(x,y)\bigl[h_{AB}(x)-h_{AB}(y)\bigr]^{2}

(A.3.16)

\displaystyle=\capacity(A,B)\;.

(A.3.17)

By bilinearity, we have

{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi_{AB})=\frac{1}{\capacity(A,B)}\;.

(A.3.18)

Furthermore, given any unit
AB
-flow
\varphi
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\bigl[h_{AB}(x)-h_{AB}(y)\bigr]\varphi(x,y)

(A.3.19)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}h_{AB}(x)\varphi(x,y)

(A.3.20)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\varphi(x,y)+\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in(A\cup B)^{c}}h_{AB}(x)\sumop\displaylimits_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\varphi(x,y)

(A.3.21)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in A}(\divergence\varphi)(x)+\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in(A\cup B)^{c}}h_{AB}(x)\underbrace{(\divergence\varphi)(x)}_{=0}

(A.3.22)

\displaystyle=1\;.

(A.3.23)

The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality thus yields

1={\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}},\varphi)^{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathscr{D}}({}_{h_{AB}}){\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)=\capacity(A,B){\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)\;,

(A.3.24)

showing that
\capacity(A,B)\mathbin{\geqslant}1/{\mathscr{D}}(\varphi)
as required.
∎

A.4
Bibliographical notes

A comprehensive account of the potential-theoretic approach to reversible
Markov chains is given in
[
BdH15
, Sections 7.1 and
7.3]
. An overview can also be found
in
[
Slo12b
]
.

Let us mention that there exists an improved version of the Thomson principle,
called the
Berman–Konsowa principle

[
BK90
]
. The fact
that the Berman–Konsowa principle is sharper than the Thomson principle is a
consequence of Jensen’s inequality, as shown in
[
Slo12a
]
. An
extension of the Berman–Konsowa principle to continuous-space Markov chains
has been obtained in
[
dHJ16
]
.
